THOSE WHO NO LONGER RUN
a science-fiction novel
EXPANDED AND REVISED EDITION
“Peace does not eliminate selection. It only moves the place where it happens.”
A GUIDE TO THE WORLD
Term
Meaning at the beginning of the novel
Arua
a tidally locked world, with one hemisphere scorched by light and the other frozen; abundant life survives in the Twilight Belt between them.
The Nivari
a social, fertile species acutely sensitive to danger; they have lateral eyes, mobile auditory sails, powerful hind limbs and cities dug or grown beneath rock.
The Long Quiet
four centuries without war, maintained through the rotation of the provincial Houses, restrictions on weapons, the management of births and central control of food, light and infrastructure.
The Great Hearing
a computing network that correlates the cities’ vibrations, population registers, production and ecological models; it makes recommendations, and institutions often claim merely to carry them out.
The Chamber of Measure
the institution that allocates birth rights and the Belt’s resources.
Ivarra and Vaor
respectively, the central capital of peace and a border domain where yields are falling, the population is concealed and the old answers no longer suffice.
CHARACTERS AT THE BEGINNING OF THE STORY
Sava Ilen — a demographer in the Chamber of Measure; she believes in the necessity of limits but knows their administrative cruelty.
Oru Ven — Sava’s partner and an engineer of the root network; he regards peace as an ecosystem to be repaired, not revered.
Nemi — their daughter; sensitive to sound, curious and close to her uncle.
Taren Ilen — Sava’s younger brother; diagnosed in childhood with a delayed flight reflex.
Seln Aru — the Custodian of Ivarra; he defends the planetary order with a lucidity that rarely presents itself as violence.
Daran Veyr — the lord of Vaor; cultivated, provocative and convinced that the centre turns the death of the provinces into procedure.
Vessa Dahr — a doctor serving Vaor’s unregistered communities.
Raal — a leader of nivari struck from the registers and driven towards the galleries of the Night.

PART I
THE LOW PEACE
•   •   •
CHAPTER I
PEACE BENEATH THE STONE
SAVA · IVARRA · THREE DAYS BEFORE THE FESTIVAL OF RETREAT
For four centuries, Ivarra had known no war. In that peace, everything dangerous had either been removed from the world, moved into a museum, or renamed a procedure.
Sava Ilen woke before the morning signal, both auditory sails raised and her heart racing at a sound she could not locate.
She lay motionless in the sleeping niche. In Ivarra’s homes, the walls were not entirely rigid. The ceramic root from which they had been grown retained a calculated elasticity, so that footsteps, blows and serious faults could be felt through the bones before they reached the ears. The floor was now sending her small, rapid vibrations. Not an alarm. Not a collapse. More like one light object striking another, with a persistence that belonged only to children.
Sava climbed down from the niche and opened the room’s panel.
Nemi stood barefoot in the shared gallery, her festival ribbon hanging over one eye. She was trying to strike the floor three times without her bone toy jumping out of a chalk circle.
“Shadow, shelter, silence,” the little girl said. “But if I strike too slowly, the instructor says the predator hears me between the beats.”
“The predator is a sheet of cloth pulled along a wire.”
“In the drill. In ancient times it was real.”
Nemi was six standard years old, an age at which a nivar could read, calculate simple rations and generate enough worry for three adults. Her lateral eyes followed the chalk circle and her mother’s expression at the same time. Her long sails trembled at every sound in the pipes. The medical system called this sensitivity auditory hyperreactivity. Oru called it an abundance of attention. Sava, who had access to the complete file, called it nothing at all.
“Go back to bed,” she said. “The signal hasn’t come.”
“Taren isn’t asleep.”
Sava turned one sail towards her brother’s room. No breathing, no rustle. The bed was empty.
“How do you know?”
“His door doesn’t smell of sleep.”
The smells of families mingled in the city’s narrow spaces: baked root, warm down, tool oil, the sweet secretion parents used to mark their children in crowds. Nemi was right. Cold gallery air was coming from Taren’s room.
Oru appeared from the kitchen with a cup between his teeth and two plates in his hands. The short fur on his forearms was stained green with nutrient, though he had washed before sleeping. Root-network engineers wore their work in their skin more than they wore uniforms.
“He’s gone to startle training,” he said. “He left me a message.”
“At this hour?”
“The machines are more convincing when the body hasn’t fully woken up.”
Sava took the cup he held out to her. The infusion was bitter and too hot.
“Does that seem normal to you?”
“No. But normality is precisely what they’re treating him for.”
Oru sat on the floor, his back against the curved wall. Nivari could use chairs, but few preferred them. A body raised above the floor lost the information carried by vibrations, and four centuries had not been enough to make that loss comfortable.
Nemi slipped between them and took a slice of baked root.
“Taren says the machine gets scared faster than he does.”
“Taren says a lot of things when he doesn’t want to answer,” Sava said.
“Do you always answer?”
Oru lowered one sail to hide a smile.
“Your mother answers in tables,” he said. “If the question won’t fit in a table, she puts it in the next cycle.”
Sava nudged a tool he had left on the floor towards him with her foot.
“And your father repairs imaginary pipes during meals.”
Oru was already watching a projection above his wrist. The supply network in the western Belt appeared as a pale branching structure. Three nodes blinked yellow.
“They’re not imaginary. Vaor has lost pressure again.”
“Vaor loses pressure every cold season.”
“Not in the same pipes. The deposits are moving towards the centre.”
“Send the report to infrastructure.”
“I’ve sent it four times. The Great Hearing says the readings are still within the compensation range.”
“Then they are.”
Oru switched off the projection and looked at her with his whole attention. For a nivar, this meant turning the head almost squarely towards someone and giving up half the field of vision. The gesture could be tender or aggressive. With Oru, it was usually a request that she stop hiding inside her profession.
“When did you start talking about the system as though it were the weather?” he asked.
“When I found out how many things break if every official thinks they’re cleverer than the model.”
“And how many break when no one does?”
Nemi looked from one to the other, chewing slowly. Sava did not want the little girl to turn every minor disagreement into a danger signal. She lowered her voice.
“After the Festival, I’ll read the raw data. I promise.”
“You said that after the last Festival too.”
“I didn’t promise then.”
Oru accepted the distinction with a movement of his sail. The morning signal came through the floor: two low pulses, then one long one. The city woke around them. Doors slid open. Water began flowing through the washing pipes. Somewhere a child cried, another laughed, and the footsteps of thousands of inhabitants gathered into a murmur carried through the building’s roots.
The peace of Ivarra had a sound. It was not silence. It was the predictable noise of bodies that knew where they had to go.
•   •   •
The Reflex Therapy Centre occupied an old level of the city, built before ceilings had been lowered to the height considered reassuring. The training room therefore seemed too vast. The lights were buried in the floor, and above them lay darkness. Machines hung there like animals that had not yet learned to come down.
Taren stood in the middle of the circle, his hind paws apart and his arms at his sides.
A mechanical arm shot out from the left. He should have crouched before he saw it fully. Taren turned his head, followed the joint, noticed the wear at the gripping end, and only then took a step.
The arm struck his shoulder.
“A delay of four tenths,” the therapist said. “Again.”
“Sensor three makes a sound before it starts.”
“In real life, danger often makes a sound.”
“Then it isn’t unexpected.”
The therapist’s name was Mera Sol, and she had treated him since he was seven. Back then, Sava would bring him by the hand and sign every consent form so hard that the tablet retained the marks of her fingers. Mera had known his bursts of anger, the periods when he had refused synthetic veyrin, and the year he had bitten two assistants. She considered him intelligent, exhausting and biologically uncooperative.
“The aim isn’t to be surprised,” she said. “The aim is for movement to come before analysis.”
“That sounds like the definition of a mistake.”
“For your ancestors, analysis before movement meant death.”
“My ancestors didn’t live beneath thirty metres of rock, and they didn’t have doors that closed themselves.”
“Your body doesn’t know that.”
“My body seems to be the very thing that does know.”
Mera stopped the machine. She approached without haste. She was small, with short auditory sails cut straight across, an old fashion among therapists, who maintained that patients became calmer when their expressions were easier to read.
“The Festival is in three days. You’ll take part in the supervision group, not in the children’s circle.”
“Nemi asked me to stand beside her.”
“Nemi has instructors.”
“The instructors tell her to run according to the marker, not according to what she sees.”
“It’s a group exercise. They have to react together.”
Taren looked at the mechanical arm. A patch of black down was glued to its end, part of the predator costume. As a child, it had frightened him. Now he could see the seam.
“When everyone reacts together, who checks whether the signal is right?”
Mera drew a slow breath.
“Taren, society cannot be built around your exception.”
“I didn’t ask for that.”
“You ask every time you turn a simple instruction into a debate about truth.”
He smiled without amusement.
“Sava says the same thing in more words.”
“Sava is trying to keep you alive.”
“I know.”
That was the part that made resistance difficult. Sava had not sent him to therapy out of shame. When he was eight, she had found him watching a cable fire while the other children fled. She had dragged him away from the smoke and cried only after they got home. Their parents had died two years earlier, crushed in a transport tunnel. Sava had become sister, guardian and official of survival on the same day.
Taren agreed to another series. An unexpected bell. A tilting floor. The smell of blood. A silhouette dropping from the ceiling. Each time, his body gave him a moment in which he could look before he became afraid. Each time, the machine called that moment a defect.
When he came out, Sava was waiting in the gallery.
“You missed breakfast.”
“I didn’t know that was a crime.”
“Not yet. The Chamber is working on the classification.”
They walked towards the central levels. Taren was a head taller than she was, but he still carried something of the child she had pulled through the smoke: a hesitation before coming closer, as though every affectionate gesture first had to be checked for traps.
“Mera says you’re not allowed beside Nemi’s circle,” Sava said.
“Mera doesn’t have a child in the circle.”
“Neither do you.”
“That’s why I can keep an eye on yours.”
Sava did not answer. The gallery widened towards Low Square. Workers were putting up the ritual shadows of saryxes, black sheets stretched over carbon frames. An enormous wing descended from the ceiling, moved by winches. Children stopped to watch it until the adults drew them closer to the walls.
Taren stood still in the stream of passers-by. People skirted him in irritation.
“What is it?” Sava asked.
“The third frame has been moved.”
“They’ve changed the route.”
“No. The wires descend towards the north, but the cloth is facing as though it were coming from the Day.”
“They’re probably adjusting it.”
Taren looked longer than necessary. Sava touched his arm.
“Not every mismatch is a warning.”
“I know.”
“Then come on.”
“But every warning begins as a mismatch.”
•   •   •
At the Chamber of Measure, Sava’s day passed among birth applications, relocations and food calculations. On her office wall, the Twilight Belt was reduced to a coloured strip. The scorched Day appeared white, the Night dark blue, and between them the habitable region pulsed. From a distance, the planet looked simple. The problems began when you enlarged the image enough for the families to appear.
At the end of the working day, the system automatically opened her personal file.
ILEN–VEN APPLICATION / SECOND BIRTH / DEFERRED: 5.7 YEARS.
Sava knew all the reasons before she read them. The density of the district. The food quota. The family’s genetic history. Nemi’s hyperreactivity. Taren’s delayed reflex. The model did not forbid. The model recommended waiting until waiting would make the decision pointless.
She closed the file and deleted the notification from the shared space. Oru would find out. Not that evening. Not with the Festival so close and Nemi practising the signal on the floor.
When she returned home, she found all three of them in the shared gallery. Oru was trying to mend the ribbon. Nemi stood still, tormented by the effort. Between his fingers Taren held a small carbon mechanism taken, probably without permission, from the Festival props.
“Where did you get that?” Sava asked.
“It was broken.”
“That’s not an answer.”
“From the square.”
“Worse.”
Taren showed her the piece. On its inner surface was a trace of black substance, like sticky powder.
“Synthetic down,” Sava said.
“Perhaps.”
“You brought scrap props home to prove to me that perhaps it’s something else?”
“I brought it because it smells of protein.”
Oru took the piece and held it near his nostrils. His sails lifted a little.
“It has an organic smell.”
“Adhesive,” Sava said. “The material is kept in damp storerooms. It gets contaminated.”
Nemi leaned forward, fascinated.
“Is it from a real saryx?”
“There are no real saryxes in Ivarra,” Sava said. “They disappeared before your grandparents’ grandparents were born.”
Taren put the piece on the table.
“Then we have no reason to hurry.”
Sava looked at him. She knew his way of constructing doubt: he asserted nothing, merely left a door ajar and waited for the others to hear the draught.
“Tomorrow you take it to the laboratory,” she said. “Tonight we eat.”
Nemi lifted her repaired ribbon.
“Will you stay with me at the Festival?”
Taren looked at Sava, not at the child.
“Outside the circle,” Sava said. “Where you won’t get in the instructors’ way.”
“Close enough to see me?” Nemi asked.
“I can see you even when you’re not close,” Taren replied.
The little girl accepted the promise. Later, after the lights went out, Sava heard him leave his room again. She did not stop him. She told herself he was taking the piece to the laboratory. She told herself her brother was eighteen and entitled to his own anxieties. She told herself the city was safe.
Beneath the homes, beneath the square and beneath the galleries that public maps called sterile foundations, an old pump ran for seven seconds.
In an unnumbered tank, something small unfolded its membranes and struck the glass once.
CHAPTER II
THE SHADOW AT NOON
TAREN AND SAVA · IVARRA · YEAR 412 OF THE LONG QUIET
Before the attack, Ivarra had not seen a predator up close for four centuries. Citizens knew its shape from lessons, museums and ritual games; they no longer knew its smell.
Taren reached Low Square before the public gates opened. He liked Ivarra in the hours when the city did not yet have to pretend to be inhabited. The elastic floor carried only the vibrations of the assembly crews. The air smelled of washed clay and winch oil. The black cloths of the ritual shadows hung motionless beneath the ceiling, and without the crowd and music they looked less like the saryxes in the textbooks than clothes hung out to dry by a careless god.
At the edge of the children’s platform, a technician was checking the retreat markers. He lit them one by one: blue for the firstborn, green for the food carriers, white for the adults who were symbolically meant to stay behind. Taren watched him retrace the route three times, each time in the same order.
“Marker seven is too close to the railing,” he said.
The technician did not look up.
“It passed inspection.”
“If two children jump at the same time, one will hit the edge.”
“The exercise doesn’t include long jumps.”
“They don’t read the instructions when they’re frightened.”
The technician turned a single sail towards him, sparing the rest of his attention.
“You’re the apprentice who doesn’t get frightened enough, aren’t you?”
There was no cruelty in his voice. Only the satisfaction of someone who had found the right drawer for something inconvenient.
“That’s what it says.”
“Then let the ones who do get frightened design the route.”
Taren could have moved the marker himself. The adhesive was fresh, and the platform still empty. He imagined the report: unauthorised intervention, disruption of a public ritual, repeated oppositional behaviour. He settled for remembering the spot.
Beneath the platform was a service hatch that the plans described as cable access. The seal had been changed during the night. The old sealing material was grey; this one had a strip that was still glossy. Taren knelt and laid two fingers on the floor. He felt the pumps, the winches and the crews’ footsteps. Lower down, after a pause too regular to be silence, came a scratch.
“What are you looking for?”
Mera Sol stood behind him. The therapist wore the green cloak of the medical corps and an expression in which fatigue mingled with the anticipation of a familiar conversation.
“The cables,” he replied.
“Have you found them?”
“I’ve found a hatch.”
“Impressive progress.”
She held out a yellow access band.
“You’ll stay in the outer sector. Nemi can see you, but you don’t go onto the platform unless an instructor calls you.”
“What if the instructor can’t call?”
“Then you follow the general signal.”
“That’s exactly what we’re practising: following a signal regardless of what happens.”
Mera turned her whole head to look at him.
“We’re practising not turning six thousand individual reactions into a collective crush.”
“And does it work?”
“For four hundred and twelve years.”
Taren stood. The question in his mind — *how many times has it been tested?* — remained unspoken. Mera had heard it anyway. She touched the thin bandage on his palm, left by the carbon piece he had taken the day before.
“Sometimes,” she said, “the fact that something can fail doesn’t mean you have to help it fail.”
“I haven’t helped it.”
“Yet.”
•   •   •
When the gates opened, Ivarra entered the square in disciplined waves. Families from the central districts came through the broad galleries, scented with sweet root; workers from the outskirts descended the service stairs, the smell of metal and mycelium in their fur. Members of the provincial Houses wore their domains’ colours, but no insignia of rank. During its year of residence, each House had to offer the capital an image of submission, even if the cut of its cloak preserved the memory of armour.
Nemi found him before Sava did. Her white ribbon sat crookedly between her sails, and the left tip trembled so fast that the fabric rustled.
“You came.”
“I promised.”
“Mother says promises don’t change probabilities.”
“Your mother uses probabilities so she doesn’t have to say she’s afraid.”
“Is she afraid today?”
Taren searched the terrace for his sister’s figure. Sava was talking to Oru, but one sail remained turned towards the children’s circle.
“A little,” he said. “It’s a healthy amount.”
Nemi laid her palm against his breastbone. Children could feel another person’s rhythm more easily than their own pulse.
“You aren’t.”
“I am.”
“It doesn’t feel like it.”
“With me, it arrives later.”
The instructor called the group. Nemi let go of him, then turned back after two steps.
“When it arrives, can you still choose?”
Taren had no time to answer. The children were arranged in circles, the gates closed, and the deep opening gong swallowed every small sound.
The opening gong sounded from the central vault. Six thousand auditory sails folded at once. Conversations died away. Seln Aru appeared on the Custodian’s platform, dressed in white, the hems of his cloak embroidered with the pattern of the ancient escape tunnels. He was not old, though the fine lines around his nostrils made him look tired. His body was small and controlled; no gesture exceeded the ceremonial space allotted to his office.
“Today we remember,” he said, “that we survived not because we were the strongest, but because we knew when to disappear.”
The crowd struck the floor once with their hind limbs. The vibration rose through the railing into Sava’s bones.
“We remember that life does not demand triumph. It demands continuity.”
The second blow.
“We remember that our ancestors’ heroism was the number of those who returned.”
The third blow rolled through the city, taken up by the secondary squares, the schools, the mycelium factories and the Gardens of Kinship, where the families of the provincial Houses lived as guests of the capital. Three low pulses, older than language: shadow, shelter, silence.
Sava felt them in her chest too. However much she despised the festivities, her body knew what the signal meant. For a moment, every exit from the square lit up in her mind. The northern tunnel, too narrow. The western gallery, crowded. The Chamber stairs, protected by fire doors. Around her, thousands of brains were drawing the same map without being asked.
The ritual shadows descended from the ceiling.
They were black cloths stretched over carbon frames, modelled on the silhouettes of the extinct saryxes. Their wings spanned ten metres and moved with the help of almost invisible wires. Each year, the shadows crossed the square, and the children had to recognise the direction of attack and retreat in the correct order: the little ones first, then the food carriers, and last the adults able to block the entrances. No one spoke of the fact that in a real attack the order had almost always been reversed.
The first shadow passed without incident. The children crouched, then ran in short arcs towards the blue markers. Nemi lost her ribbon. Oru made a triumphant sound.
The second shadow descended lower. Taren stepped back with Nemi’s group, but a fraction too late. A supervisor caught his arm and pulled him out of the path.
“You see?” Sava said. “He’s learning.”
“He doesn’t look happy.”
Taren never looked happy when he managed to behave normally.
The shape in the north caught Taren’s attention a moment before Sava recognised it. Not because he saw better, but because he was looking at precisely the things the ceremony had declared irrelevant: the cables, the air currents, the blind spots between the cloths. That shadow was attached to no wire. It moved by its own effort, and when it drew its membranes together, Taren saw a joint that did not belong to the props.
He wanted to shout. Before he could draw breath, the creature fell.
The third shadow was not part of the ritual.
Sava noticed it because it came from the wrong direction. The mechanical cloths moved from the Day towards the Night, imitating the flight of saryxes that rode the warm currents. The shape in the north was moving against the air, close to the vault. It was smaller than the props, and its edges trembled irregularly.
For a heartbeat, Sava’s mind tried to fit it into a category: maintenance drone, a break in the ceiling, fallen material. Then the shape closed its wings and dropped.
Seln Aru was still speaking.
“Peace is not forgetting, but the disciplined memory of danger—”
The creature struck the schoolchildren’s platform with a wet sound. It had six limbs, two of which unfolded into grey membranes. Its head was narrow, with no visible eyes, and its mouth opened vertically, from its chest almost to its brow. Its skin was covered in black down that absorbed the light.
No one moved.
That was the first mistake.
The Festival had taught them that shadows had routes and that signals came before danger. Thousands of bodies received the impossible image, but waited for permission to understand it.
The saryx drove its forelimbs into a supervisor. The sound its mouth made was not a howl, but a vibration so low that the floor carried it before ears could hear it.
The crowd broke apart.
The panic was nothing like the drills. It had neither direction nor centre. The floor had become a single creature striking in every direction, and the vibrations were so dense that Taren could no longer distinguish Nemi’s footsteps. An adult slammed into him from the side. Another caught his arm and pulled him towards the exit. Taren tore himself free, not out of courage, but so that he could see.
Later, in the recordings, the first seconds would seem slow. Each body could be followed separately, each choice frozen and discussed. From within, there were no choices, only a succession of pressures: a back against a chest, a shoulder against a cheek, claws on the floor, the sudden smell of alarm glands. The little ones had vanished below the adults’ waists. Three bodies covered marker seven.
Nemi was no longer there.
Later, Sava could not remember how she had come down from the terrace. She remembered only separate details: Oru’s hand vanishing from hers; a child crushed against the railing; white ribbons floating in the air; the Custodian being evacuated through an opening that had appeared on no public plan. Nivari ran well when they had space. In an enclosed square, each leap became someone else’s obstacle.
Taren remained standing.
Not out of resolve. Not at first. His body had not issued the command the other bodies had received. He saw the creature, saw the fallen supervisor, and saw Nemi, too small to get through the mass of adults, being pushed towards the centre of the square.
Curiosity lasted a moment.
In that moment he noticed that the saryx turned its head towards vibrations, not images. He noticed that the Festival props hung from carbon rods three bodies long. He noticed that everyone running offered the creature a field full of signals, while he, motionless, did not exist for it.
Then fear came. It came late, but it came whole, like a door opening onto a vacuum. The muscles of his hind limbs tightened so hard that his joints hurt. His auditory sails flattened against the back of his neck. His mouth filled with a metallic taste.
He could run. The service tunnel was seven leaps away.
Nemi fell.
Taren tore a rod from the mechanical shadow’s support. The carbon cut his palm. He moved not towards the exit but sideways, stepping lightly, his weight on the front of his paws. The saryx raised its membranes and attacked a running figure. Its claws tore through a cloak and exposed a thin, white back.
Taren struck the floor once.
The creature turned towards the vibration.
He struck again, closer.
“Taren!” Sava shouted from somewhere in the crowd.
The third time, he did not strike the floor. He drove the rod into the vertical opening of the mouth.
The rod vibrated in his hands. Taren did not know whether it was the carbon, the creature or himself that was shaking. The saryx was lighter than it had seemed as it fell. Its membranes smelled of warm mould and sour milk. Up close, the black down was not uniform: each filament ended in a hook that caught dust, skin and threads of cloaks from those it touched.
He saw the mouth closing on the rod and understood, with the cold clarity that therapy had tried to remove from him, that the first strike had not been fatal. The creature followed vibrations. The crowd offered it thousands. He had to offer it just one.
The saryx closed over the rod. The force of its jaws cracked the carbon, but the tip had gone far enough in. Taren felt resistance, then a soft giving way. The creature struck him with one of its limbs. A claw opened his shoulder and flung him across a blue marker. Pain made the world clear.
Nemi was two steps away. She was crawling towards him, one auditory sail torn at the tip and her eyes so wide that the black irises seemed to fill her head.
“Don’t run,” Taren told her.
It was the most absurd thing anyone could say to a nivar child.
“Stay down. Don’t strike the floor.”
The saryx thrashed around the rod. Its wings beat the air, and black down rose in clumps. Taren stood. Every limb was trembling. He moved forward when his body demanded retreat, and pushed again.
This time, the rod pierced the back of the head.
The creature stopped.
Not immediately. First it drew its membranes around Taren like a cold cloak. Then the vertical mouth opened without a sound. Clear fluid ran down the carbon rod and gathered in his cut palm. The smell was unexpectedly sweet.
Taren held the rod until someone grabbed him from behind and pulled him away. The saryx fell onto its side.
The square was still emptying. Those who had not seen the creature die were still running. Those who had seen it had stopped at a distance and were watching Taren with all their auditory organs turned towards him. For the first time in his life, he had a city’s whole attention.
Sava reached Nemi and gathered her to her chest. Oru appeared a few moments later, limping, with a cut above his eye. He touched his daughter, then Sava, then turned towards Taren.
Oru caught his wrist before the medics reached him. There was blood on his cheek, and it was not his. With his other hand, he held the back of Nemi’s neck, pressing her to his chest. The little girl was not crying. Her sails remained folded so tightly that the tip of the injured one had turned white.
“You’re hurt,” Oru repeated, as though the sentence could restore the order of things.
Taren looked at the opening in his shoulder. The edges of the wound pulsed faintly. Fear had arrived in full now, after there was nothing left for him to do with it. His knees were shaking. His teeth knocked together.
“Not badly,” he said.
Sava laid her palm against his cheek, then immediately drew it back, as though closeness were too painful. There was a long scratch on her arm. Taren saw her eyes searching, by professional reflex, for the number of living people around them.
“Did you know what to do?” she asked.
“No.”
“Then how—”
“I saw what it was doing.”
Nemi lifted her head from Oru’s chest. Her voice came out thin.
“You told me not to run.”
“Yes.”
“Was that allowed?”
Around them, agents of the Quiet were trying to rebuild a world in which the question made sense. They lifted the wounded, closed entrances, put screens around the corpse. Taren looked at the black body lying across the blue markers, then at the children who still did not dare get up.
“No,” he said. “But it was true.”
CHAPTER III
THIRTY-SEVEN ABSENCES
SAVA · IVARRA · THE FIRST TWO DAYS AFTER THE ATTACK
In Ivarra’s reports, the attack lasted ninety-three seconds. For those who had survived it, it continued in smells, startles and unfinished sentences long after the square had been washed.
The doctor asked Taren to hand over the carbon rod. He first used the gentle voice reserved for the wounded, then an administrative tone, and finally called an agent. Taren held the object in both hands. Dried blood had glued his fingers to the cracked surface.
Taren looked at his shoulder. Blood was running down his arm in thin lines.
“Not badly.”
“You killed a saryx,” Oru said.
The word passed through those around them like a disease. Saryx. A creature children drew without believing it had existed. A form preserved in genetic museums so that peace would have a past.
“There are no saryxes,” someone said.
“There isn’t this one any more,” Taren replied.
It might have been a joke, if his voice had not been so quiet.
The Custodian’s agents entered the square in formation. They carried no visible weapons; the white uniforms of the Quiet had no pockets large enough to hide any. Two lifted the wounded. Four others unfurled an opaque screen around the corpse. A doctor approached Taren, sprayed coagulant on his shoulder and asked him to hand over the rod.
Taren did not let go.
“It’s contaminated,” the doctor said.
“So is my hand.”
“Your hand can be cleaned.”
“Then clean the rod too.”
Sava saw his jaw moving. His front chisel teeth had emerged a little from beneath his lip, a threat reflex that the well brought-up controlled before the age of six. The agent behind the doctor took a step.
“Taren,” she said. “Give it to him.”
He turned his whole head to look at her. For a few moments, he was not her brother. He was an animal covered in blood, black down stuck to his chest, with an expression Sava had never seen on any nivar’s face: not anger, not panic, not relief.
Pleasure.
Not the pleasure of pain or death. It was closer to the deep satisfaction of a jammed mechanism beginning to move again. For once, the world had asked of him precisely the thing for which he had been treated as defective.
After letting go, he could no longer stop shaking. The doctor laid him on a low stretcher and injected him with a veyrin precursor. The substance warmed his throat, quickened his pulse and made the edges of the square seem closer. It was the treatment he had received in childhood, when the therapist wanted to teach his body the correct order: first terror, then thought.
This time, the order made him nauseous.
“I don’t want any more,” he said.
“You’re having a severe delayed reaction.”
“The danger has passed.”
“That’s precisely why your body is releasing it now.”
“Let it.”
The doctor hesitated. The agent who had taken the rod was looking towards the corpse, not at them. Around them, the wounded were being separated by the colour of their bands. Red for those who might die, yellow for those who could wait, white for those whose wounds had already been closed by the administration of death. Taren saw a child’s ribbon beneath a white cloak and sat up too quickly.
“It isn’t Nemi,” Sava said.
He did not know where she had come from. She was pressing a dressing to his shoulder and spoke in the voice of someone who had repeated the sentence to herself.
“Nemi is with Oru. They’re taking her to the auditory infirmary.”
Taren sat back. On the other side of the square, a parent was striking the floor rhythmically, calling a child who would never answer again. The agents were asking him to stop so that the medical signals could be heard. He went on. One blow, a pause, two blows. The child’s name in a language older than words.
“Thirty-seven,” Sava said.
“You’ve counted already?”
“The estimate.”
“I didn’t ask you how many.”
She took her hand off the dressing.
“Then I don’t know what you asked me.”
Neither did he. Sava was doing what she had done after their parents died: gathering the world into numbers small enough to carry. Taren remembered the day the tunnel collapsed, the smell of dust and the register open on their table. Sava was fourteen and had read the identity column eight times, as though a spelling error might give her family back.
“Go to Nemi,” he said. “I’m not dying.”
Sava looked at him. For a moment, he saw her anger: not at him, but at having to choose again whom to protect.
“Don’t decide that on your own.”
“Then tell the system to postpone it.”
It was not a good joke. Sava left anyway, because Nemi was calling her from the corridor.
•   •   •
The auditory infirmary had been designed for quiet, but after the attack quiet had become a form of threat. The children lay in shared rooms, four to a room, with damped floors and dimmed lights. Nemi refused to stay on the bed. She had crouched beneath it, in a space no adult could enter without crawling.
Oru sat on the floor an arm’s length away. Sava remained in the doorway.
“May I turn off the light?” he asked.
“No.”
“I’ll leave it on.”
“And the door.”
“The door stays open.”
Nemi clutched the torn ribbon in her fist. The transparent splint on her sail held its edge in place, but any movement in the corridor made her start. When a trolley passed the room, she struck the floor three times with a hind paw. The children in the other beds answered before the nurses could stop them. Within moments, the whole wing was vibrating with the shadow signal.
Sava felt her body preparing to run.
“There’s nothing here,” she said too quickly.
From beneath the bed, Nemi’s eye turned towards her.
“That’s what you said at home too.”
Sava sat down. She could find no administrative wording that could get past that sentence.
Oru laid his palm on the floor.
“Can you feel the pump?” he asked.
Nemi did not answer.
“It’s the water pump. Two small pulses, one long one. It isn’t a signal. It’s just pushing water up to level four.”
After a while, the child’s hand came out from beneath the bed and settled beside his. Oru named each vibration: the medical lift, the air pipe, the passing of the night shift. He did not say they were safe. He said what they were.
Sava listened to him and understood why Taren had saved her daughter. Not because he had felt no fear, but because, for a moment, he had named the things around him correctly.
•   •   •
By evening, Low Square had been washed. The Festival’s black cloths had vanished, and the information channels were broadcasting the same wording: an experimental organism, accidentally released from a research facility, had caused a crowd incident. Custodian Seln Aru had appeared with a mourning band on his arm and praised the discipline of the emergency services. Not a single name had been spoken.
At the Chamber of Measure, Sava received the preliminary list of victims.
Thirty-seven dead.
One had injuries caused by the saryx: the supervisor attacked at the beginning.
Twenty-nine had been crushed.
Four had died after jumping into a service shaft.
Three children had been suffocated by the bodies of adults trying to protect them.
Sava read the report twice. In the “institutional cause” column, all thirty-seven deaths were classified as accidental biological release. She moved the cursor. The system demanded high-level authorisation for any correction.
“It wasn’t the saryx,” Oru said from the doorway.
Nemi was asleep in the infirmary, sedated. Oru had refused to go home and had washed the nutrient stains from his arms, but green remained beneath his nails.
“It started because of it,” Sava said.
“But it didn’t kill them.”
“The distinction won’t help their families.”
“It might help us.”
Sava closed the report. On the wall, the Twilight Belt’s population map pulsed with thousands of points of light. Each point was an authorised birth, a death, an approved migration. The system showed her an orderly world in which life could be preserved by counting.
“Not tonight,” she said.
“Especially tonight.”
Oru came in and sat on the floor beside the desk. Nivari avoided high chairs; even after four centuries without aerial hunters, the exposed position remained uncomfortable.
“I ran past Nemi,” he said.
Sava looked up.
“No.”
“I didn’t step on her. But I went past her. For a moment. I saw the exit, and my body chose it before I understood that she wasn’t with me.”
“You came back.”
“Taren didn’t have to come back.”
“Taren is ill.”
Oru raised both sails towards her.
“Do you still believe that?”
Sava wanted to answer. Instead, she reopened the report and looked at the figures. An incident. Thirty-seven dead. An anomaly. A rescuer who had not run.
Oru did not leave after the conversation. He took off his footwear and stayed beside the desk. Sava kept reading the list until the names no longer resembled people. At the seventeenth, she recognised a woman who had applied for a third birth two months earlier. She had been refused. Her two children had survived, but were left without a parent. The model would distribute them among the relatives with the lowest housing burden.
“We have to tell them the truth,” Oru said.
“Which truth? That an animal was released and we killed one another trying to run from it? Each family will choose the half that suits them.”
“Then we tell both halves.”
“The channels have already received the wording.”
“You’re in the Chamber.”
“The Chamber counts. It doesn’t speak.”
“Everything it counts becomes what the others are allowed to say.”
Sava rubbed her eyes. In the next room, an operator was processing compensation claims. Each time he validated a death, the floor transmitted an almost imperceptible pulse to the central archive. The system remembered without grieving.
“I ran too,” she said.
Oru looked at her.
“You went back to Nemi.”
“After I hit the railing. I didn’t turn back. I was deflected.”
“You can’t judge a body’s first two seconds.”
“I judge entire lives on the basis of percentages.”
“That’s precisely why perhaps you should start with your own.”
Sava raised a sail in irritation, but did not send him away. Between them settled the familiar weariness of people who love each other yet live in different moral systems. Oru repaired what he touched. Sava decided what could not be repaired without breaking something else.
Before they could continue, a request from the Restitution Service appeared on the screen: two families had identified one body, and its release had to be authorised by a Chamber official. On an ordinary day, the dispute would have been resolved through genetic fingerprints and scent histories. After the attack, the sensors had been contaminated with festival substances, blood and the foam used to wash the square. The system offered a probability of ninety-six per cent. Neither family accepted the remaining four.
“I can send someone else,” Sava said.
Oru looked at the request.
“You can. But you won’t.”
The restitution hall had been set up in the storeroom where, the day before, traders had kept trays of baked root. The sweet smell lingered in the walls and now mingled with antiseptic. Thirty-seven low niches had been separated by grey cloths. A number pulsed above each one. There were no names until official confirmation; the administration avoided giving grief the wrong identity.
At niche fourteen, three adults and a child were waiting. Two of the women wore the same House band, but did not look at each other. The man stood with his forehead against the wall, listening to the city’s vibrations as though the person behind the cloth might send a signal through the pipes.
“I’m Sava Ilen,” she said. “I’ve come for the confirmation.”
The older woman held out a family plaque.
“She is my daughter, Lera Naur.”
The other woman raised her sails.
“She is my partner, not the property of House Naur.”
“I didn’t say she was my property.”
“You asked for her to be returned to your family’s cavity.”
“Her ancestors are there.”
“She left that place so she could live with me.”
The child held on to the hem of the second woman’s coat. He had a scratch on his cheek and a yellow band on his wrist, a sign that he had been treated for chest compression. He was not crying. He watched the niche number, which lit up and went dark with each breath of the air system.
Sava opened the file. Lera had two recognised addresses, a reproductive bond ended twelve years earlier and a care contract with the woman beside the child. Funeral law had been written in an age when a person belonged to a single House, a single home and a single cavity after death. The Quiet had changed families faster than it had changed the forms.
“We can divide the ashes,” Sava said.
Lera’s mother turned towards her.
“She wasn’t a ration.”
“That wasn’t what I meant.”
“But that’s what you know how to do. Divide what there isn’t enough of.”
Oru took a step, but Sava touched his arm. The woman had a right to that cruelty. Perhaps not towards her; perhaps towards the only uniform she could find.
“You’re right,” Sava said. “The procedure wasn’t made for the life she lived.”
Lera’s partner looked at her for the first time.
“Then don’t use the procedure.”
Sava cancelled the automatic allocation and entered joint funerary custody. The system marked the decision in red, demanding an exceptional reason. She wrote: “The life of the deceased person encompassed a greater number of family relationships than this form is capable of representing.” The field accepted only a hundred and twenty characters. The last words disappeared. She rephrased it until the sentence fitted.
The cloth drew back far enough to allow confirmation. Lera Naur’s eyes were closed, and a dark mark ran along her jaw. She had not been touched by the saryx. The report said she had died of compression near the service shaft. Her arms were still clasped around an empty space.
The child came closer.
“She put me underneath her,” he said.
No one answered.
“She told me not to move. I moved.”
Her partner gently drew him back.
“It wasn’t because of you.”
“If I’d stayed still, would she still be alive?”
The question was not the age of the child who had asked it. Sava looked in the file, though she knew there was no answer there. The crowd model could simulate pressure, directions, the probability of survival. It could have calculated a world in which the child had not moved. It would have produced a percentage and, with it, a new guilt.
She closed the file.
“Did she get you out of there?” she asked.
The child nodded.
“Then the last thing she did worked.”
“But she died.”
“Yes.”
Sava did not add that sometimes a thing could work and still destroy the person who had done it. The child would learn that soon enough.
On the way out, an official asked her to validate the institutional cause of death. “Accidental biological release” appeared on the screen again. Sava opened the locked field and used her emergency rank.
“What are you writing?” Oru asked.
“Death through protection in a crowd incident.”
“That’s a new category.”
“It exists now.”
The official folded his sails uneasily.
“If we separate the causes, the compensation will be contested. The families of those crushed can demand accountability for the management of the square, not just for the animal’s release.”
“They can.”
“The Chamber will have to open inquiries.”
“Then it will.”
On the way back, they passed through Low Square. The wash water had been drained, the stalls put up, the cracks in the floor covered with resin. A group of workers was reinstalling the Festival panels for the mourning ceremony. The imprint of a body remained on one of them; the resin had made it shine. The supervisor ordered it sanded down.
Sava stopped until the trace disappeared.
“Tomorrow,” Oru said, “people will cross this place without knowing where it was.”
“They have to be able to cross.”
“That doesn’t mean they have to forget.”
Sava watched the worker vacuuming up the dust. The capital was very good at the distinction between restoration and memory. It restored immediately. It kept memory beneath levels the public could not access.
When she returned to the office, the change she had introduced had already propagated through the system. Thirty-six files awaited reclassification. Each family now had the right to ask not only what had triggered the incident, but what had actually killed their person.
“You’ve just turned a statistic into thirty-seven inquiries,” Oru said.
“No. There were thirty-seven before I counted them.”
The door opened.
The door opened. Taren came in without asking permission. His shoulder had been bandaged, and the fur on his chest was still damp from decontamination. In his hand he carried a piece of black material no larger than a leaf.
Sava stood.
“Where did you get that?”
“From the membrane. It got caught in the bandage.”
“It has to be handed over to the laboratory.”
“The laboratory already knows what it is.”
He laid the piece on the desk. In the light, it looked less like down than a network of fine filaments, each ending in a microscopic hook.
“Seln says it escaped from a facility,” Taren continued. “The agents say there are no other specimens. The doctor said the species can’t reproduce in our climate.”
“And?”
“There was milk in its ventral tissues.”
Oru came closer.
“Was it female?”
“It was feeding something.”
The room became very quiet. In the wall, the root network pumped water with a slow, almost organic rhythm.
Sava reached towards the fragment but did not touch it.
“It could be a hormonal reaction induced in the laboratory.”
“It could,” Taren said.
“Then why have you come?”
Taren turned his head towards the map of the Belt. The points of red light reflected in his lateral eyes, so that he seemed to be looking at the world and at something outside it at the same time.
“Because when I pushed the rod, I heard two beats beneath the floor.”
Sava felt her sails rise on their own.
“What kind of beats?”
“Not from the crowd. From below. One, then another. Like an answer.”
“The pipes vibrate.”
“I know how pipes vibrate.”
Oru laid his palm on the floor.
“The genetic museum’s old tanks are beneath the square.”
Sava looked at him.
“They were emptied eighty years ago.”
“That’s what the registers say.”
Taren smiled for the first time since the attack. It was a small, almost embarrassed smile, but the white chisel teeth showed again.
“Then the registers should be the first to run,” he said.
The blows in the tank were not heard by the public. The lower-level sensors recorded them and classified them as structural expansion. A tired operator confirmed the classification without opening the waveform.
Taren, Sava and Oru nevertheless went down as far as the first seal. They had no access below it. The gallery was narrow, its walls made of old alloy, and the lights came on late, letting the darkness run ahead of them.
Taren laid his palm on the door. Nothing.
“Perhaps you heard the echo of the struggle,” Sava said.
“Perhaps.”
“Or a ventilation mechanism.”
“Perhaps.”
Oru examined the console. Someone had replaced the access seal with a new one, then falsified its installation date. The trace was small: a batch of polymer that had not existed on the declared date.
“It’s been opened,” he said.
Sava looked down the empty corridor. In Ivarra, doors opened for the authorised; the idea that authorisation could lie seemed more serious than a forced entry.
From beyond the alloy came a light touch. Not a blow. A claw testing the surface.
All three remained motionless.
Taren turned his head towards his sister.
“Now you know why I don’t want to be the first to run.”
CHAPTER IV
THE CHAMBER OF MEASURE
SAVA · IVARRA · THE THIRD DAY AFTER THE ATTACK
The Chamber of Measure preserved peace through a simple and impossible arithmetic: each permitted life had to occupy the place of a life ended or a harvest still uncertain.
The Chamber of Measure did not forbid births. It placed them in the future.
That was the official wording and, on most days, Sava managed to believe it. Arua could not be made larger. To the east, living tissue cooked in the open air, and water boiled in the surface fissures. To the west, gases froze in hollows and winds carried needles of solid nitrogen. Between them lay the Belt: a fertile strip seven thousand kilometres long, maintained by orbital mirrors, geothermal channels and a demographic discipline older than every building in Ivarra.
Each family received the right to a first birth. The second depended on deaths, root yields, space available in the depths and the region’s genetic diversity. A third was rare. A fourth existed only in the provinces’ obscene songs.
Nivari generally lived between fifty and sixty standard years and matured quickly. Without predators and restrictions on births, the models showed that the population would double every six years. Within a single lifetime, the Belt would become a hungry mass driven towards fire and ice.
Peace meant more than the absence of death. It meant administering those who were not yet allowed to exist.
On the morning after the Festival, Sava received two hundred and eighty-four applications. The system had sorted them by urgency, which usually meant by the likelihood that the applicants would cause a scene.
The first to enter were two growers from the South Root district. The woman was visibly pregnant, though their permit had been deferred for four years. She kept her auditory sails tightly folded and breathed through her mouth so as not to smell the office’s antiseptic.
“We’re not asking for an exception,” her partner said. “We’re asking for it not to be called a violation.”
“A deferral was issued,” Sava replied. “You acknowledged receipt.”
“We acknowledged that we’d read it. Her body acknowledged nothing.”
The woman looked up. Nivari eyes did not meet easily; to look directly, they had to turn almost the whole head. Her gesture was therefore more brutal than an accusation.
“When we received the first deferral, I was twelve,” she said. “At the second, I was fifteen. Now I’m nineteen. I may have six more years in which I can give birth. How much future must we be given before it becomes a form of no?”
Sava opened the file. The couple’s genetic prospects were good. But the southern districts’ food reserves were below the threshold, and seven hundred births had been moved to the next cycle.
“The child will not be taken away,” she said. “No law allows that.”
Her partner gave a short laugh.
“Of course. We’ll only lose our housing rights, our extra ration and our trades. The child will stay with us while we’re moved towards the edge of the Night. Very peaceful.”
Sava felt one of her sails turning towards the door. Her body was assessing the exit, though there was no danger. She forced her attention back.
“I can reclassify the pregnancy as documented contraceptive failure. You’ll receive a labour sanction, not relocation.”
“It wasn’t a failure,” the woman said. “We wanted it.”
“Then I can’t help you.”
“You can help us. You don’t want to lie to the system.”
“The system isn’t a person I can lie to.”
“Then why are you sitting here?”
After they left, Sava kept the file open. She changed a nutrient-absorption parameter and sent it through the model again. The application went from red to yellow. Probable contraceptive failure. A sanction of a hundred and twenty hours of public maintenance.
The growers left behind two wisps of grey down and the sweet smell of a pregnancy approaching term. Sava called up the next file, but the system asked her to confirm the sanction. The cursor pulsed above the word *intentional*.
She changed it to *probably accidental*.
In the Chamber of Measure, mercy had no field of its own. It had to be entered as measurement error, data loss or sample contamination. Good officials learned to falsify just little enough for the whole to remain true. Perfect officials no longer noticed they were doing it.
Sava remembered her first day at work. She had been thirteen and was still in mourning for her parents. Her mentor, Lio Marr, had shown her the map of the Belt and told her to look at the red points.
“Each one is someone we say no to,” he had explained. “If we cannot bear that, we’ll say yes to everyone, and in twenty years there will be no one left to say anything to.”
At the time, the sentence had seemed mature. Now it seemed only a blade kept well sharpened.
The rest of the morning left her no time to think about her mentor. The Chamber called the next applicants, and the wall projected a family so large that the silhouettes had to be shrunk to fit.
They called themselves the House of Cloth, though they belonged to no political House. Twelve adults lived together on a mushroom farm, raised eighteen children and were requesting four births in the same cycle. The eldest of them spoke: Yara, with broad sails and down cut short from working among spores.
“Your model treats us as twelve separate pairs,” she said. “We share food, sleep and care. A child doesn’t lose a parent when there are eleven others around.”
“The model treats consumption,” Sava replied. “Four simultaneous pregnancies reduce your production at precisely the time your needs increase.”
“Then we rearrange our shifts.”
“You’ve had two deficits in the past five years.”
“Because the city sent diseased spores and called it harvest variation.”
The official beside Sava opened the history. Yara was right: the loss had been assigned to the farm, though it came from a contaminated central batch. If the error was corrected, the family met the threshold for three births, not four.
“Three now, the fourth after two cycles of stable production,” Sava said.
Yara did not accept immediately.
“Which of the four people waits?”
The Chamber could calculate rations, obstetric risks and genetic diversity. It could not decide who would bear the deferral without turning administrative order into an intimate hierarchy.
“You decide,” Sava said.
“Then you’re not giving us three permits. You’re giving us a conflict.”
“The alternative is for me to choose the body.”
Yara looked at her with a weariness free of hostility.
“You say you don’t intrude into the family. Then you leave exactly enough food for the family to intrude into its own bodies.”
In the end, they accepted. By the time they left, the twelve had already divided into two camps. Sava heard them arguing over who had worked more and who had waited longer. Her decision had preserved the Belt’s arithmetic and moved the violence inside a house.
The next file belonged to an orbital-mirror technician, Lir Sen, and his partner. Service in the swarm automatically gave him reproductive priority because of radiation and mortality. Neither of them wanted children. They asked for the right to be transferred to his partner’s sister, who had been deferred three times.
“Permits aren’t property,” the official explained. “They can’t be transferred.”
“Then why does my service produce them?” Lir asked. “If they’re compensation, let me choose what they compensate for.”
“They are a measure to ensure the continuity of exposed lineages.”
“I don’t want the continuity of my lineage.”
Sava read the sister’s assessment. She was healthy, a grower in a region with a temporary surplus. She had been rejected only because the order of the lists gave precedence to hazardous occupations.
“I can’t transfer the right,” she said. “I can correct the region’s priority and open a new place.”
The official leaned towards her.
“The surplus is projected for a single cycle.”
“Then the authorisation is tied to that cycle.”
“What if the harvest falls?”
“We’ll reduce other consumption.”
“Which?”
Sava stopped. Every act of mercy required an accounting victim, even if it had no name yet. Lir was looking at her. He had not come to ask for generosity, but consistency.
“The district’s ceremonial budget,” Sava said. “The Festival has a two-per-cent reserve.”
The official blinked.
“Ceremonies are protected.”
“Children aren’t?”
The permit was issued through a budget correction. A few weeks later, the district would protest that the Festival routes could not be fully repaired. No one would connect the old railing with a child not yet born. The Chamber lived on such invisible connections.
The last applicants of the cycle wore around their necks the black thread of the Whole Hearing, an old tradition from the galleries of the Night. They believed that every birth brought the community a combination of hearing that had never existed before, and that choosing embryos by scores meant closing off part of the world before it could be heard. They had refused the mandatory genetic testing.
“The test doesn’t oblige you to select,” Sava said. “It only identifies risks.”
“An identified risk becomes a reason for deferral,” the man replied.
“Sometimes.”
“Then the test chooses even if we don’t.”
His partner had a mutation that could cause complete deafness in one child in six. The Chamber recommended an embryo without the marker. The two wanted random conception.
“If the child is born without hearing,” Sava asked, “can the community support the implants and tactile education?”
“Yes.”
“Do you have space?”
“We’re building.”
“Food?”
“We share.”
“Promises aren’t enough.”
The woman untied her black thread and laid it on the table.
“Your model doesn’t promise there will be food in fifteen years either. It only turns its uncertainty into authority over me.”
Sava approved the birth, requiring a community care fund. She did not know whether she had protected autonomy or transferred a risk onto a child who could not consent. The Chamber demanded that every question end in a green or red field. Life continued in areas the interface could not colour.
After they left, the room stood empty for a few minutes. The official sent Sava the cycle report: seven approvals, nine deferrals, two sanctions, one budget correction. The equity indicator had risen by a tenth. No column showed the quarrels, the shame or the future moved from one body to another.
Then the system automatically opened her own file.
The next cases followed without a break. A family line requested reproductive rights after its only child died in Low Square. An elderly woman sought custody of the three newborns her daughter had hidden in a heating pipe. A high-ranking official asked for his embryo to be exempted from screening for “affective instability”, because the trait had belonged to a famous composer.
At midday, Sava reached her own file.
She had not opened it for eight months.
ILEN–VEN APPLICATION / SECOND BIRTH / DEFERRED: 5.7 YEARS.
The official reason was the district’s density. The real reason appeared only in the professional layer of the data: Nemi carried a rare combination of auditory hyperreactivity and a tendency to panic. She was not ill. She was simply closer to the ancestors than the model liked. The Chamber recommended that the line not be multiplied until a compensating partner appeared in the next generation.
Taren, with his lack of flight, was considered equally unsuitable from the opposite direction.
Her personal file remained closed, but the number 5.7 continued to exist at the edge of her vision. Five years and seven tenths were not a future. Nivari had no true seasons on Arua; they measured time through changes in orbital light, harvest cycles and the rapid ageing of children. In 5.7 years, Nemi would be almost an adult. Sava would be twenty-nine, and the risk of giving birth would have risen enough for the model to recommend another deferral.
During lunch, Oru came to the Chamber with two containers of mycelium soup. He was not allowed in the decision area, so they ate in a corridor niche beside a decorative map of the ancient migrations.
“Nemi is asleep,” he said. “Taren is sitting beside her door, frightening the nurses.”
“How is he frightening them?”
“He asks why they give her sedatives before explaining what sounds she’s hearing.”
“That isn’t a medical question. It’s an accusation with a question mark.”
Oru held out the container. Sava took it, trying not to show him that her hands were shaking.
“You opened our application again,” he said.
It was not a question. In families, the systems shared too many traces for complete lies.
“It appeared automatically.”
“And?”
“Nothing new.”
“Five point seven?”
Sava looked at him.
“You checked.”
“You hid the notification.”
“I didn’t want this conversation today.”
“You chose the day for both of us.”
A woman passed through the corridor and lowered her sails, pretending not to hear. Privacy in Ivarra did not mean the absence of witnesses, but their agreement not to remember.
“The model says Nemi carries my family’s hyperreactivity,” Sava said. “And the Ilen line has Taren’s deviation too. It recommends that we wait for genetic compensation.”
“A child isn’t a statistical repair.”
“Precisely because a child isn’t, we can’t ignore what we pass on.”
“We gave Nemi good hearing, a good mind and a mother who knows every reason she should never have existed.”
Sava put the container down.
“Don’t say that.”
“I’m not the one saying it. The Chamber says it. You only lend it your voice.”
Oru regretted it at once. It showed in the way both sails lowered, but the sentence remained between them.
“I changed the growers’ file,” Sava said after a while.
“What file?”
“An unauthorised pregnancy. I spared them relocation.”
“And that’s supposed to prove to me—”
“I’m telling you I’m not the Chamber’s voice.”
“Then who are you when it comes to us?”
The answer was interrupted by the vibration of a priority message. The Custodian’s white seal appeared on Sava’s wrist.
The lift to the Lower Archive did not descend immediately. The doors closed, the light went out, and the cabin remained motionless while the system checked the occupant’s scent, body mass, heart rhythm and vibrational profile. The two agents beside Sava did not speak. They carried no visible weapons, but the soles of their uniforms had heavy inserts, unusual in a city where the floor was meant to be felt.
As they descended, the sound of Ivarra thinned. First the voices disappeared, then the district pumps, then the tremor of the trains. Only the friction of the cable and her own heart remained. Sava’s body had not been built for such isolation from the herd. Her sails swivelled uselessly, searching for information.
“How far down are we going?” she asked.
The agent on her right answered:
“Below what is counted.”
The cabin stopped with a jerk. The door opened onto the grey alloy of an age the capital preferred to keep in museums. The air was cold and dry and carried a faint smell of living protein.
Sava took two steps, then saw Seln Aru beside the first tank.
In the transparent liquid floated a mouth not yet born.
CHAPTER V
THE LOWER ARCHIVE
SAVA AND TAREN · IVARRA · THE END OF THE WEEK
The Cleansing had turned predators into exhibits and peace into infrastructure. The Lower Archive preserved proof that neither had been final.
The Lower Archive did not exist on the public plans. Sava descended in an unmarked lift with two agents of the Quiet. The door opened beneath the level of the old tanks, into a gallery so low that she had to keep her auditory sails folded. The walls were not grown from root but cast in a grey alloy, technology from the time of the Cleansing.
Seln Aru was waiting beside a transparent tank.
A saryx embryo floated inside.
It was the size of her palm, its wings drawn around its body. A thin cord joined it to a nutrient membrane. Beyond the first tank stretched dozens more, some empty, others inhabited by shapes Sava recognised from textbooks on the vanished ecosystem: fissure hunters, worms with radial jaws, blind birds that sensed the warmth of blood.
“The tanks were declared emptied,” she said.
“They were declared closed,” the Custodian replied.
“The report uses the word ‘sterilised’.”
“The public report.”
Seln moved his fingers across the glass of the tank. The embryo contracted.
“The Cleansing was the greatest biological intervention in our history. We eliminated ninety-two species that ate us, parasitised us or competed with us for edible roots. Twenty-three were essential nodes in the food webs. We replaced their functions with engineered organisms. Most systems work. Some only appear to.”
“You keep copies for restoration.”
“We keep options.”
“And one of the options ate a supervisor.”
The Custodian turned his head towards her. His whole attention was cold, not aggressive.
“One of the options was released. It did not escape.”
Sava felt the impulse to look behind her.
“By whom?”
“Access was authorised under the identity of Reu Tal, a former curator of the genetic museum.”
“I don’t know the name.”
“He died thirty-one years ago.”
An agent held out a data slate. The recording showed the seal opening four days before the Festival. A completely covered figure entered the room and transferred three incubators onto a service trolley. The image preserved no scent, temperature or proportions. The Great Hearing had reconstructed the route as far as the tanks beneath the square, where the signal was lost.
“Three,” Sava said.
“Yes.”
“Taren heard two answers beneath the floor.”
Seln did not seem surprised.
“Your brother notices things the others cover with their own noise.”
“Why did you summon me?”
“Because you work with populations. I want to know who benefits from fear.”
“Anyone who sells shelters, medicines or authority.”
“Too general.”
Seln set off along the tanks. Sava followed, the agents keeping their distance.
“Over the past twenty cycles,” he continued, “the border provinces have requested exemptions from the biological protection law. They say the replacement organisms no longer keep the root consumers under control. They say clandestine gangs steal harvests and retreat into the Night. They ask for the right to form guards, to carry thermal projectiles, to kill.”
“The Chamber refused.”
“Correct.”
“You think a provincial House released the saryx to demonstrate the need for weapons.”
“I think peace produces industries of danger just as war produces industries of safety.”
They reached an empty tank. Fresh traces of condensation remained on the glass.
Sava approached the empty tank. There was a recent scratch on its metal rim, hastily covered with lacquer. A sweetish smell, the same one she had noticed on the fragment Taren brought, came from the ventilation filter.
“How many mature specimens were kept here?” she asked.
“None,” Seln replied.
“You said the animal was released, not raised.”
“It was taken out as an embryo. Incubation took place in the museum tanks.”
“Using what food?”
“The biomass missing from the agricultural chambers.”
Sava looked at the data slate again. The route had gaps precisely where the public maps became schematic. Someone not only knew the city beneath the city, but knew what the Great Hearing could not see.
“Why don’t you stop all the trains and search the Houses?”
“Because fear needs a story before it will accept a search. If we supply it, the author of the attack will receive more than they asked for.”
“Aren’t thirty-seven dead enough for a story?”
Seln laid his palm on the glass of a tank. Inside, a small creature with radial jaws contracted towards the warmth.
“Too many for truth and too few for stability,” he said. “Families want personal answers. Institutions can offer only repeatable ones.”
“That is a way of saying lie.”
“It is a way of saying time.”
Sava understood then why the Custodian always seemed tired. Not because doubt gnawed at him, but because he considered himself the only adult in a world of quick reactions. It was a cold arrogance, but not a simple one. He really did see how many systems could break.
“The domain of Vaor has lost twenty-one per cent of its harvest,” Seln said. “Its lord, Daran Veyr, is in the capital for his year of residence. Today he will request defence rights again.”
“And you will refuse again.”
“Of course.”
“Then what do you want from me?”
“For you to go to Vaor and verify the data.”
Sava stopped.
“I’m not an agricultural inspector.”
“No. You are one of the few demographers who have ever corrected a decision of the Great Hearing without leaving enough traces for a sanction.”
Sava held her breath.
“I don’t know what you mean.”
“The growers from South Root will be grateful.”
Seln kept walking. There was no threat in his voice. That was why it was a good threat.
“Vaor declares four hundred thousand inhabitants,” he said. “Consumption indicates at least four hundred and seventy thousand. Either they are hiding births, or they are feeding something that does not appear in the registers. I want to know which possibility is more dangerous.”
“And Taren?”
“What about him?”
“You didn’t bring me here to talk only about figures.”
The Custodian stopped at the end of the gallery. Behind the glass, a fissure hunter opened its many eyes and closed them again.
“In our society, each generation contains a few thousand individuals with a reduced flight reflex. Most die in accidents before maturity or are chemically stabilised. Some become good technicians. A few seek danger with the persistence of those who want to prove that their diagnosis is an insult.”
“Taren doesn’t seek danger.”
“Yesterday he went towards it.”
“To save a child.”
“Motives are the stories behaviour tells after it has happened.”
Sava raised both sails.
“You’re talking about my brother as though he were a variable.”
“That is what you do with other people’s children.”
The blow was precise. Sava could find no answer that was not defensive.
“I do not intend to arrest him,” Seln continued. “Nor to turn him into a symbol. Both would be mistakes. He will go with you to Vaor as your security officer.”
Sava laughed in disbelief.
“There is no such position.”
“There will be by sunset.”
“You believe the saryx’s release is connected to Daran Veyr, and you’re sending him the very individual who killed one?”
“I am sending a question to a place that claims to need an answer.”
“And if you don’t like the answer?”
Seln folded one sail in an almost amiable gesture.
After giving Sava her assignment, Seln led her into a side room. There were no tanks there, but old maps of the Belt, drawn before the Great Hearing. The provinces appeared as networks of independent tunnels linked by narrow corridors. Each House marked its territory with a symbol of dentition: long chisels, split chisels, edges painted with ochre.
“Before the Quiet,” Seln said, “one House did not conquer another’s city. It blocked its water and waited for births to do the rest.”
“I know the history.”
“You know the version in which the Custodian persuaded the warriors to lay down their weapons. The truth is that they were exhausted. Peace won because it allowed them to keep their descendants and some of their prestige.”
He pointed to a circle drawn around Ivarra.
“The Houses’ annual residence is not a ceremony. Nor are the Gardens of Kinship an elite school. They are well-treated hostages. If Vaor rises, Daran knows that half his nephews, nieces and cousins sleep under my control.”
“And yet you think he would release a saryx in their square.”
“Perhaps precisely because they are there. A leader who wants to change the rule must show that the rule does not protect even his own.”
Sava looked at the maps. Four centuries of peace had removed the weapons, but not the architecture of suspicion. The provincial cities sent their children to the capital; the capital sent them food, light and models. Each side called the exchange cooperation so as not to have to call it a guarantee.
Seln detached the map of Ivarra from its frame. Behind it was a handleless door fitted so closely into the alloy that Sava had mistaken it for the wall. The Custodian pressed his wrist against an old reader. The mechanism answered with a heavy crack, unlike the discreet sounds of the modern city.
“Since you know the history,” he said, “you should see the place where it stopped.”
A narrow staircase descended beyond the door. The air was dry and tasted of metal. No living root crossed the walls; the builders of the Cleansing had feared that the city’s organisms could carry poison, spores or signals between the factions. Each surface had been isolated from the others. Sava could hear her footsteps without the reassuring vibration through which inhabited buildings usually answered the body.
The room below did not look like a monument. It was a defensive shelter left almost intact: water containers, dismantled filters, sleeping hollows dug one above another, and a pipe plugged with fragments of furniture. Pale circles marked the floor, traces of the vessels in which emergency food had been grown. Along one wall, someone had scratched lines in groups of nine.
“The third siege of Ivarra,” Seln said. “Eighteen hundred people stayed here for twenty-six days. The House of Ochre controlled the water gallery. The House of the Core controlled the exits to the surface. Each waited for the other to run first.”
Sava counted the groups of lines until she gave up.
“Who won?”
“The fungus in the filters.”
He lifted a mineralised sieve. Black filaments remained imprinted in its mesh.
“It came in from the food tanks. It sickened both sides. When the gates opened, the warriors could no longer hold their weapons, and those they were supposed to protect were trying to kill them for water.”
In a lower sleeping hollow lay a small belt reinforced with bone plates. It had been made for an adolescent. On the inside, its owner had scratched three names and, beneath them, a series of shorter notches.
“Couriers,” Seln said. “Small bodies fitted through the ventilation ducts. The Houses selected for hearing, speed of maturation and the ability to stay awake under stress. The children who returned were praised. Those who refused were called gentle and received less food.”
“The textbooks say the warriors kept children out of combat.”
“The textbooks are written for the children we want to keep out of combat.”
Sava touched the belt. The material crumbled at the edge, and she withdrew her fingers.
At the far end of the shelter, a door had been welded shut from the outside. Old blows marked it, some at adult height, others much lower.
“Who was in there?”
“The sick. The first Aru was the quartermaster who ordered it sealed. He did not found the Quiet with a speech. He kept the infection from rising into the city and left two hundred and twelve people to die behind this door.”
Sava turned towards him. For the first time, the Custodian’s name no longer seemed a neutral title, but an inheritance.
“And he was rewarded.”
“He was the only administrator who still had water, registers and a functioning distribution network. The reward came after necessity, then was rewritten as virtue.”
“Why do you keep this?”
“For the same reason we keep the embryos. An institution that destroys its past runs out of warnings. One that exposes it to everyone risks turning the warnings into models.”
Sava looked at the welded door. The argument repelled her precisely because she understood the mechanism. Children learned by imitation. So did Houses. An atrocity described precisely enough could become an instruction.
“Did you inherit the decision too?” she asked.
“I inherited the conditions in which someone will have to make it again.”
“Is that what you tell yourself so you can sleep?”
Seln ran his fingers over one of the low dents.
“I don’t sleep well.”
The answer asked for no pity. That was precisely why Sava believed him.
On the way up, she noticed thin channels cut into the steps. They had been made to drain blood or collect water; she could not decide. In modern cities, every structure had one declared function and ten hidden ones. In the old shelter, the functions had never been separated.
When they reached the maps again, their gentle light seemed indecently clean. Seln put the plan of Ivarra back over the door, covering the shelter with the drawing of a pacified capital.
“What aren’t you telling me?” she asked.
Seln raised a sail, almost appreciatively.
“Many things. Some because I don’t know them.”
“About Taren.”
“Your brother is not the first individual with a reduced reflex to be used in a time of crisis. During the Cleansing, such individuals entered nests, led predators into traps and stayed beside unstable machinery. Afterwards, almost all their lines were reproductively discouraged.”
“Why?”
“Because a society may need a few who do not run. It cannot trust an entire population that does not know when to.”
Sava felt anger rising in her throat.
“The Chamber still screens out the trait.”
“The Chamber screens out many biological memories of the age when we killed one another.”
“And now you’re sending Taren to do exactly the thing for which you prevented his line from continuing.”
“I did not build the contradiction. I merely recognised it in time.”
•   •   •
On returning to the inhabited levels, Sava found Daran Veyr waiting in the provincial audience hall. He was a head taller than the Custodian and wore Vaor’s brown cloak, reinforced at the edges with plates of fossil wood.
