At first, Vaor's inhabitants avoided them. When a patrol passed, conversations stopped and children were pulled towards the walls. After the depots remained full for three weeks in a row, fear changed into something else.
Growers began leaving warm portions at the entrances to the posts. Traders offered clothes without payment. Families with adolescents came to ask when the next selection round would open. Songs about Leth appeared on the local channels, the young man who had run and who, in the songs, had no longer run.
The mating register changed fastest.
Vessa showed Taren the figures one evening in the infirmary set up beneath the House courtyard. A screen displayed a hundred names and, beside each, the number of private bonding requests. Members of the First Face received, on average, nine times as many messages as other young people of the same age. Those wounded in the depot fight received twenty-three times as many.
“You can't tell them to stop,” Vessa said. “The messages are legal.”
“The births aren't.”
“In the lower chambers, that didn't stop them before, either.”
Taren scrolled through the list. His name had no number, only an overload sign.
“Delete them.”
“They aren't mine.”
“Then don't show them to me.”
“You need to understand what you've done.”
“I guarded food.”
“You changed what is desired.”
Taren looked up. Vessa's coat was open at the neck, and the scar on her right sail ran almost to her temple. She did not have the smooth beauty of those selected by the capital's registers. Her body preserved accidents, deprivations and decisions.
“Do you think it's bad?” he asked.
Vessa had not shown him the register to provoke him. The figures frightened her. Until then, the unregistered population had chosen partners mainly according to proximity, resources and sufficiently distant kinship. In just six weeks, the Face symbol had become a stronger reproductive advantage than living space.
“It isn't just attraction,” she explained to Sava. “Families believe a child connected to a fighter will get food and protection.”
“Will they?”
“Daran makes sure they do.”
“Then it isn't natural selection. It's resource policy.”
“Nature doesn't ask through which institution the resource reaches the body.”
Sava examined the messages. Some were direct, others almost commercial. Whole families offered a better room, access to food or care for existing children in exchange for a bond with a member of the unit. It was not the romance simplified by the songs. It was a population shifting its risk towards those who seemed able to carry it.
“We have to separate rations from unit status,” she said.
“Then the fighters will refuse patrols.”
“People repair pipes without receiving nine bonding requests.”
“Because no one sees them when they succeed.”
Vessa looked at her with an almost tender weariness.
“You think the problem is that Taren is becoming too important. The bigger problem is that everyone else is beginning to feel useless.”
That evening, Sava found Taren repairing a net alone. She told him about the figures.
“I can't control what they want,” he replied.
“You control who gets food, rooms and the sign.”
“I don't allocate rooms.”
“Daran does it in your name.”
“Then stop him.”
“That's exactly what I'm trying to do.”
Taren passed the thread through the net. His fingers moved clumsily; fighting came more easily to him than fine work.
“All my life you've told me my trait mustn't be passed on,” he said. “Now you're telling me it's being passed on too fast. I don't know what you want me to do with that change, Sava.”
“Understand that it isn't about you.”
“Everyone tells me that while arranging things around me.”
The following day, Daran Veyr granted every member of the unit a provisional right to one birth, transferred from the House quota. He had authority to allocate only twelve such rights a year. He issued a hundred.
Sava entered his hall with the documents in her hand and threw them onto the root table.
“Revoke them.”
Daran went on eating. He was cutting a piece of glassback meat, a food forbidden in Ivarra that had become a delicacy in Vaor.
“On what grounds?”
“There is no quota. There is no food. There is no space.”
“Food has increased by fourteen per cent since Taren's patrols began protecting the pipes.”
“For two months. These children will eat for twenty years.”
“Some will die before then.”
“That is not a planning model.”
“It's the only model nature uses.”
Sava pushed one of the documents under his hand.
“You granted the right not to the workers repairing the network, not to doctors, not to growers. To those who wear the sign of the Face.”
“They made the surplus possible.”
“And you want more like them.”
Daran chewed slowly.
“Don't you?”
“I want a population not to be built to suit the fashion of a crisis.”
“Your Chamber builds it to suit the fashion of peace. You select obedience, a rapid alarm response, tolerance of crowding. Through postponement, you eliminate everything that makes administration difficult. I choose individuals who stay when a pipe breaks.”
“You choose individuals who guard your power.”
“Of course. Just as Ivarra chooses individuals who count it.”
Daran kept the issued permits. Sava marked them as disputed, and the Great Hearing could not validate them while the population register remained incomplete. In practice, nothing stopped. Couples formed, pregnancies appeared, rooms were divided.
At the cloak ceremony for the second group, Daran climbed onto the platform uninvited. He spoke of Vaor's continuity, of children who no longer had to be hidden, and of those who stood between food and chaos. Taren let him finish. Then he spoke.
“The sign doesn't make you more valuable,” he told the recruits. “It makes you more visible when you're wrong. The food you guard isn't yours. The person who runs isn't prey. Someone who surrenders isn't material for your story.”
The words were good. Yet the crowd listened chiefly to the grey cloak, the scar on his shoulder and the fact that Daran stood one pace behind him.
After the ceremony, a mother brought him a six-year-old child and asked him to test the child's reflex.
“Too young,” Taren said.
“Not for the Chamber. The Chamber assessed him at three.”
“We aren't the Chamber.”
“Not yet,” the woman replied.
Taren looked at the crowded courtyard. On one side, Oru was teaching children to graft a root. On the other, Raal was showing recruits how to take a weapon from someone's hand. The activities took place beneath the same roof, but only one drew spectators.
For the first time, Taren understood Seln's warning. He had not merely become an answer. He had begun to change the questions.
CHAPTER X
THE CHILD WITHOUT A NUMBER
ORU AND TAREN · VAOR · THE TWELVE DAYS OF ISOLATION
Vaor was not threatened by a single shortage. Food, light, space and legitimacy supported one another; when one failed, all the others became political.
In the seventh week, Oru took Nemi to the surface. It was not a children's outing. He needed someone small enough to enter a secondary channel and attentive enough to tell him what she saw. Sava would have refused, but she was meeting Daran, contesting the First Face's permits.
They emerged through a covered shaft. The Belt's air struck them cold, laden with minerals and the sour smell of silicon ferns. There was nothing overhead. Nemi immediately crouched beneath the edge of the hatch.
“We don't have to go far,” Oru said.
“How far is far?”
“Twelve steps.”
“Yours or mine?”
“Yours. I'm old and I cheat.”
He was twenty-four, but Nemi accepted the joke. They came out. The red light of the star Vei fell almost parallel to the ground and stretched their shadows to the next hill. To the west, the Night was an ever-present blue edge. To the east, the air shimmered with heat.
Oru opened the channel. Inside, the nutrient roots were covered in a white crust. Nemi crawled in and touched the central fibre.
“It's soft.”
“Where?”
“Beyond the node. And it smells sweet.”
Oru cut a sample. The root bled sugar-rich sap, exactly the signal that attracted glassbacks.
“Sick?” Nemi asked.
“Tired.”
“Do roots get tired?”
“Everything that does someone else's work for too long gets tired.”
In the field, a grey herd moved unhurriedly. The animals did not lift their heads at the sight of them. They had no enemies. They ate whatever they found and reproduced until the food ran out. Oru looked at the bare ground behind them and sensed in the image a simplified version of his own people.
“Why don't they run away from us?” Nemi asked.
“They haven't learned they should.”
“Are they like Taren?”
“No. Taren knows he should. He just has time to ask himself whether it's worth it.”
A glassback approached. Nemi pressed against him. Oru raised a rod, and the animal bit its end. It was heavy, healthy and absurdly incautious.
“When you take something out of a world,” Oru said, “it isn't only that thing that disappears. Everything that no longer has to respond to it changes.”
“The saryxes?”
“The saryxes too. And us.”
Oru was waiting for her in the agricultural tunnels. In three months he had grown thin because he forgot to eat while working, but his eyes were alive. He had uncovered an old section of the root network and was projecting flow models above it.
“I've found why the crops are failing,” he said.
“They're being eaten.”
“That's the symptom. Look.”
He enlarged the image of a root. Mineral deposits were constricting the transport fibres.
“For millions of years, the Belt's soil was fertilised by the carcasses of large predators and their excretions. After the Cleansing, we replaced the cycle with engineered bacteria. The bacteria began losing their synthetic plasmids thirty years ago. Ivarra compensates with nutrients from reactors. Vaor gets too little. The roots weaken, the animals sense the released sugar, and they come.”
“Can you repair the bacteria?”
“Temporarily. But the ecosystem will find another way to escape the function we've imposed on it.”
“What do you propose?”
Oru opened a second image. A slender animal with four limbs and long jaws pursued a herd of glassbacks in an ecological reconstruction.
“Edge hunters. They ate root consumers and moved nutrients between areas. There are still embryos in the capital's reservoirs.”
Sava felt a cold that did not come from the tunnel.
“You want to bring predators back.”
“Sterile. Monitored. Few.”
“After the Low Square?”
“Precisely after the Low Square. We confused safety with simplification. We removed everything that could hurt us and discovered that some of it had been holding the world up.”
Sava watched the simulation. The hunters ran in arcs, choosing the weak individuals. The herd moved, and behind it the soil recovered.
“Taren will love the idea.”
“I'm not doing this for Taren.”
“No one thinks they're doing anything for him. And everything is beginning to arrange itself around him.”
Oru closed the model.
“Not around him. Around a need. If it hadn't been Taren, Vaor would have invented someone else.”
Ivarra rejected the restoration project in seven minutes. The reply bore the Great Hearing's automatic signature and cited psychological risk to the population. Oru submitted it again, with crop-loss estimates. It was rejected in three minutes.
The third time, he attached images of the malnourished children in the hidden chambers.
Sava found Oru's message about the edge hunters just as she was leaving Daran's hall. The permits remained active, and Vaor's master had told her politely that a rule impossible to enforce was no longer a rule, but decoration.
She met Oru in the agricultural tunnel. Nemi slept in a tool cart, a sample of white crust in her fist.
“You took her to the surface without telling me.”
“If I'd told you, you'd have refused.”
“That's not an argument.”
“It's a prediction.”
Sava wanted to argue, but she saw how thin he had become. His shoulder bones stood out beneath the fur. He spent his nights among the pipes and his days explaining to the locals why repairs could not produce food immediately.
“Have you eaten?” she asked.
“Nemi has.”
“That isn't what I asked.”
“Then probably not.”
She took a ration from her bag and put it in his hand. Oru ate without resisting. After a few mouthfuls, he said:
“The hunters are the right solution.”
“There is no right solution. Only one whose problems we can't see yet.”
“I know. But I prefer a living problem that adapts to a dead network we force to imitate an ecosystem.”
“And if one attacks a child?”
“Then it will be killed.”
“By Taren.”
Oru put the food down.
“Not every sentence has to end with your brother.”
“In Vaor, more and more of them do.”
“Perhaps because you push them there. So you don't have to see that the problem was already in the system you're defending.”
Sava looked at Nemi. The little girl slept with one sail raised, listening even in sleep.
“I'm afraid,” Sava said.
Oru did not answer at once. She rarely used the word without adding a probability.
“Of what, exactly?”
“That Taren is right too soon. That Daran is making him necessary. That you're bringing predators back and I can no longer tell whether I'm protecting the world or only the version of it I know how to work in.”
Oru touched her hand.
“Then stay close enough to see when we're wrong.”
It was the last quiet promise between them.
Instead, Seln Aru requested a direct conversation.
His face appeared on the apartment wall, slightly distorted by the transmission delay. Behind him, a white gallery in Ivarra and part of a mourning flag were visible.
“Your report is overdue,” he told Sava.
“The data are more complicated than the estimate.”
“Does the hidden population exist?”
“Yes.”
“Has Daran Veyr formed a unit beyond the authorised limit?”
“Yes.”
“Does your brother command it?”
“Officially, he trains it.”
“Sava.”
“Yes.”
Seln folded one sail, a sign of fatigue.
“The dissolution order goes out today. Taren must return to the capital for assessment. Daran will be replaced pending the inquiry. The unregistered population will receive temporary status and be redistributed.”
Oru approached the transmission.
“Redistributed where? The central Belt is at capacity.”
“To districts with labour shortages.”
“The edge of the Night,” Sava said.
“Some settlements are there.”
“You'll move tens of thousands of children into the cold to save a table.”
“I'll spread the pressure before it breaks the domain.”
“And the ecological restoration?”
“We will not reintroduce hunting organisms into a traumatised population.”
Oru raised his voice.
“The population will be more traumatised by hunger.”
“Hunger can be distributed. Fear cannot.”
Taren had entered the room without them hearing him. He wore the First Face's grey cloak and a working blade at his hip, still technically an agricultural tool. He stopped within the transmission's range.
“Is that why you're calling me back?” he asked. “To distribute fear?”
Seln looked at him for a long time.
“I'm calling you back because you've become an answer before we've established the question.”
“The question is who guards the food.”
“Today. Tomorrow it will be who guards the guards. After that, who deserves to have children. I see Daran has already reached the third question.”
Taren glanced at Sava. She realised he knew about the permits.
“I didn't ask for them,” he said.
“You don't have to ask for power to benefit from it,” Seln replied. “Return voluntarily. The unit will be absorbed into the agricultural services. No one will be punished.”
“And if not?”
“The trains to Vaor stop. The mirrors' light quotas are revised. Every illegally issued birth right is revoked.”
Daran appeared in the doorway, drawn by the conversation. He smiled without pleasure.
“That's the capital's peace,” he said. “It doesn't hit you. It moves the light a little farther east and waits for you to freeze.”
“Daran Veyr,” Seln said, “you are removed from the administration of the domain until the inquiry is concluded.”
“Come and replace me.”
The transmission broke off.
Seln had not stopped it. All the lights in the room went out at once. The floor stopped vibrating. The ventilation sank into a sigh and died.
For a few moments, there was only the sound of their breathing.
Then, through the rock overhead, the sky lit up.
The solar storm struck Arua's illuminated side like an open hand. The orbital magnetic fields deformed, the mirrors moved into their safe position, and the Great Hearing fragmented into thousands of local nodes. In Vaor, backup systems started one by one, but the link to Ivarra did not return.
Daran took off his ceremonial cloak and left it on the floor.
“How long are we isolated?” he asked.
Oru checked the dead terminal.
“Maybe two days. Maybe twenty.”
“Then, for twenty days, the Custodian's order does not exist.”
Sava turned to Taren.
“The unit stays within the limit. You accept no more recruits. You use no thermal weapons.”
Taren was listening to something else. Far away, from the city's tunnels, came an irregular pounding. Not an alarm signal. Thousands of people were striking the floor, each in a different rhythm, after learning that the link to Ivarra had failed.
“It isn't a unit any more,” he said. “It's the only thing that can receive orders.”
That night, Daran opened House Veyr's museum armoury. The ceremonial blades, magnetic-arc projectiles and old armour had been preserved for four centuries as objects of a shame left behind.
By morning, all were missing from the display cases.
When the museum armoury opened, people did not immediately rush for the weapons. They stood in the doorway, looking at the objects in the cases: curved blades, magnetic bows, armour plates, helmets that forced the auditory sails to remain directed forwards. Every piece carried a label about the barbarity of the past. Some also bore the names of House Veyr's ancestors.
Daran took out the first blade. He tested its balance with a familiarity that betrayed practice in secret.
“They're ceremonial objects,” Sava said.
“Ceremonies are how we preserve an object's use after pretending we've forgotten it.”
Taren took nothing. He kept his working blade.
“Who distributes them?”
“The House.”
“Then only the House will have weapons.”
“The House preserved them.”
“The city preserved the House.”
Raal laughed behind them.
“Here is the first quarrel of the army that doesn't exist.”
Taren divided the weapons among the groups and ordered every piece to be publicly registered. Daran agreed too easily. By morning, a third of the inventory had disappeared before being counted.
•   •   •
The first birth of the new age took place in darkness, beside a draining water tank.
The mother was one of the unregistered, a fourteen-year-old grower named Eri. Labour had begun six hours after the storm, while the main systems were still unresponsive and the House infirmary was occupied by the injured. Vessa found her crouched between two pipes, with three other women holding her body so she would not strike the floor at every contraction.
Taren had come to check the rations. He stayed at the entrance, useless and unable to leave.
“Bring water,” Vessa told him.
“The tank is below the emergency quota.”
“Then bring emergency water.”
He left and returned with his own flask. Vessa used it to wash away the blood. The child was born small, eyes closed and auditory sails pressed to its skull. It made no sound until Vessa tapped it lightly between the shoulder blades. Then its body arched and a thin cry filled the chamber.
All the adults startled.
Taren did not.
Eri noticed.
“Touch him,” she said.
“Why?”
“So the first thing he feels won't be a frightened person.”
Taren held out a finger. The child gripped it with disproportionate strength. There was no authorisation bracelet around the mother's wrist, and the medical terminal could not issue an identity without a connection to the Great Hearing.
“What's his name?” Taren asked.
“Sio.”
“His House name?”
Eri looked at him as if the question were indecent.
“He has none.”
One of the women dipped her finger in the blood on the floor and approached the child. She wanted to trace the First Face's frontal sign on his chest.
Taren caught her hand.
“No.”
“He was born under your protection.”
“Protection isn't ownership.”
“Then what does he get?”
Taren looked at the child without a number, born in a city cut off from the world.
“Time,” he said. “For now, that's all.”
Sio slept through his first hours in a filter box lined with clothes. Vessa had no incubator, and the room's temperature was falling. Taren kept the box beside the hot-water pipe and changed the child's position every few minutes so his skin would not burn.
Eri watched him with eyes clouded by exhaustion.
“You don't look as though you know how to hold a newborn.”
“I don't.”
“Then why don't you give him to someone else?”
“You gave him to me.”
“I wanted to see whether someone who kills can hold something without squeezing too hard.”
“And?”
Eri closed her eyes.
“I don't know yet.”
Nemi came with Oru and sat beside the box. She looked at the child's tiny hands.
“Is he allowed to exist?”
Sava, standing in the doorway, felt the question like a blow.
“He already exists,” Oru replied.
“That's not what I asked.”
No one found an answer that did not reveal too much.
Outside the room, Vaor organised itself in the dark.
During the twelve days of isolation, time became Vaor's currency.
The backup network could sustain full ventilation for nine more days, agricultural lighting for six, heating in the outer chambers for four. Oru reorganised the flows and gained a few hours here, a day there. Sava calculated rations by age and activity. Daran turned the calculations into orders. The First Face enforced them.
Hierarchy appeared before anyone gave it a name.
Those repairing the pipes received larger portions. Those guarding the repairs received the same. Doctors and nursing mothers came next. Children, the elderly and the sick had a protected quota. The rest of the population shared what remained.
On paper, the system worked. In the galleries, every category became a judgement on the value of a life.
On the fifth day, two hundred inhabitants of the Warm quarter gathered at the central depot. They had heard House agents were hiding food. The rumour was false, but not absurd. Daran had indeed kept a private reserve; Taren had discovered it and moved it to the public depot the night before.
The crowd did not know that. They saw only closed doors and grey cloaks in front of them.
“Open up!” someone shouted. “Our children are getting half portions!”
“All children get the same quota,” Taren replied.
“Yours don't.”
“There is no ‘ours’.”
A stone struck his chest. The First Face members raised their working blades. Taren gestured for them to lower them.
The crowd pushed.
Nivari were not built for frontal pressure. At the first contact, those behind leapt forwards while those in front tried to retreat. Bodies became entangled. A woman fell. Taren heard the sound of the Low Square before he saw the danger: the shortening collective breath, claws scraping the floor, panic finding its direction.
He had struck a saryx with a rod. Here, there was no single body against which to direct his strength.
He turned to the depot door and opened it.
The people stopped, surprised. Inside, the shelves were almost empty. Thirty sacks lay on the floor, each marked with the day on which it was to be opened.
“This is all the food,” Taren said. “If you take it now, you'll eat for three days. On the fourth day, you'll come back, and there'll be nothing here. The first person who thinks this is a lie can come in and count.”
An old man walked past him. He counted the sacks. Then he counted them again.
“The House has its own reserve,” someone said.
Taren pointed to the sacks in the corner, their Veyr seals cut.
“That was it.”
Daran, standing behind him, said nothing. His face remained calm, but his smell turned bitter.
“Then why do the guards get full portions?” a woman asked.
Taren held out the blade he wore at his hip.
“Take it. Go to tunnel four. There are seventeen glassbacks beside the air pipe. If they break it, we lose heating for three quarters. Stay there six hours and you get my portion.”
The woman looked at the blade. She did not take it.
“I don't want to be like you.”
“I don't want you to be hungry either. But these are the things we have to choose between.”
The crowd dispersed without violence. That evening, however, someone wrote on the depot wall: THOSE WITH BLADES EAT FIRST.
The sentence on the depot became a real problem. The First Face received full portions because patrols and repairs consumed energy. With every day of isolation, the explanation turned into a privilege. Some members began taking their food before leaving for their posts, afraid none would remain. Others kept portions for their families. Taren punished the first two and discovered that one of them had three hungry brothers.
He took away his cloak for one cycle, then sent the family his own ration. The gesture was retold as proof of nobility. Taren considered it proof that the regulations did not know what they were doing.
Sava calculated the distribution in a cold hall. Daran demanded more for workers, Vessa for the sick, Raal for the children from the Night, and Oru for systems that could not explain why they needed food: technicians, pumps, bacterial cultures. Every request was justified. Their sum exceeded what existed.
“The Chamber does this every day,” Daran said. “Why is it hard for you now?”
“Because here I see the faces.”
“Then perhaps all Chambers should be built beside empty depots.”
Raal came on the seventh day.
His gang emerged from the Night galleries with no visible weapons, carrying three backup batteries and two wounded people. Frost had whitened the edges of their sails. The children walked in the middle, tied to one another with a rope so they would not get lost in the blizzard.
“I heard you opened the depot,” Raal told Taren.
“Not for you.”
“You opened a door. That's enough.”
He offered the batteries in exchange for food and the right to join the unit. Daran wanted them arrested for their previous thefts. Sava requested their temporary registration. Vessa treated them before the argument was over.
Taren took Raal into the training circle.
“You killed a worker,” he said.
“Yes.”
“Why should I trust you?”
“You shouldn't. Put me where your distrust is useful.”
Taren threw him a rod. The suspended trunk began to swing.
Raal did not retreat too early. Nor too late. He turned around the weight and pushed it from behind, redirecting it towards Taren.
Taren barely evaded it.
For the first time, the recruits laughed at their commander.
“Good,” Taren said. “You'll lead the third group.”
“That's it?”
“That's it until you make a mistake.”
“And after?”
“Depends who survives the mistake.”
That same day, Oru opened a portable genetic reservoir Daran had kept in the House archive. Inside were six edge-hunter embryos, stolen decades earlier from the capital's collections. Daran claimed he had not known about them. No one believed him.
Oru showed Sava the sterilisation sequences.
“They can't reproduce. We can stop their metabolism remotely. They'll hunt only glassbacks; their receptors have been restricted.”
“That's what they said about the substitute organisms.”
“The difference is that these evolved for the function. They weren't designed to imitate it.”
“And if one attacks a child?”
Oru looked at her wearily.
“Then Taren will kill them, and you'll remind me for the rest of my life that you were right.”
Sava touched the reservoir's glass. The embryos did not yet resemble anything alive.
“I'm not afraid of being right,” she said. “I'm afraid every problem is starting to have the same solution.”
“Death?”
“A dangerous thing, controlled by someone who promises it will remain useful.”
Oru closed the lid.
The edge hunters were taken out of the reservoir on the ninth day. They had slender bodies, coarse fur and jaws less impressive than in Oru's reconstructions. The first woke slowly, sniffed the air and tried to hide beneath the laboratory table.
“That's the predator?” Nemi asked.
“It's a cub,” Oru said.
“It's afraid.”
“Things that hunt don't stop being things that can be hunted.”
Taren sat at a distance. The animal pointed its muzzle at him and growled. Everyone else took a step back. Taren did not.
“Don't provoke it,” Oru said.
“I'm not doing anything.”
“To it, staying may be something.”
Taren deliberately moved back. The hunter came out from beneath the table. Oru smiled.
“See? Sometimes flight is a tool.”
“Don't tell Sava you won this argument.”
“I'll send her a report in triplicate.”
For a few moments, before the backup lights flickered again, the room resembled a family sharing a joke.
On the eleventh night, Vessa entered Taren's room. There was no mating ritual between them, no request sent to the system. She unfastened her cloak, touched the scar on his shoulder and said:
“I don't want a permit from Daran.”
“Neither do I.”
“I'm not talking about us. I'm talking about a child.”
Taren remained still. In another time, it would have been a declaration of affection. In Vaor, it was also a political, biological, perhaps military decision.
“Did you choose me because I have a delayed response?” he asked.
“I chose you because you know you have it.”
“That's not the same thing.”
“No choice has a single cause. The Chamber lies when it claims it can separate love from statistics.”
Vessa rested her forehead against his.
“The children of the First Face will be born anyway,” she said. “Daran wants that. The families want it. The bodies want it. The difference is whether we acknowledge what we're doing.”
“And what are we doing?”
Vessa did not leave after resting her forehead against his. Taren's room was a guard alcove with a mattress, a lamp and three maps. Nothing suggested someone lived there except an unwashed bowl and a badly folded blanket.
“I don't want to be your symbol,” she said.
“I didn't ask.”
“That's precisely what worries me. The best symbols are made from people who claim they never wanted to be.”
“Then why did you come?”
Vessa picked her cloak up from the floor and hung it on a peg.
“Because when you held Eri's child, you were afraid of breaking him. Because at the depot, I stopped you. Because I don't know how long this part of you will last.”
Taren looked at her face-on. The gesture was hard to sustain; his peripheral field disappeared.
“And you want a child before it disappears?”
“I don't want to leave it only to Daran to decide what reproduces out of the crisis.”
The sentence was too cold for the closeness between them. Vessa knew it. She touched his cheek.
“And I want you,” she added. “I don't know how to separate that from the rest.”
“Neither does the Chamber.”
“The Chamber pretends.”
When the light went out for the third time, they stayed in the dark. The floor carried the city's footsteps, thousands of uncoordinated rhythms. Taren listened until, among them, he began to distinguish Sio's small beats in the next room.
The following day, the Great Hearing returned.
CHAPTER XI
THE GENTLE MIST
SAVA · VAOR STATION · AFTER THE CONNECTION WAS RESTORED
After the connection was restored, neither Ivarra nor Vaor uttered the word war. Each side preferred terms that preserved the appearance that only the other had broken the peace.
The connection to Ivarra returned the following morning.
Every screen lit up at once. Seln Aru's face appeared in houses, depots, hidden chambers and the courtyard where the First Face trained.
“Citizens of Vaor,” he said, “the storm has passed. Planetary order has been restored. Daran Veyr is charged with usurpation, demographic concealment and illegal distribution of weapons. Taren Ilen is suspended from every position and must be handed over to the central authorities. Anyone carrying out his orders after this message excludes themselves from the protection of the Quiet.”
Taren stood in the centre of the courtyard. Around him, four hundred First Face members listened.
The message repeated.
No one moved towards him.
Raal raised a hind limb and struck the floor once.
Vessa struck a second time.
The third beat came from the child without a number, through the heel of the mother holding him to her chest.
Then all of Vaor answered.
The pounding on the floor continued for almost a minute. It had not been planned. It had begun in the First Face's courtyard, passed into the hidden chambers and risen through the official galleries. Some struck three times, others only once. There was no common code yet; there was only the refusal to surrender Taren.
Sava stood at the edge of the courtyard and did not strike. Nemi held her hand. Oru was beside them, dirty from the backup network, two fingers bandaged after a repair.
“What does it mean?” Nemi asked.
“That they don't want him to leave,” Oru said.
“Why isn't Mother answering?”
Sava looked at the four hundred grey cloaks. Around them, people who two months earlier had pulled their children towards the walls were now raising their fists. Daran stayed at the back, letting the image seem spontaneous.
“Because my answer would mean something else,” she said.
“What?”
“That I'm choosing their side.”
“Aren't you with us?”
Sava bent to the child's level.
“I'm with you. With your father. With Taren. With the people here. The problem is that all those things can end up on different sides.”
Nemi freed her hand and struck the floor. Just once. Sava did not stop her.
•   •   •
In the days that followed, words became the adults' preferred weapons.
Wars did not begin in the Long Quiet. Administrative incidents began and refused to end.
Daran Veyr did not declare Vaor's independence. He announced the temporary suspension of compliance with central orders pending assessment of their demographic effects. He did not mobilise an army. He expanded the agricultural protection unit to twelve hundred members. He did not seize the trains. He introduced a health inspection so slow that no transport from Ivarra could pass.
Seln Aru replied with equal care. He did not impose a blockade. He recalculated energy and food quotas based on the domain's actual population, reducing per-person allocations by thirty-one per cent. He did not send troops. He sent mediators, public-order specialists and six carriages of medical equipment.
In the third carriage were two tonnes of aerosolised veyrin.
Sava learned this from the loading list, two hours before the train arrived.
“It's a sedative,” Daran said. “A sedative doesn't conquer a domain.”
“In low concentrations, it reduces the alarm reflex,” she replied. “In high concentrations, it blocks coordination and initiative. They want to come in without a fight.”
Taren was studying the station map. The First Face had occupied the access junctions, but he had forbidden thermal projectiles in inhabited spaces.
“We can stop the train in the outer tunnel.”
“If you break the magnetic conduit, we lose our only transport link to the centre,” Oru said.
“If you let them in, they'll put us to sleep.”
“We don't let them open the tanks.”
Sava looked at Daran.
“Accept the mediators. Send half the guard out of the station. Let them see there's no insurrection.”
“There is,” Daran said.
Sava had known Mern Sai since their training years. They had learned together how to draft a refusal that did not cause panic and how to tell a family that its future had been moved beyond its own reproductive lifetime. Mern was good at it. He did not lie; he arranged truths so the decision seemed inevitable.
Before the train arrived, he sent her a private message.
SAVA. DON'T TURN A LOCAL ERROR INTO A PLANETARY RUPTURE. BRING TAREN TO THE NEGOTIATIONS. I CAN GUARANTEE HE WON'T BE IMPRISONED.
She replied:
CAN YOU GUARANTEE THE POPULATION WON'T BE REDISTRIBUTED?
Mern did not answer directly.
I CAN GUARANTEE INDIVIDUAL ASSESSMENT.
Sava knew what that meant: families separated according to their ability to work, children sent where there were beds, adults to districts requesting labour. No one would be punished. Everyone would be administered until the community disappeared.
Oru read her messages.
“Do you still believe him?”
“I believe him when he says he doesn't want war.”
“And Seln?”
“Yes.”
“Then why are they bringing gas?”
Sava looked at the loading list. Two tonnes. Too much for a simple precaution, too little for the whole city. Calculated for the station, for the leadership and for anyone who might gather to watch.
“Because they think war only begins when someone dies,” she said.
Oru pressed his forehead to hers.
“After this, we're leaving.”
“Where?”
“Anywhere Nemi can sleep without asking what's coming through the floor.”
“That place doesn't exist.”
“Then we'll make it smaller. A room. A tunnel. A life where we don't have to save a civilisation before dinner.”
Sava smiled wearily.
“After the negotiations.”
“After the negotiations,” Oru repeated.
•   •   •
The negotiations lasted forty minutes.
The central delegate, Mern Sai, had been Sava's colleague at the Chamber of Measure. He had the soft voice of those trained not to stimulate panic and wore an unmarked cloak. He demanded Taren's surrender, the unit's disarmament and access to the House registers. In return, he promised amnesty for the unregistered population and a reassessment of quotas.
“Reassessment when?” Sava asked.
“In two cycles.”
“The children eat in the meantime.”
“They'll receive emergency assistance.”
“After you redistribute them.”
Mern folded one sail.
“We cannot administer eighty thousand people in infrastructure designed for fewer.”
“They've been administered until now by people you say aren't competent.”
“They've been hidden and underfed.”
“And now they're seen and threatened.”
Behind Mern, central agents were unloading crates marked medical. Taren recognised the shape of the pressure tanks.
“Stop unloading,” he said.
Mern did not turn.
“First-aid equipment.”
“Then open a crate.”
“I don't answer to orders from a suspended individual.”
Taren gestured. Twenty First Face members advanced.
The central agents raised short white projectors. They were not lethal weapons. In the culture of the Quiet, that distinction carried the weight of absolution.
Mern said a single word:
“Now.”
The tanks opened.
The mist was transparent at first. It came through the valves like slightly cooler air, smelling sweetly of wet bark. Sava felt the effect before she saw the cloud: her muscles relaxed, her auditory sails lowered, and urgency disappeared from her thoughts. She knew she had to flee the station. The idea was clear and utterly unimportant.
Taren drove his chisel teeth into the inside of his lip until he tasted blood. The pain kept him functional.
“Masks!” he shouted.
The First Face had come prepared. Those at the front covered their muzzles and attacked the tanks, not the agents. Raal threw a blade and cut the first pipe. Gas burst towards the ceiling. Vessa pulled Sava behind a panel.
The mist reached Sava like a false kindness. Every urgent thought lost its edges. Mern stood two paces away; she could have hit him, torn off his mask or closed the valve. Instead, she remembered an afternoon at school when they had shared a root cake, and it seemed absurd that two people who knew each other could end up on opposite sides of a table.
Vessa pulled her behind the panel and pressed a mask to her muzzle.
“Take small breaths,” she said.
“It's quiet.”
“It isn't quiet. It's incapacity.”
Around them, the station came apart in slow movements. A child fell and remained on the floor, watching the feet stepping around him. A central agent took off his helmet, probably convinced the medical apparatus was safe. Raal struck him. Mern shouted orders that even his own people no longer considered important.
Oru was in the ventilation room beneath the station.
When the detectors registered the aerosol, the automatic system closed the dampers to prevent its spread. It was the correct procedure for a biological agent. In a crowded station, it meant the gas stayed concentrated exactly where it had been released.
Oru opened the first channel manually. The mechanism crushed two of his fingers, but air began to flow. The second damper was blocked by roots grown over its shaft. He took the cutting tool and entered the duct.
Above, the first central agent died.
Raal struck him with his knee and tore off his mask. The agent fell, inhaled veyrin and lay motionless. A First Face member, a young man from the hidden chambers, raised a museum blade and drove it into his throat.
“He was down!” Taren shouted.
The young man stared at the corpse, astonished by his own arm.
“He was getting up.”
“He wasn't getting up.”
Another white projectile burst against the wall. Taren turned, too late to stop the response. Three central agents were hit by thermal needles fired from the gallery. Their ceremonial armour offered no real protection. One caught fire.
The gentle mist filled with the smell of burning flesh.
Taren struck the floor three times, slowly. The retreat signal. The First Face ignored him. The fight had given each of them a narrow tunnel of attention, and at its end there was no longer a commander, only the body in front of them.
Then Taren threw himself between the two groups.
He took a white needle in his shoulder and a blade in his thigh. He struck Raal with the flat of his weapon, then his own recruit, who was trying to kill a second fallen agent. He shouted until his voice cracked.
“Anyone who strikes after surrender leaves the Face! Anyone who pursues someone fleeing stands alone!”
Taren did not remember the white impact in his shoulder. But he remembered the face of the recruit who had killed the fallen agent. His name was Sef, he was fifteen, and he had been selected for his ability to remain functional under alarm. There was no hatred in his eyes. There was the horror of discovering his hand had done something before the rule reached it.
“Drop the blade,” Taren told him.
Sef did not answer.
“Drop it.”
The blade fell. Taren sent him to the rear, then saw a second body catch fire. The code did not yet exist. It had to be invented while it was being broken.
Mern Sai lay beside the negotiating table, his mask torn. Sava crawled to him.
“Stop the tanks.”
“They're automatic,” he whispered. “Thirty minutes.”
“There are children here.”
Mern looked at her without expression, sedated by his own instrument.
“It was calculated for five minutes of exposure.”
The floor shook.
In the ventilation room, Oru had managed to open the second damper. The accumulated pressure drove the mist into the old ducts. Some went out towards the surface. Some entered the maintenance chambers where dozens of children had taken shelter.
Nemi was among them.
When the gas entered, the children stopped looking for the exit. They sat along the wall, drowsy, while an overheated pipe began burning the insulation above them.
Oru heard the fire alarm and crawled through the duct. The veyrin made his limbs heavy. He found the first grille, tore it off and fell into the children's chamber. He lifted them one by one, pushing them towards the open tunnel. An adult could have carried two. He carried three, returned, then four small ones at once, tied to his body with his own cloak.
On his last return, Nemi had still not come out.
He found her beneath the burning pipe, watching the flame with sedated curiosity. He took her in his arms. The insulation came away from the ceiling.
When Taren arrived, the fire was out, but part of the chamber had collapsed. He pulled out seven children. The eighth was Nemi. Oru held her beneath him, protected from the stone that had crushed his back.
He was still alive.
Sava came running, her mask hanging round her neck. She fell beside him and tried to lift the slab. Taren caught her before she broke her hands.
“We need a jack,” she said. “Oru, stay awake. Vessa's coming.”
Oru blinked slowly. Blood came from his nostrils.
“Nemi?”
“She's alive. She's here.”
The little girl pressed against his shoulder, crying without a sound.
“The dampers,” Oru whispered.
“They're open.”
“Good.”
“Don't talk.”
“You were right.”
Sava brought her forehead close to his.
“About what?”
“Solutions.”
His breathing grew irregular.
“Don't give the dead reasons they didn't have,” he said. “I didn't die for Vaor. Or for Taren. I wanted to get our child out of here.”
Vessa arrived with her instruments. She looked at the position of the slab, the colour of Oru's mucous membranes and the amount of blood. She did not lie.
“I can sedate him.”
Sava made a low, ancestral sound. It was not crying yet. It was the body learning before the mind.
“No,” Oru said. “Enough quiet.”
When the slab fell onto Oru, the air duct opened fully and drew the smoke upwards. For a few seconds, the children's chamber became clear. Later, Nemi remembered that light: red, trembling, with particles of ash rising towards the grille. Her father was above her, and his back no longer had the right shape.
“Don't leave me,” she told him.
“I'm not leaving,” Oru replied.
It was not a promise about life. It was one about the last moments. Nemi did not understand the difference.
After he died, Sava stayed beside the body until Vessa closed his eyes. Taren took Nemi out of the chamber. The little girl bit his arm and struggled to go back.
“He said he wasn't leaving!”
“I know.”
“Then lift the stone!”
Taren held her while she hit, scratched and screamed. The child's body wanted to run from death and towards it at the same time. There was no exercise for that.
•   •   •
By the end of the day, the death toll had reached a hundred and six. Twenty-eight were central agents. Thirteen belonged to the First Face. The rest were inhabitants, most crushed, suffocated or caught in the fire while veyrin suspended their reflexes.
Daran appeared on every Vaor channel and called the event the Mist Massacre. He showed the children's bodies. He showed the armed central agents. He did not show the first First Face member who had killed a fallen opponent.
Seln Aru replied that a non-lethal device had been sabotaged by insurgents and that the deaths were the consequence of armed resistance. He did not show the order to bring two tonnes of aerosol into a crowded station.
Truth was divided so precisely that each side received exactly the amount it needed for hatred.
At Oru's ceremony, Sava refused House Veyr's flag. She also refused the white ribbon sent by the Chamber of Measure. They buried him in the fertile layer beneath a new crop, according to the root engineers' custom.
Taren stood apart. When the ceremony ended, Sava came to him.
“Daran will use his death,” she said.
“I know.”
“Seln will use it.”
“I know.”
“Will you use it?”
Taren looked at the fresh earth.
“I can't promise I won't.”
Sava struck him. Her palm split his lip.
Taren did not defend himself.
“Then at least don't call it courage,” she said.
That night, the First Face wrote its first code on the station wall: no killing after surrender; no pursuing those who flee; guarded food does not belong to the guard; children do not wear the sign; an order demanding the death of the unarmed is not an order.
Beneath the five rules, someone added in dried blood:
PEACE FIRED FIRST.
Taren erased the sentence.
There was not enough room at Oru's ceremony for everyone who had come. The root engineers wore green stains on their forearms, deliberately left there. The children from his workshop had brought a root they had grafted; the joint was crooked and would probably not survive. Sava asked for it to be planted anyway.
Bes, the local technician, told how Oru had stuck his head into a pipe on his first day. Eri said he had repaired the clinic's heating without asking the children's names. Raal stood at the edge, without the sign on his chest. Daran came with an escort and left when Sava refused the House cloth.
After striking Taren, Sava looked at her palm. His blood remained on her fingers.
“I'm sorry,” she said.
“You aren't.”
“No.”
Taren wiped his lip.
“Oru was right about the hunters. They went into the field today. They followed the herd, but they didn't attack anything of ours.”
Sava closed her eyes. Even dead, Oru continued to send consequences.
“Don't make a lesson of this.”
“No.”
“Don't make his death a reason for what comes next.”
“I can't promise.”
After the blow, the code was written on the station wall. Taren reread it several times. Five sentences born of failure, not virtue.
When someone added *PEACE FIRED FIRST*, he rubbed out the words until his fingers bled.
By morning, they had been written again, larger.
That same morning, observers in the Night reported the central column approaching Deaf Lake.
CHAPTER XII
DEAF LAKE
TAREN · THE EDGE OF THE NIGHT · SEVEN WEEKS LATER
Deaf Lake was a place where vibrations were lost in ice and steam. For a species that had built its society on warnings transmitted through the floor, the absence of an answer made it almost uninhabitable—and suitable for an ambush.
Two days before the battle, Taren walked the route with Raal. The wind from the Night slipped beneath their improvised armour and froze the moisture in their fur. Every time they emerged from the shelter of a rock, Taren's body calculated the distance to the next tunnel mouth.
“You're looking for places to run,” Raal observed.
“Everyone does.”
“Not everyone admits it.”
On the lake, the ice was translucent. Beneath it, large bubbles of steam could be seen, trapped like organs in a frozen body. Oru had marked unstable points with technical symbols. Taren recognised his handwriting: quick lines, abbreviations no one else fully understood, little observations about pressure. At one junction he had written: *do not open both valves simultaneously; the basin breathes badly*.
“That's exactly where you want to bring them,” Raal said.
“Yes.”
“Would your engineer have approved?”
“No.”
“Then why are we using his plan?”
Taren put the slate in his pocket.
“Because the dead can't defend their purposes.”
Raal looked at him, probably remembering Daran's words about Leth.
“You learn fast.”
Taren did not reply.
In camp, the First Face numbered twelve hundred members. The number sounded like an army, but the bodies behind it were growers, guards, thieves, adolescents and a few former House agents. Only a hundred had seen fighting at the depot or the station. The rest had practised with suspended trunks and unloaded weapons.
Taren divided the groups according to who knew whom, not rank. Those who had grown up together could recognise one another's panic before it became flight. Raal asked for the members from the Night to stay separate.
“They don't trust the House people,” he said.
“That's precisely why we mix them.”
“You don't build a society in battle.”
“Then what do you build?”
“A situation in which the society that follows will belong to those who remain.”
Taren put Raal at the tunnels, where his decision could save or bury the entire flank.
On the night before the attack, no one slept. Some sharpened blades that were already sharp. Others sent messages to their families. One young man recited the surrender instructions like a prayer. Taren walked among them and found nothing useful to say. Courage, sacrifice, defence—all the words seemed borrowed from stories written by survivors.
Vessa called him to the lakeside.
The central army was coming from the Night, where there was no road.
Seln Aru had chosen the route because Vaor controlled the trains and the eastern galleries could be collapsed. Four thousand agents, technicians and House Selc volunteers advanced along the Belt's frozen edge, protected by thermal suits and low vehicles. Officially, their mission was to restore planetary infrastructure. Their inventory included dispersal cannons, immobilising foam and enough veyrin for three more stations.
They had no combat experience.
Neither did the First Face, but it had survived a trial.
Taren chose to stop them at Deaf Lake, a depression covered in porous ice, twenty kilometres west of the inhabited chambers. There, the wind from the Night erased smells, and the snow hid the mouths of the old geothermal tunnels. Oru had mapped the area before he died. His working slate still marked the points where underground heat had thinned the ice.
Taren held the slate in his palm when Vessa came beside him.
She wore armour made of hardened root plates, too large for her body. In recent days she had eaten little and vomited twice during training. Taren had not asked. Before a battle, bodies had a right to their own secrets.
“I'm pregnant,” she said.
The wind folded both her sails eastwards.
Taren looked at the lake.
“How long have you known?”
“Since before the mist.”
“Why did you come here?”
“Because I'm a doctor.”
“There are other doctors.”
“And because I want to see what kind of father I'm choosing before I let him become a story.”
Taren turned his whole body towards her.
“I don't need this now.”
“Now is exactly when you need it. If you die, the child will be raised by those who use your death. If you live, it will be raised by what you do today.”
“You could lose it in the cold.”
“You could lose twelve hundred people. Don't turn care into a privilege just because it's yours.”
She took the slate from his hand and pointed to two places.
“These tunnels connect. If we open the valves, the steam will weaken the ice beneath the central flank.”
“Oru marked the area as unstable.”
“That's why it will work.”
Taren wanted to tell her he had chosen none of this. That the saryx had fallen in front of him, that Daran had put weapons within reach, that Seln had sent gas, that every step had been a response to something already done. But the child in Vessa's picture turned defence into an excuse. A future needed more than an explanation of causes; it needed someone who accepted having taken part.
“If I live,” he said, “I don't want Daran to name the child.”
“Then live long enough to argue with him.”
“You stay at the medical post.”
“I'll stay where I can save the most people.”
“That's an order.”
Vessa came closer. She did not raise her voice.
“You said protection isn't ownership. Don't make me the first exception.”
Taren lowered his gaze. Power was simple when directed at an enemy. Beside those he loved, every boundary resembled possession.
“Then promise me you'll leave if the line falls.”
“I promise I'll decide when the line has fallen.”
It was all he would get.
Three kilometres to the west, Aven Sor received the same hours under a different name. For the central army, the night before the battle was “the final phase of service deployment”. Seln's orders avoided every military word. The agents were to restore access, isolate armed elements and protect the population from escalation.
On the maps, Vaor appeared as an infrastructure problem surrounded by civilians.
Aven had set up his post in a converted distribution vehicle. Around him, officers rehearsed dispersal routes and technicians checked the foam cannons. Most agents carried weapons that immobilised. The thermal blades were sealed for extreme situations. The word *extreme* had no shared criterion.
Iren Sol, the mirror technician, arrived with the geothermal map.
“The lake route isn't safe,” he said.
“The models show it as stable under our weight.”
“The central models don't include local pipes. There are temperature anomalies beneath the ice.”
“How large?”
“We don't know. Vaor switched off the sensors.”
Aven enlarged the image. The alternative route passed through a gorge where the tunnels could be collapsed. The lake was open, visible and, according to every simulation, allowed deployment without close contact.
“The Custodian approved the route,” he said.
“The Custodian approved the model.”
“It's the same decision.”
Iren looked at him face-on.
“Only for the person who won't be walking on the ice.”
Aven did not punish him. He asked him to mark every suspicious point and ride in the second transporter, not the front line. Iren refused.
“If the relays break, I need to be beside command.”
“You have two children.”
“That's precisely why I want this mission finished before it's called a war.”
Outside the vehicle, the agents ate in groups of twenty. They had been trained for crowds, fires and evacuations. They had learned that a frightened group seeks openings and that slow pressure can push it without visible violence. Few had killed an animal larger than farming insects. Yet some sharpened their sealed blades with stones brought from home.
Aven walked among them. A House Selc volunteer asked whether the First Face really faced forwards in battle.
“It's a sign,” he replied.
“In the images, they don't seem to run.”
“The images begin after those who ran have left the frame.”
“Then why does the world follow them?”
Aven looked towards the lake's dark line.
“Because the rest of us made retreat into an administration.”
He had not meant to say it. The young man did not understand and returned to his weapon.
An hour before departure, Seln appeared on the channel. His face was evenly lit, without a background.
“Avoid casualties,” he said. “Taren Ilen must be separated from the population. Daran Veyr will negotiate when he can no longer use the unit as proof of sovereignty.”
“If they offer armed resistance?” Aven asked.
“Maintain proportionality.”
“In relation to what?”
The pause was very brief.
“Systemic risk.”
Aven knew the language. It meant local losses could be accepted if they prevented the revolt from spreading. It also meant no one would later be able to point to the exact moment the order had become permission.
“I want authorisation to retreat if the terrain differs from the model,” he said.
“You have it.”
“Without central confirmation?”
“You have it,” Seln repeated.
The connection closed. Aven should have felt free. He felt abandoned inside a decision broad enough for any outcome to be attributed to him.
Iren was recording a message for his children. Aven saw him stop three times before sending it. At last, the technician deleted the solemn beginning and said only that he would bring them a piece of deaf ice, if the containers could preserve it.
“Do you think we'll fight?” Aven asked him.
“I think each side has prepared an explanation for why the other started.”
At the instruments' artificial dawn, the vehicles set off. The public services' white cloths unfurled in the wind. The agents looked at them as a guarantee they were not an army. From a distance, to those in Vaor, the white resembled the mist that had killed their people.
•   •   •
The central army appeared on the horizon as a moving stain. It carried no war flags; such objects did not exist. Public-service white cloths fluttered on the vehicles, signalling that any resistance would be classified as an attack on civilian aid.
Daran Veyr had asked Taren for a visible victory.
“We mustn't just stop them,” he had said. “The other Houses must believe Ivarra can be defeated.”
“Then send yourself to the front,” Taren had replied.
Daran had stayed in Vaor, for continuity of leadership.
The First Face split into three. Raal led the group hidden in the lake tunnels. Vessa remained at the medical post behind them. Taren took four hundred members onto the open surface, where any healthy nivar felt the need to run even before the enemy appeared.
He asked them to run.
When the first dispersal projectiles struck the snow, Vaor's line broke. The fighters leapt eastwards in irregular arcs, exactly like a crowd without command. The central agents advanced. Their cannons projected foam that froze on contact and immobilised limbs. Several First Face members were caught; one fell face-down in the snow and suffocated before he could be turned over.
The central commander, Aven Sor, saw the retreat and sent his right flank forwards. His training had been in disturbance control: constant pressure dispersed groups, and fugitives were not to be pursued beyond the inhabited area. But Vaor was retreating towards its own chambers. If he let them go, the mission failed.
Aven followed them.
Taren ran ahead of the line, every muscle begging him not to turn back. The plan had been simple in the hall. On the surface, under projectiles and wind, feigned flight quickly mingled with the real thing. Some passed the marked point. Others threw down their weapons to leap faster.
Taren struck his heel against the ice once.
The sound was lost in the wind, but the resonator at his foot amplified it through the layer below.
In the tunnel, Raal felt it. He opened the first valve.
Steam rose into the pockets beneath the lake.
The second beat.
The groups on the sides stopped running and descended into the concealed tunnel mouths. Those who did not remember the plan followed them by reflex.
The central army reached the thinned ice.
The third beat.
Taren turned.
Four hundred bodies did the same. It was not a natural manoeuvre. To present their faces, nivari had to fight the position of their eyes, the orientation of their limbs, every gene telling them their backs should already be far away. The grey line turned towards the white.
The others burst from the tunnels.
Raal opened the second valve. The ice gave way beneath the central vehicles. It did not break into water; underneath was hot geothermal mud. Two transporters tilted and sank halfway. Steam covered the flank. The agents, trained to hold formation before a crowd, found themselves attacked from the sides and below.
When the ice gave way, it did not sound like a fracture. It was felt through the soles: a sudden lowering of the world. White vehicles tilted, agents clutched one another, and steam erupted in columns that hid the star Vei.
Taren turned with the line. For the first time, the manoeuvre rehearsed in the courtyard carried the weight of real bodies. Some did not stop. They kept running towards Vaor. Taren saw them disappear into the white and felt anger, then envy. They had accepted what their muscles demanded. He had used the very instinct to flee as bait, and now had to persuade a thousand bodies to betray it.
The first agent he struck was an older woman. She held a dispersal tool and shouted that she was a technician. Taren's blade stopped before her throat; he hit her with the hilt and took her weapon. To his right, another Face member did not stop. Black blood spread across the ice.
The code existed, but the fighting made it hard to read.
Steam erased their field of vision in less than ten seconds. Taren could see only bodies entering and leaving the white. The signs on cloaks disappeared beneath moisture. For a species accustomed to tracking the world's edges with lateral eyes, the battle suddenly became a tunnel without walls.
To his right, Emon Veyr fell onto a central agent. Both had lost their weapons. The agent was smaller and was trying to free his foot from frozen foam. Emon raised his short blade.
“Surrender!” he shouted.
The agent could not hear him. His helmet covered his sails. He struggled and searched for something at his belt.
Emon struck the helmet, not the throat. He broke the visor and repeated the order. The agent took his hand from his belt. He held a medical kit.
“Technician,” he said.
The same word the woman Taren encountered a few moments later would shout. Emon pulled him out of the foam and pushed him towards the prisoner line. A Face member reproached him for wasting time.
“At the gallery, you ran,” he said. “Now you're hesitating.”
“Now I know what I'm protecting,” Emon replied.
In the tunnel, the second valve did not open fully. Raal felt the resistance through the handle. Mineral deposits had blocked the shaft, exactly as Oru's slate warned. Two people helped him. If they forced it too far, the pipe could rupture the gallery and boil them before the lake gave way.
“Open it!” a young man shouted.
“Slowly.”
“Taren gave the third signal.”
“Taren is above and can't feel the valve.”
The young man pushed. The shaft moved abruptly, and the sideways jet stripped the skin from his arm. Raal knocked him to the floor and closed it a fraction. Above, the ice groaned. Around him, the fighters waited for the order with their eyes raised, as though authority could descend through rock.
“Now,” Raal said.
They opened it together. The ceiling vibrated. A piece of root broke free and struck a woman on the head. She did not get up. Raal had no time to learn her name. He ordered the others out through the northern mouth and stayed last to lock the valve in position. Years later, songs would say he had stood alone beneath the lake. In reality, two people came back for him, and one cursed him all the way for wanting to turn a technical task into a memorable death.
At the medical post, Vessa lost track of the order of the wounded after the first minute. The central foam caused hypothermia, the mud burns, and blades mixed tissue with armour fibres. She tied three bands to her arm: one for those who could wait, one for those who needed immediate treatment and one for those she could no longer save. After five people, the colours were covered in blood and ceased to be useful.
An agent in white was brought beside a Face member. Both had burns over half their bodies. The assistant asked whom to treat first.
“The one breathing worse.”
“He's central.”
“His lungs don't know that.”
The Vaor fighter heard her and caught her wrist.
“I came with you.”
“Then help me and breathe for one more minute.”
She fitted the agent with the breathing tube. The fighter survived. The agent did not. Later, the first man's family would say Vessa had chosen the enemy. In the medical report, the choice appeared as a twenty-three-second difference in saturation.
On the surface, signals were being lost. Taren struck once for the right flank and received two replies from different directions. One Face group had descended into the wrong opening and emerged behind its own people. For a few moments, two grey formations attacked each other in the steam. They recognised their shared smell only after the first death.
Taren shouted for them to stop, but the wind took his voice. He made his way to a messenger's fallen resonator. A foam projectile struck his thigh and froze his knee. He cut the material away before it hardened, losing a strip of skin with it. Around him, the battle had no centre. His plan continued through hundreds of people remembering different fragments.
He saw an agent raise a blade over a fallen recruit. He struck him from the side. Then he saw a Face member attack the same agent after his weapon had been taken. He stopped him too. In less than a minute, both sides had considered him a traitor.
“The signal!” he shouted to those around him. “Three short beats means stop!”
“They haven't surrendered!” someone replied.
“You aren't listening any more either!”
When he found Aven beside the transporter, Taren had not reached the enemy commander as in a duel. He had reached him because every other part of the field had become impossible to control. Capturing him was the only command simple enough to cross the steam.
Taren reached Aven Sor beside the overturned transporter. The central commander held a foam projector and was trying to organise the retreat.
“Stop the attack!” he shouted. “The mission is cancelled!”
Taren knocked the weapon from his hand.
“Hand over the vehicle codes.”
“You attacked the planetary services.”
“You entered with gas a domain that had just buried its children.”
“I didn't order the mist.”
“And I didn't order every blade surrounding you.”
Aven looked around. First Face members were approaching. Some wore the frontal sign on their masks. In their eyes was the same pleasure Sava had seen when Taren killed the saryx, now multiplied by belonging.
Aven lowered his hands.
“I surrender.”
Taren struck the ground three times, quickly. The new signal to stop fighting.
On the left, a group kept pursuing the fleeing agents. Raal ran after them, caught up, and knocked down the first Vaor fighter before he could drive his blade into a white back.
“The code,” Raal said.
“They killed us at the station.”
“The code.”
The young man spat at him. Raal broke his arm.
Raal broke the fighter's arm without looking towards Taren. The gesture was effective and disproportionate. The young man was no longer pursuing anyone. The pain made him fall, and two comrades immediately understood the order.
“You could have stopped him another way,” Taren said.
“Yes.”
“Why didn't you?”
Raal watched the fleeing central agents.
“So the others would understand faster.”
Taren should have punished him. Instead, he ordered him to take the prisoners to the rear. He chose efficiency in a situation where every second mattered. Later, when situations would be constructed precisely to make seconds matter, he would remember that compromise.
The battle lasted twenty-seven minutes.
Three hundred and nineteen central agents and a hundred and four First Face members died. Another two hundred fell into the hot mud; Vessa treated burns until her hands shook. She did not ask which side the wounded belonged to. Taren sent the prisoners to carry the bodies.
Sava arrived from Vaor after the fighting stopped. She had passed through days of mourning without crying in front of anyone. When she saw the field, she stopped.
Arua's snow was not naturally white. Its nitrogen crystals reflected the star's red light and had the colour of pale flesh. Nivar blood was almost black. From a distance, the dead looked like holes opened in the ground.
“Did you win?” she asked Taren.
He stood beside Aven Sor, who had surrendered his codes.
“I stopped them.”
“That isn't what I asked.”
Taren looked at the disarmed central army, the captured vehicles and the First Face members striking the ground in a victory rhythm. The vibration passed through the lake and raised small waves of snow.
“Yes,” he said.
Sava approached a covered body. She recognised a mirror technician's uniform, not an agent's. He was very young.
“Did he know how to fight?”
“No.”
“Then whom did you defeat?”
Sava knelt beside the dead technician. His badge read *secondary mirrors, eastern sector*. He carried no weapon. He had probably been sent to restart the nodes once the agents took the ground.
“His name was Iren Sol,” Aven Sor said. “He had two children.”
The central commander was disarmed, with a cut on his forehead. Sava knew him from reports, not personally.
“Why was he in the front line?”
“He wasn't. The line broke over us.”
“Who decided on the route across the lake?”
“The Custodian.”
“Did he know it was unstable?”
Aven looked at Oru's slate in Taren's hand.
“The central maps didn't show active valves.”
“Because Vaor hid the network,” Sava said.
“Because Ivarra would have shut it down,” Taren replied.
Truth passed from one to another like a weapon no one wanted to hold for long.
•   •   •
The messages arrived before the dead were lifted.
House Orm in the south requested twelve First Face instructors. House Tel was suspending its year of residence in Ivarra and requesting weapons. Three communities without a House declared they would no longer transmit birth registers. In the capital, the provincial families in the Gardens of Kinship had been moved into guarded compounds for their own safety.
Daran broadcast images of the sunken central vehicles and announced “the victory of a local administration against a disproportionate intervention”. He did not utter the word war.
In the medical camp, Vessa showed Taren a trembling image of their embryo. It was only a pale curve and a rhythmic point.
“It survived,” she said.
The victory beats came from outside. Three, pause, three, pause. The old signal for shadow, shelter, silence had been turned inside out. Now it meant: stop, face, attack.
Taren touched the screen.
“What will we tell the child happened here?”
“The truth,” Vessa replied.
“Which one?”
Vessa looked at the dead through the tent entrance.
“The one they'll have to move beyond.”
At artificial sunset, Taren sent the same reply to the three Houses:
I WILL NOT SEND YOU WARRIORS. SEND ME THE YOUNG PEOPLE WHO NO LONGER HAVE A PLACE IN YOUR PEACE.
Before sending his reply to the Houses, Taren walked among the wounded. A central agent asked him for water. A First Face member refused, saying their own reserves were low. Taren took his container and gave it to the prisoner. The gesture stirred a murmur around them.
“They brought us the mist,” someone said.
“This one wasn't at the station.”
“He wore their white.”
“So when he wears our cloak, will he be allowed to be thirsty?”
No one answered. Taren moved away, aware that a just sentence did not change the distribution of resentment.
At the medical post, Vessa showed him the embryo. The pale curve pulsed. Taren did not feel the simple joy he had expected; he felt a fear more precise than all the others. The child would be born into a world where its father's victory had just set twelve trains in motion.
“Don't send instructors,” Vessa said. “Daran wants branches of his own.”
“If we don't send them, they'll make their own.”
“Then bring them here. Make them see the dead as well as the images of victory.”
Taren wrote the message.
I WILL NOT SEND YOU WARRIORS. SEND ME THE YOUNG PEOPLE WHO NO LONGER HAVE A PLACE IN YOUR PEACE.
After it was sent, the pounding spread across the lake. Three, pause, three. Fine snow leapt above the ice like shivering skin.
Taren listened to the new signal and understood it could no longer be withdrawn. A code becomes tradition not when it is declared, but when people use it to recognise one another.
Within a week, twelve trains set off for Vaor.
PART III
THE VERTICAL HOUSES
•   •   •
CHAPTER XIII
AFTER VICTORY
SAVA AND NEMI · VAOR · THE NEXT TWO YEARS
The victory at Deaf Lake did not produce peace, but a period in which every institution claimed the emergency would end after the next temporary measure.
Two years after Deaf Lake, the first towers appeared on Arua's surface.
They had no clear function. Weapons could be mounted more safely underground, and solar storms made height a vulnerability. The builders claimed the towers allowed surveillance of the roads. Sava knew drones could observe the roads better.
Their real usefulness was to be seen.
For thousands of generations, nivari had hidden their homes from the sky. Even Ivarra's palaces lay beneath rock, their entrances imitating folds in the terrain. Daran Veyr raised the first tower above Vaor, a brown blade of fossilised root visible to the edge of the Night. He placed the First Face's frontal sign at its summit.
“It's an insult to instinct,” he said at the inauguration. “That's why it's art.”
The other Houses imitated him. Towers became depots, command posts, shrines to the dead and places where young people climbed to prove they could sleep beneath a sky without a ceiling. A new political expression formed around them: the Vertical Houses.
Ivarra called them emergency regimes.
The emergency had already lasted twenty-seven months.
In the registers, the twenty-seven months between the lake and the tower occupied only a few lines: stabilisation, population integration, reconstruction, defensive reorganisation. For those who had lived them, nothing had been stable.
In the first week after victory, Vaor did not know what to do first with survival. The clinics were full, the main pipes worked only a few hours each cycle, and central prisoners received the same food as the fighters who had captured them. That had been Taren's rule. On the third day, a mother came to the Civil Office and asked why the agent who had burned her home received medicine while her son waited.
Sava had no answer that did not resemble cruelty.
“Because he's wounded now,” she said.
“And when was my son?”
“Then he should have received it too.”
“He didn't.”
“I know.”
“Then your equality begins exactly after it would have helped us.”
The woman left before Sava finished explaining the law, prisoners and what kind of people they would become if treatment depended on hatred. In the report, the case appeared as a “dispute over clinical priority”. Sava erased the phrase and entered the woman's question exactly. Daran approved the change, then projected it in a speech about Vaor's nobility. The woman asked him to remove her name. Daran removed the name and kept the pain.
In the second week, demobilisation began. Taren sent home half of those who had fought at the lake. Two days later, three communities asked for their guards back: depots had been attacked, former central agents wandered without food, and groups wearing the Face sign were confiscating things in victory's name. Taren sent temporary patrols and forbade them to stay more than one cycle in the same settlement.
The patrols returned after a cycle, then again. Sleeping places were built for them. A technician was assigned to repair their weapons. A kitchen was allocated exclusively to guard shifts because they could not wait in civilian queues. Every measure was temporary. Together, they formed an institution.
Raal handled recruitment and brought Taren candidates no school would have accepted: over-impulsive youths, women who had defended their alcoves with tools, former thieves who knew the Night roads, central agents who could no longer return to Ivarra. Taren rejected those who demanded rank before training and those who spoke too often of blood.
“You'll keep only the ones who lie better,” Raal told him.
“Then stay and show me which ones they are.”
“I'm an excellent liar.”
“That's why I keep you close.”
In the second month, Daran proposed a Festival of Staying. He wanted enormous cloths, fires on the surface and the dead people's names projected onto rock. Sava showed him the calculation: the ceremony's materials could repair two reservoirs and feed the clinics for nine days.
“People don't live on nutrient alone,” Daran said.
“They die very concretely without it.”
“They need to know what the others died for.”
“The dead no longer need your explanation.”
The ceremony took place anyway, but without fires. Workers hung a piece of burnt pipe at every entrance. Each family wrote its own name, without Daran being able to choose the order. Some pieces carried thanks to the First Face. Others accused Daran, Ivarra, the Chamber or even Taren. One carried only the words: *we don't yet know why*.
For Oru, Nemi chose a green piece cut from the pipe he had opened before dying. She wanted to write “he saved the children”. Sava stopped her.
“He did more than his last act.”
“Then what do I write?”
They stayed a long time beside the crooked root planted at the ceremony. The graft looked sick, but Bes had found a living strand and connected it to a water circuit. Around it, the soil smelled of wet minerals and ash.
Nemi wrote: *Oru Ilen. He repaired what he could touch.*
“It doesn't say he was my father.”
“Those who know you know.”
“But a long time from now?”
Sava thought of all the people reduced in archives to a function or a relationship with someone more important.
“Add that too.”
Beneath the first line, Nemi wrote: *He was my father, and not only that.*
In the fourth month, the children began playing Deaf Lake. Some were agents running across the ice, others Faces striking imaginary valves. No one wanted to be the civilian caught between them. When an instructor tried to introduce the role, the children turned it into the person who brought water after the fighting.
Taren watched once. He saw a boy imitate the way he had raised Oru's rod. The movement was wrong and heroic. He showed him the correct angle, then regretted it. The next day, all the children repeated the gesture better.
“You gave them the lesson they wanted to hear,” Vessa said.
“He could have broken his shoulder.”
“Now he'll break it precisely.”
Vessa worked in the united clinics of the upper and hidden cities. Some doctors refused patients without identities because medicines had to be accounted for. She introduced a provisional register based on biological traces rather than names. Sava approved it as a six-month measure. After six months, she extended it.
