Every title became a position at the depot.
•   •   •
Sen was seven and could already evade Raal three blows out of ten.
The boy had grown tall, with unusually broad shoulders and shorter-than-average auditory sails. Vessa refused to say whether the difference was hereditary or the result of fighters trimming children's fragile tips before training. Sen did not startle at alarms. He startled when Taren spoke too calmly.
One morning, Sava found him in the food depot dividing rations with First Face members.
“Why aren't you studying?” she asked.
“Raal says numbers are learned better when someone dies if you get them wrong.”
“Raal says many things that sound profound only because he's lost half his teeth.”
Sen smiled. His front chisel teeth were thick and regular, selected not in a laboratory but through the long series of choices that had brought his parents together.
“Nemi says I have to record the difference between stock and distribution.”
Nemi was fourteen standard years old and already led the Memory Corps. She wore three data slates at her belt and had been to the front line twice, despite every promise made to Sava.
She entered the depot with snow on her cloak.
“The Orm domain has closed its border,” she said. “It's no longer taking Protected from the west.”
Sava felt her old revulsion at the word.
“They're refugees, not Protected.”
“In the ration tables, they're Protected.”
“The tables mustn't enter the language.”
“Language enters the tables anyway.”
Sen resembled Taren only to those looking from a distance. Close up, he spoke faster, was more easily provoked and far more attentive to approval. Taren had endured years of isolation before his defect became a virtue; Sen had grown up surrounded by adults searching for signs of that virtue. Every absence of fear was praised. Every cruelty could be interpreted as firmness until Vessa intervened.
In the depot, the boy calculated portions precisely. He knew how many calories a fighter lost on patrol, how much energy lactation required and why small children could not receive the same concentrated food as adults. When he saw the refugee column on the map, he did not ask who they were. He asked what they consumed.
Sava recognised in him not only the Face's selection, but her own administrative education.
The first refugees arrived that evening. They came from a community called Hes, two hundred kilometres west. Their bodies were wrapped in insulating cloth, and the edges of their sails bore white frostbite lesions. A woman dragged a sled carrying two children and an old man. At Vaor's entrance, the official asked her classification.
“Grower,” the woman replied.
“Does the farm exist?”
“No.”
“Then the skill must be reassessed. Temporarily, Protected.”
The woman looked at the children.
“I fed six hundred people before the light left.”
“We aren't disputing your skill. The field requires your current function.”
Sava took the terminal from the official's hand and wrote: HANDS — AGRICULTURAL RESTORATION.
“We have no restoration programme,” he whispered.
“We do now.”
“On what land?”
“We'll find it.”
For three days, Sava created functions for people the system considered a burden. Restorers, carers, ice cartographers, seed recoverers. Some jobs were real. Others were names given to survival so it could receive a ration without humiliation. Daran would have admired the manoeuvre. The thought irritated her.
Nemi organised the refugees' testimonies. Every community described the same change in light, but at slightly different times. When she overlaid the data, the resulting edge was too precise for a malfunction. The mirrors had been oriented to keep loyal cities inside the band and let those associated with the Confederation slide towards the cold.
“Seln isn't striking us,” she said. “He's cutting the world around us.”
“He has a technical argument too,” Sava replied. “Our transport is no longer safe, and he's concentrating production where he can distribute it.”
“Are you defending him?”
“I'm trying to understand how he justifies each step. Otherwise we'll describe him as evil and be surprised by the reasonable people who follow him.”
Nemi closed the map.
“Sometimes understanding is a form of delay.”
“And haste is how you end up calling every body in front of you an enemy.”
Her daughter turned her profile, signalling the discussion was over. Sava noticed how much body language had changed. Nemi's generation used the face for confrontation and the profile for deliberate withdrawal. Gestures thousands of years old had acquired political meanings in less than a decade.
“We don't have food for them,” Sava said.
“We do if we reduce the Faces' portions.”
Sen stopped writing.
“The patrols are freezing outside,” he said.
“I know.”
“If they eat less, they'll die.”
“If the refugees don't eat, they'll die.”
The boy looked at the shelves, as though there were an arithmetic hidden in the wood.
“Who decides?”
“Your father,” Nemi said.
Taren decided to reduce everyone's portions by eight per cent and open the military reserves. The commanders protested. Three threatened to withdraw their Corps to their own domains. Taren let them leave and confiscated their transports before they got out of Vaor.
“You violated the Charter,” Sava told him.
“The Charter didn't anticipate winter.”
“No law anticipates the next excuse.”
“Would Oru have let the children die to preserve an article?”
Sava struck him with her gaze.
“Don't use him.”
Taren lowered his head.
“You're right.”
Taren opened the military reserves that day, but the gesture solved only a week. The Face commanders did not object solely from selfishness. Patrols spent hours in the cold, escorted convoys and defended pipes attacked by Houses closing their borders. With reduced portions, reactions slowed and wounds healed poorly.
Sava proposed rotation: half the patrols would return to civilian work, and fit civilians would do short service. The Faces protested that their training could not be replaced.
“Neither can food,” she said.
Raal supported the proposal. He had understood before the others that a caste becomes permanent when it alone possesses the skill to exercise violence. In the Night camps, every adult had guarded, stolen and repaired as needed.
“Teach the Hands to hold a weapon,” he told the officers. “Otherwise one day they'll hold one only against you.”
Taren approved the programme. In practice, commanders sent their weakest people on rotation and kept the good fighters. The Hands received minimal training and were stationed at secondary gates. The institution absorbed the reform without losing its shape.
Sava began to understand there was no single day on which a revolution became a regime. There were hundreds of small adjustments through which every new rule became useful to someone.
•   •   •
The edge hunters Oru had released six years earlier had survived. Sterilisation worked, but the individuals lived longer than the models predicted and had learned to follow warm pipes. They kept the glassback herds under control and had attacked only two nivari, both after attempts to catch them for training. In Vaor, they had become sacred animals to engineers and ritual targets for young Faces.
Taren had forbidden hunting them. Sen had broken the ban.
Vessa brought him into the council hall, holding him by the nape. Claw marks showed on his arm, and she threw a necklace made of a hunter's teeth onto the floor.
“He killed it with four other children,” she said.
Taren looked at the necklace.
“Why?”
Sen kept his body face-on, according to the Law.
“To see who stays.”
“Stays where?”
“Beside it when it attacks.”
“Who told you to do that?”
“No one. Everyone does it.”
Vessa projected data from the nine Houses' schools. Chase games, once based on flight and hiding, had turned into confrontations with animals, falls from heights and group fights. Faces' children had three times as many injuries and twice the mortality. Those who survived received rank early, partners early and more birth rights.
“We no longer select only in clinics,” she said. “We select in courtyards, songs and mating rooms. Those who avoid the games are called soft. The soft get Hands or Memory. The Faces breed among themselves.”
“You warned about this,” Taren said.
“I thought the warning absolved me while I gave birth to your son.”
Sen shifted his gaze between them.
“Did I do something bad?”
Taren picked up the necklace.
“You killed something useful to prove you could kill.”
“You killed the saryx.”
“The saryx was attacking a child.”
“The hunter attacked me.”
“After you cornered it.”
Sen thought. He did not look ashamed, only attentive.
“Then next time I'll let it choose the place.”
Vessa closed her eyes.
Taren broke the necklace and placed the teeth on the table, one by one.
“There won't be a next time.”
“For me or for the hunter?”
The hunter Sen killed was named Arh, after the marking on its transmitter. Oru had released it as a cub. For six years, the animal had followed herds, brought nutrients into the soil and learned to avoid settlements. The engineers knew its routes. Some left food scraps at the edges of fields, although Oru had forbidden accustoming it to the smell of nivari.
When Vessa put the necklace on the table, two engineers were also in the room. One burst into tears.
“Arh held sector four,” he said. “Without him, the herd will enter the winter crops.”
Sen looked at him, puzzled by such intense grief for an animal.
“We can take another out of the reservoir.”
“It isn't a part,” the engineer replied.
“My father says the function matters more than the story.”
Taren closed his eyes for a moment. He did not remember saying exactly that. But he recognised the logic.
“Your father is sometimes wrong,” he said.
“When?”
“Now, if he made you think a living thing can be replaced simply because another can take over its function.”
Sen looked at the necklace. Shame finally appeared, but not because of the death. Because his father was judging him in front of others.
Vessa noticed and sent the child out. Once he was beyond the door, she turned to Taren.
“You talk to him as if he were a commander.”
“He came to the council hall with a useful animal's teeth.”
“He's seven.”
“At seven, I was already entering alarm therapy.”
“That's exactly why you should know what a diagnosis made too early does.”
“I didn't diagnose him.”
“You're correcting him before the institution waiting to inherit him.”
Taren looked towards the door.
“What do you want me to do?”
“Be a father before you're a precedent.”
That evening, Taren took Sen to the field where Arh had died. They wore no armour. Traces of the fight remained in the snow: black stains, down, the children's footprints. Taren showed him the pipe the hunter had protected through its presence.
“You'll work here until the herd stabilises,” he said.
“As punishment?”
“So you can see what you took out of the world.”
“For how long?”
“Until you can explain without talking about yourself.”
Sen pulled his cloak tight.
“Raal says those who die get their reasons from the living.”
“Raal is right.”
“Then I can give him a good one.”
“No. You can repair part of the consequence. Arh's reason was to live.”
The child stayed silent. In the distance, glassbacks moved towards the undefended crop.
Winter deepened. House Orm attacked a refugee convoy for its thermal batteries. The First Face intervened and occupied two of its cities. House Tel split into three factions. In the south, communities that had never seen a central agent raised towers and appointed their own commanders in Taren's image.
Every victory against Ivarra produced two local wars.
“Seln doesn't have to defeat us,” Nemi said at a meeting. “He only has to keep us cold enough to eat one another.”
She projected the orbital swarm map. Primary control lay beneath Ivarra, but four secondary stations could take over the mirrors if they received the Great Hearing's consensus keys. One had been built directly beneath the Gardens of Kinship.
“The Houses' families are still there,” Sava said. “Guarded.”
“And connected to the old network. Every House had emergency access before Seln suspended it.”
“You propose using hostages as an entry point.”
“I propose getting them out and going in at the same time.”
Taren studied the map.
“Ivarra has two hundred thousand inhabitants in its central levels. A siege will kill them before we reach control.”
“Then we don't besiege it yet,” Nemi said. “We send someone Seln will receive.”
Every gaze turned to Sava.
“No,” Taren said.
Sava almost smiled.
“Power has made you forget how little you can order me to do.”
“Seln will use you.”
“He's done that since my first day working for the Chamber. The difference is that now I know.”
“He could detain you.”
“Then Nemi will have access to someone inside.”
Taren struck the table with his palm.
“I'm not sending you both.”
Nemi turned her body face-on.
“You aren't sending us. You're opening a road for us.”
Sava saw in her gesture not only Taren's influence but a new cultural anatomy. Her daughter no longer offered her face as threat or love. She offered it as a right to participate in danger.
After the meeting, Sava found Taren alone. Ivarra's map remained projected above the table. The entrances, the mirror stations, the Gardens of Kinship. It was the map of the city where they had grown up and, now, of a target.
“Do you really want to go?” he asked.
“No.”
“Then don't.”
“That's the difference between desire and decision. You should know it.”
Taren ran his fingers over the scar on his shoulder.
“Seln respects you enough to use you well.”
“And I know him well enough to notice.”
“If he detains you, I'm coming for you.”
“If you come only for me, you'll turn the negotiation into a real hostage situation. I'm going for light, for the families and for access. Nemi is going for the archive.”
“Nemi is fourteen.”
“You were eighteen when he sent you to Vaor.”
“Exactly.”
Sava looked at him. Despite the title, scars and army, he still kept the expression of the boy who asked why a simple instruction had to be followed.
“I can't keep her a child by refusing every risk,” she said. “The Chamber tried that with you and produced the exact opposite.”
“Perhaps it produced something we still don't understand.”
“All parents discover that too late.”
The next day, she sent the offer. Seln answered immediately.
CHAPTER XVIII
THE CITY THAT REPAIRS ITS TRACES
SAVA · IVARRA · DURING THE WHITE WINTER
Ivarra did not look like a city that had won a war. It looked like a city where war had been removed from the field of vision and turned into graphs, discreet checks and sufficient light.
The next day, Sava sent Ivarra an offer to negotiate.
Seln answered personally after a minute.
“The Chamber of Measure always welcomes its former officials,” he said. “Even when they come with an army behind them.”
“I'm coming with a list of the dead.”
“You all come with lists. The difference between administration and revenge is what we do with them.”
“I want the light restored, the families released and local registers recognised.”
“I want the Corps disarmed, Taren surrendered and a return to the rotation of Houses.”
“Then we have enough impossible things for a serious conversation.”
Seln folded one sail.
“Bring Nemi. Our archives on Lur are incomplete.”
Sava did not ask how he knew her daughter intended to come.
Before departure, Taren gave her the repaired carbon rod, the weapon with which he had killed the first saryx.
“I can't take it through the capital's checks,” Sava said.
“It isn't for fighting. Daran's museum key is inside. Nemi can use it on the old network.”
Sava took the rod.
“Daran started everything with that key.”
“Then perhaps it can open something else too.”
The train to Ivarra departed through snow. From the platform, Sen watched them with Vessa. When the carriages disappeared, the child struck the floor three times.
It was neither alarm, attack nor retreat.
It was the new generation's way of saying come back.
On the train, Nemi did not sleep. She read the Gardens' old archives and Oru's infrastructure plans. Her father's map was incomplete, annotated in engineers' language: *the root breathes badly here*; *do not close both valves*; *useless sensor, but they'll keep it because it's in the budget*. Amid the technical jokes was a network of abandoned routes running beneath the capital.
Sava sat by the window as the train climbed to the surface. Winter could be seen behind them, a white band wider than when they had left. To the east, the central crops were still green. The line between famine and normality was a few dozen kilometres and an orbital decision.
“Do you hate Seln?” Nemi asked.
“Sometimes.”
“You don't look it.”
“Hatred is easy when the person in front of you can't answer coherently. Seln can.”
“Does that make him less guilty?”
“It makes him more dangerous. And harder to replace with something better.”
Nemi closed the map.
“Taren can replace him.”
Sava looked at her daughter.
“Taren can win control. That's not the same thing.”
“Then who?”
“Perhaps no one alone.”
“In a crisis, groups are slow.”
“That's the sentence every personal power begins with.”
Nemi turned her profile towards the window. Sava recognised Taren's voice in some answers and her own in others. Children inherited more than genes. They inherited arguments and used them without remembering the source.
•   •   •
Ivarra was still beautiful.
That was the first betrayal Sava felt when she left the train. She had expected a starving, armed city emptied of truth. She found clean squares, children going to school, warm pipes and shops displaying sweet roots without guards. The Festival of Retreat's cloths had been replaced. Their shadows crossed the Low Square again every cycle, and tourists stopped beside a discreet plaque dedicated to “the victims of the biological incident of the year 412”.
Taren's name did not appear.
Nor Oru's.
“It looks as if nothing happened,” Nemi said.
“That's one of the things peace does well.”
“Repair?”
“Make repair resemble innocence.”
In the Low Square, the memorial plaque was so discreet Nemi walked past it. Sava stopped. The text listed the number of victims, not their names. “Biological incident” had been chosen after three commissions and two public consultations. The term avoided both “attack”, which implied intention, and “accident”, which the families had rejected.
A few steps away, children rehearsed the Festival. The instructor triggered the mechanical shadows, and the circles retreated in order. After the attack, routes had been widened, marker seven moved and railings replaced. The system had learned exactly the physical lessons. There was no test in the programme for what to do when the signal was wrong.
Nemi sat where the saryx had fallen.
“This is where Taren told me not to run.”
“Yes.”
“I remember the smell. Not Oru's face in the burning room, but the smell of the animal here.”
Sava knelt beside her. They had not spoken so directly about the memory of that day before.
“The brain keeps what it thinks can save it next time.”
“It didn't save me from the mist.”
“There isn't one memory for every danger.”
Nemi touched the floor.
“The city only remembered how to make better railings.”
“Cities remember through what they can change without acknowledging their guilt.”
An instructor asked them to leave the route. He had not recognised them. Sava stood and left without telling him who she was. For a few minutes, Ivarra had offered them the anonymity of a life that could have continued here.
They passed their old home. The door bore another family name. Through the thin wall came the smell of soup, arguing and laughter. Nemi wanted to knock. Sava stopped her.
“The home doesn't owe us remaining empty.”
“I don't want to go in. I want to listen.”
They stayed in the gallery until the family inside turned off the light. For a moment, Sava heard Oru's voice in a man explaining to a child why a pipe creaked. There was no real resemblance. It was the mind's need to repair the traces.
They did not immediately return to the route towards the Chamber. Nemi said she was hungry, and Sava understood she meant more than food. They entered a neighbourhood canteen they had frequented before Vaor. The ceiling had been rebuilt in pale root, the tables were farther apart, and a message on the wall pulsed about saving light in solidarity with the western regions.
The menu had three hot dishes.
In Vaor, the evening ration had become a bitter paste divided according to the Charter of Continuity. Here, people complained that sweet root had been replaced by a more fibrous variety. Nemi looked at the trays without hiding her anger.
“Don't look at them as though every bowl moved the mirror,” Sava said.
“Every bowl shows who was left beneath it.”
“The people here don't control the swarm.”
“They accept it.”
“And Vaor's inhabitants accept many things they can't change.”
Nemi took only soup. At the next table, two officials discussed the “western redistribution crisis” and the risk that refugees would overburden the capital's schools. They did not speak cruelly. One proposed additional housing, the other tax reductions for host families. Their language was caring and entirely detached from the cold.
A man at the counter recognised Sava. His name was Rin Aro, and for years he had sold spices beside the Low Square entrance. Now he ran the canteen. One sail had been cut in half, and a scar lay beneath his left eye.
“The Chamber said you'd died,” he said.
“The Chamber no longer knows much about me.”
Rin looked at Nemi's black cloak.
“You're the girl with the recordings from Lur.”
“I'm Nemi.”
“My son was an agent at Deaf Lake.”
The canteen's ordinary sounds continued: spoons, water, a child scolded for beating the table too hard. Nemi put down her bowl.
“What was his name?”
“Rel Aro. In your archive, he's number two hundred and eleven, central flanking unit.”
Nemi opened the slate. Rel had been twenty. He died when the ice collapsed beneath his formation. The report said only “loss in hostile manoeuvre”.
“I can add his name to the public index,” she said.
“His name was already recorded. I want to know whether he ran.”
The question surprised her.
“Why?”
“Because before leaving he said the people of Vaor were animals who didn't respect retreat. After his death, command sent me a medal for discipline. If he ran, I don't want him buried in a lie that would have shamed him. If he stayed, I want to know what for.”
Nemi searched the lake images. The recordings were fragmentary, many made by Faces. She found the flanking unit in the second the steam erupted. Rel had turned towards the retreat corridor, then seen another fallen agent. He had gone back to lift him. The ice gave way beneath them both.
She showed him without comment.
Rin watched the scene three times. At the second, he lowered his sails. At the third, he stopped before the collapse.
“He didn't run,” Nemi said.
“Yes, he did. Then he came back.”
His voice held no pride, but the painful relief of someone who had received a dead man more complicated than the monument.
“Did Taren open the lake?” he asked.
“Yes.”
“Did he know they would die?”
“He knew it was probable.”
“Then why do you serve him?”
“I don't serve him.”
Rin touched the Memory slate on her cloak.
“You wear his sign.”
“The sign existed before him and will have to exist after him.”
“Everyone with power says the institution is older than the person. Then the institution keeps their face.”
Sava intervened:
“Your son was sent to Vaor by Seln.”
“I know. And yet I can buy soup here without anyone asking me to carry a weapon. I can hate Seln and not want Taren's Corps in my gallery.”
“Vaor is freezing.”
“Then move the light. Don't move the soldiers.”
“The swarm won't open without pressure.”
“That's the argument every army uses to reach my pipe.”
Rin brought them bread without asking for payment. Nemi refused at first. He left it on the table anyway.
“It isn't for Taren. It's because you showed me the return.”
At the far end of the room, a class of children was eating. Their instructor spoke about the western regions. Every child had drawn a white root and a blue circle representing the cold. The drawings would be sent to “relocated families” with clothing parcels. No child had seen mineral snow.
A little girl noticed the scar on Nemi's sail and came to the table.
“Have you been outside?”
“Yes.”
“Does the sky really have no end?”
Nemi remembered her first journey, the blanket over her head and Oru's voice describing the world without forcing her to look.
“You can't see the end,” she said. “But you always feel where you could hide.”
“I'd stay outside.”
“Many people inside think that.”
The instructor called her. The girl left, waving both sails at them.
Rin sat beside them for a moment.
“Do you know why Ivarra seems quiet?” he asked. “Not because no one knows. Because everyone knows a piece small enough to continue their day. I know about Rel. The woman at table three hosts two refugees. The cook lost a brother in the train attack. The officials beside you work on housing. We all do something that fits inside a life. No one can carry the whole map.”
“Seln carries it and decides for you,” Nemi said.
“And Taren carries it and decides for yours.”
“That's why we're here.”
Rin raised one sail.
“Or why you think you are.”
When they left, the canteen was preparing for the next shift. On the wall, the solidarity message became a graph of saved consumption. The capital's percentage was small, almost symbolic. Yet to the people inside, the percentage was proof they participated.
Nemi looked again at the entrance to their former home.
“It's easier when you think they're indifferent,” she said.
“Yes.”
“It doesn't make them innocent.”
“No.”
“Then what does it change?”
Sava drew her cloak closer.
“It changes what kind of world has to be built if we want to persuade them, not only defeat them.”
•   •   •
Seln Aru received them in Sava's old office.
He had changed almost nothing. The Belt map pulsed on the wall, whiter in the west than she remembered. On the table lay the ILEN–VEN file, the second-birth application, still marked POSTPONED.
“Some systems are more persistent than families,” Seln said.
Sava did not sit.
“Oru died.”
“I know.”
“Then close the file.”
Seln closed it with a touch.
“I'm sorry.”
“Your regret arrived in two tonnes of aerosol.”
“The order was for low-concentration dispersal. The tanks were damaged in the fighting.”
“The fighting began because you brought them.”
“If I'd sent lethal weapons, you'd have accused me of choosing death. I sent a non-lethal means, and you accuse me of choosing control.”
“You chose not to accept a no.”
“No planetary administration can accept a no from every domain without ceasing to exist.”
Nemi had started the recording slate. Seln noticed it.
“You've grown,” he said.
“It's one consequence of time.”
“And of your uncle opening the quotas.”
“I'm not his child.”
Seln served them an infusion in the same office where Sava had once processed permits. Her old cup was no longer there. But on a shelf stood a measuring instrument she had left behind, with a scratch on its edge.
“You kept the things,” she said.
“Offices are more stable when every successor finds traces of the previous occupant.”
“I had no successor.”
“Your files were divided. No one had the right combination of competence and discreet violation.”
Nemi started the slate.
“Is that a compliment or an accusation?”
“In administration, the difference depends on who writes the assessment.”
Sava put the list of winter deaths on the table. It was incomplete; many communities had lost contact. Seln did not shy away from it. He opened his own data and added central mortality, attacks on transports, harvests captured by Houses. For an hour, the discussion was work, not a duel: figures, routes, capacities, thresholds. It was precisely this normality that angered Sava. Together, they could model a technical solution while bodies froze because of the political choice beneath the model.
“Restoration in two stages takes four months,” she said. “Vaor doesn't have four months.”
“A faster change would overheat the eastern crops.”
“We can compensate with local shading.”
“If the Corps allow central teams access.”
“Send mixed teams.”
“Taren will use them for reconnaissance.”
“And you use them for control.”
Seln put down his cup.
“See? Even a solution about light comes down to who carries a weapon.”
“Because you made infrastructure into a frontier.”
“Because Daran made the frontier into identity.”
“Daran is dead.”
“His stories aren't.”
Seln led them through the Chamber of Measure. Officials worked silently, allocating food, energy and births for the central regions. Requests from the west appeared on screens, marked low priority because of “access instability”.
“You moved the light as a weapon,” Sava said.
“I moved it towards the population I can feed without transports being confiscated by Houses.”
“You created refugees.”
“The war created territories where distribution is no longer predictable. Mirrors can't be administered according to local honour.”
“Children freeze predictably.”
Seln stopped before the map.
“In the past six years, the Face Corps have directly killed eleven thousand nivari. Wars between Houses have caused another forty thousand deaths and reduced the harvest by eighteen per cent. In the centre, mortality is below its pre-storm level. You've come to ask me to move resources from those who remained at peace to those who chose commanders.”
“Most chose nothing.”
“Exactly. That's my argument against commanders.”
For a moment, Sava remembered why she had respected him. Seln did not lie simply. He built a room from partial truths and invited you to live in it.
“What do you offer?” she asked.
“I restore the light in two stages. I recognise every existing birth and grant domains administrative autonomy. In return, the Corps are reduced to local services without a shared command. The reproductive Charter is repealed. Taren comes to Ivarra and stays here under protection.”
“In the Gardens of Kinship.”
“In a home of his own.”
“With doors that close from outside.”
Seln's offer was good enough to divide the Confederation. Sava knew it. Many Houses would accept autonomy and light in exchange for dissolving the shared command. Communities without a House would fear returning under local administrations. The Faces would refuse repeal of the Charter. Taren would become the price of peace.
“If he agrees to stay here,” Seln said, “I'll treat him as a former ruler, not a prisoner.”
“The doors?”
“They'll have protection.”
“From outside.”
“A man followed by armies can no longer have a door that is only personal.”
Sava stood.
“That's the problem with all your systems. Every collective consequence becomes a reason to take away individual choice.”
“And the problem with Taren's system is that every individual choice with a weapon becomes a collective consequence.”
Neither won. Seln granted them access to the Gardens for interviews about Lur and let them leave unescorted as far as the lower level. It was either a gesture of trust or a trap elegant enough for the distinction not to matter.
At the Gardens' entrance, Nemi adjusted her cloak and checked the carbon rod.
“Aren't they following us?” she asked.
“The city is following us,” Sava replied. “Agents would be redundant.”
The gates opened onto an artificial blue sky.
CHAPTER XIX
THE GARDENS OF KINSHIP
NEMI · BENEATH IVARRA · THE SECOND DAY OF NEGOTIATIONS
The Gardens of Kinship had been created to preserve peace between Houses: rulers' relatives lived in comfort in Ivarra, and that comfort could be suspended if the provinces forgot cooperation. After nine years of conflict, almost no one knew any longer whether they were hostages, citizens or privileged survivors.
The Gardens lay beneath the old genetic reservoirs. They had been built for House families: spacious rooms, warm pools, edible-moss orchards and ceilings projecting a predator-free sky. The gates were hidden in the walls. The children there no longer noticed them.
Nemi entered on the pretext of interviews for Lur's archive. She met descendants of every House, some held for seven years, others born into polite captivity. Not all wanted to leave.
“There is no home left in Orm,” an old woman told her. “My son was killed by Taren's Corps when he closed the border. Here I have food.”
“Not Taren's Corps,” Nemi said. “Vaor's Corps.”
“When it wins, it's his. When it kills wrongly, it belongs to someone else.”
The projected sky had no star. Diffuse light moved slowly across the ceiling, imitating a natural cycle Arua had never known. The Gardens' children drew sunrises and sunsets as ordinary landscapes, although in the real world Vei did not move. Sava wondered whether this was cruelty or a gift: growing up with an image of change the planet could not offer.
The homes were spacious, separated by low moss orchards and warm channels. The food was good. The schools had teachers. There were no visible locks. Only when someone approached an exit gallery did the wall turn opaque and a gentle voice recommend returning.
A woman from House Tel invited them to eat. Her husband had died at Lur, fighting under Orven. Their daughter had grown up in the Gardens and met him only through messages.
“You recorded the trial,” she told Nemi.
“Yes.”
“Do you have the execution too?”
“Yes.”
“I want to see it.”
Nemi hesitated.
“Why?”
“To know whether he died believing he'd been right.”
“The recording doesn't show what he believed.”
“Then what is Memory for?”
The question followed Nemi. Until then, she had thought a good archive preserved the limits of certainty. To the woman before her, precisely that limit was unbearable.
She showed her the trial, not the execution. The woman listened to the testimonies and stopped several times at her husband's voice, a junior officer sentenced to labour, not death. He was still alive in Lur. She did not know.
“Seln told us all the commanders had been executed.”
“Your husband wasn't in the command group.”
“Then why didn't he write to me?”
Nemi checked the registers. His messages had been blocked because House Tel refused to recognise the tribunal. A dispute between institutions had become nine years of administrative widowhood.
Sava obtained temporary access and sent the first message. The woman did not thank them. She cried with her face turned to the wall, as though freedom of information were a wound added too late.
•   •   •
A young man named Hale Orm agreed to help her. He had been a network technician before his family was brought to Ivarra and knew the old pipes beneath the Gardens. He showed her a maintenance room concealed behind a decorative waterfall.
“The secondary station is two levels below,” he said. “The House's access was revoked.”
Nemi took the key from the carbon rod.
“This one isn't House Orm's.”
“It's a curator's key. The museum network doesn't communicate with the mirrors.”
“It did before the Cleansing. The reservoirs needed independent light.”
Hale Orm was nineteen and had never been to Orm. His parents had come to the Gardens for their residency year and had not left after the conflict began. He had learned networks from manuals and clandestine repairs to the decorative waterfalls.
“Why do you want to leave?” Nemi asked behind the curtain of water.
“I don't know whether I do.”
“You're helping us open the gates.”
“I'm helping there be a choice. That's not the same thing.”
“That's a Sava sentence.”
“Who?”
Nemi smiled. It was strange to hear her mother's thinking in someone who had never known her.
Hale removed a panel. Behind it, the duct was narrow and damp. The smell of preservative came from below.
“My mother says Orm has nothing left for us. My father says any land where our ancestors died remains home. I've seen neither the land nor the dead.”
“Then what do you have?”
“Here? Food, friends and doors that decide for me. Outside? A story about a place that may not want me.”
Hale did not immediately lead them towards the hidden duct. He asked them to visit his family's home first. If the gates were going to open, he said, he did not want the first discussion about leaving to happen after the choice became a fact.
The home occupied the edge of an artificial orchard. Its walls retained warmth, and its windows projected a landscape from Orm: dark fields, low condensation towers, a line of mountains Hale had never seen. The image had been taken twenty-three years earlier. Since then, half the fields had been abandoned, and the mountains were entering the Night after the light's shift.
His mother, Sera, had grown up in Orm. His father, Tel Orm, wore the House name as an assertion and corrected anyone who called the Gardens home. With them lived Hale's younger sister, two cousins from House Nur and a Veyr woman whose partner had died during the siege of a transport. The families the system had kept as political guarantees had mingled until no single punishment could be applied to one House without striking all the others at the same table.
Sera put fruit grown under blue light before them. It had thin skin and tasted too sweet.
“Hale says you can open the gates,” she said.
“We can try,” Nemi replied.
“And after?”
“Whoever wants to leaves.”
“Where?”
Nemi looked towards the projection of Orm.
“Home.”
Sera smiled without joy.
“That's the image they brought us here with. My real home was divided among three families. My brother died. The neighbourhood's language changed. If I get there, I'm the relative of a defeated leadership who lived well in Ivarra while the others froze.”
“We didn't live well,” Tel said.
“We had food, medicine and blue sky.”
“We had guards.”
“We had both.”
Tel struck the table with two fingers.
“A large cage is still a cage.”
“And a ruin doesn't become home simply because you're allowed to enter it.”
Hale's sister, Meni, was eleven and did not understand why adults used the same word for different places.
“Can I stay here if you leave?” she asked.
Tel abruptly raised his head.
“No.”
“Then the gate doesn't give me a choice.”
“You're a child.”
“Hale was my age when the war started. No one asked him to choose to stay.”
Sera touched her hand but did not silence her.
Nemi took out her slate and opened a list.
“We can request a transition period. Group departures, contact with communities of origin, a temporary right of return.”
“Who guarantees it?” the Veyr woman asked.
“The families together. The keys are no longer controlled by one House.”
“Does Taren guarantee it?”
“No.”
The answer surprised them.
“Then why should we trust it?”
“Because a personal guarantee dies, loses or changes its mind. We need rules Taren can't change alone.”
Tel laughed.
“You talk like your mother.”
Sava, sitting beside the door, replied:
“It's one of the diseases we failed to prevent.”
The tension broke enough for Meni to laugh. Hale did not laugh. He was looking at the departure list.
“I want to see Orm,” he said. “I don't know whether I want to stay there.”
“You can go and come back,” Nemi said.
“What if the gates close again?”
“That's why we have to open them so no one can close them alone any more.”
Sera asked whether their homes would be preserved. Tel said that would turn the Gardens into a hotel for the undecided. The Veyr woman demanded protection for those unable to return to their Houses. The Nur cousins wanted to leave immediately. Meni wanted to take her teacher and the tree beside the window.
The discussion lasted longer than the technical planning of the gates. Every freedom produced questions about food, property, children and who bore the risk of the first night outside. Nemi had begun by believing the system kept people inside. Now she saw that, given enough time, captivity had built infrastructure around them: friendships, treatments, schools, graves, the habit of knowing where the next meal came from.
As they left, Meni gave Hale a leaf from the artificial tree. It was real, although its light and soil were controlled.
“So you have something from home,” she said.
Tel opened his mouth to correct the word. Sera stopped him.
Hale put the leaf in his pocket.
“See?” he told Nemi when they reached the waterfall again. “I don't know whether I want to be outside. But I want the answer to be mine tomorrow too, not only in the minute you open the door.”
Nemi understood why opening the Gardens would not be a simple liberation. Long captivity had created dependencies, mixed families, real safety. An open gate could not restore the years in which the alternative had been destroyed.
They descended.
One was open.
There was no embryo inside any more, but the walls retained recent scratches.
“Are there creatures left?” Hale asked.
“Taren heard two beneath the square. They found only one after the attack.”
A rustle came from somewhere in the dark.
Both froze. Nemi felt veyrin flood her blood, her limbs ready to leap. Then she remembered her uncle's lessons: alarm is information, not an order.
She raised the light.
A mature saryx stood confined in a side cage, fed through tubes. Its membranes were atrophied. Electrodes on its skull connected it to a simulation system.
Nemi opened the data.
The Great Hearing had used the creature to model panic responses. Entire generations of urban policies, educational programmes and medical selections had been tested against a virtual predator. The same archive contained a project called THE ADAPTIVE RESERVE.
The lists contained individuals with reduced flight reflexes, grouped by usefulness in a catastrophe: high-risk workers, rescue teams, biological control, last-resort defence.
Taren Ilen appeared at position 17.
The inclusion date was twelve years before the Festival.
“They've watched him since childhood,” Nemi said.
Hale scrolled through the file.
“Not only him. Look at the distribution. Most were sent to the provinces through placements, sanctions or relocations.”
“The Chamber cultivated its monsters at the margins.”
“It kept its options.”
The caged saryx did not resemble the animal in Nemi's memory. This one was thin, its membranes drooping, electrodes embedded in bone. When it raised its head, chains limited its movement. Nemi felt fear before realising some of it came from the creature. The animal was trembling.
Hale pressed against the wall.
“Do we kill it?”
Nemi took the blade from her bag. Sava had forbidden her to carry it; the ban belonged to another city and another age.
The saryx opened its vertical mouth but did not attack. Nutrient tubes entered its abdomen. On the screen, the simulation ran thousands of virtual crowds: alarm signals, corridor widths, veyrin doses, dispersal rates. The creature was not hunting. The system stimulated its brain and used the response to optimise nivar behaviour.
“They frighten it to learn how we get frightened,” Hale whispered.
Nemi lowered the blade.
In the Adaptive Reserve file, Taren's name appeared beside childhood assessments. At eight, after the cable fire, he had been classified “fit for persistence in a hostile environment”. Therapy aimed not only to normalise him; it measured how much deviation could be preserved without affecting his integration.
There were notes signed by Mera Sol. *The patient responds to argument, not command. I recommend technical use, not authority over others.*
“Use,” Nemi said.
Hale scrolled through the list. Hundreds of people had been directed towards high-risk trades, provinces and emergency teams. The Chamber had not eliminated them. It had distributed them where society could benefit from them without letting them become a model.
“Taren didn't emerge against the system,” Hale said. “He emerged from one of its drawers.”
Nemi copied the entire archive.
“And Daran opened the drawer.”
•   •   •
The curator's key opened the next node. The mirror station was not guarded from inside; its security depended on no one in the Gardens having access. Hale connected the slate to the Houses' old network. Four red consensus keys appeared on the map. Taking control required three.
“Orm has one,” he said. “Veyr had another. Tel and Selc have the rest.”
“The families are here.”
“The families no longer have authority.”
“Authority is what an old system accepts.”
Over the next hours, Nemi gathered the House representatives in the waterfall chamber. Some refused. Others signed only after being promised evacuation. The Veyr key responded to Daran's rod. Orm and Tel responded to relatives' biological signatures. Three lights turned green.
Secondary control opened.
The orbital swarm map floated above them. Nemi could move the light.
She did not do so immediately.
Any sudden change could burn crops in the east or freeze cities in the west. The Great Hearing administered millions of variables. A military takeover of the sky was easier than using it without killing.
“Send access to Vaor,” Hale said.
“No. Taren will be forced to use it for war.”
“Then why did we come down?”
“So Seln is no longer the only one compelled.”
Persuading the families did not resemble a military conspiracy. It resembled an association meeting that risked changing the sky. Representatives argued over who had ancient rights, whether biological signatures remained valid and what guarantees they would receive after the gates opened.
An old Veyr man refused Daran's key.
“He kept us here through his provocations,” he said. “I won't use his object to finish his plan.”
“The light is dying in Vaor,” Nemi replied.
“And my son died at Deaf Lake because of Taren.”
“I'm not asking you to forgive him.”
“You're asking me to give him the sky.”
“I'm asking you to stop giving it to one person.”
That changed the discussion. Nemi proposed a three-key threshold, automatic limits and publication of every orientation. Hale showed how a rapid change could be blocked. The families did not have to choose between Seln and Taren, but refuse both men's exclusivity.
The keys turned green.
When the orbital swarm map opened, Nemi felt for the first time the temptation of power that had no weight in the hand. One gesture could move kilometres of fertile band. She could warm Vaor immediately, but the price appeared in the east: burnt crops, overloaded pipes, millions of rations. A local emergency always demanded someone else become a variable.
She set a slow restoration.
“They'll die before the light arrives,” Hale said.
“Fewer than if I move everything now.”
“That's exactly the Custodian's sentence.”
Nemi kept her hand on the control.
“Yes.”
There was no moral purity in administering a finite sky. The difference had to lie in limits, transparency and who could stop the decision—things less satisfying than justice.
The central agents arrived before the first families left. They did not fire. They stood in formation and asked everyone to return to their rooms for safety. Many obeyed. Others remained between the gates, unable to decide whether freedom was the road to a ruined home or another room where they would be used.
Seln Aru came in person.
He looked at the mirror map, the open gates and the saryx connected to wires.
“Daran always had a taste for old keys,” he said.
Nemi raised the slate.
“Taren was in the Adaptive Reserve.”
“Yes.”
“You sent him to Vaor knowing what he could become.”
“I sent him because Vaor needed someone who would stay when the others ran. I didn't order anyone to follow him.”
“You built a society that made him rare, then put that rarity in a hungry place.”
“And Daran offered it a story.”
Seln approached the station. The agents waited for an order.
“Can you close access?” Sava asked.
“I can. But the mirrors would enter safe mode and the west would lose another week of light.”
“Then you can't without killing.”
When Seln arrived, Sava was already in the waterfall chamber. She had seen the gates open and people stop at the threshold. Some clutched their bags. Others returned towards their homes. The agents did not strike them. Their formation alone was enough for the old reflex of submission to return.
Seln looked at the Adaptive Reserve copies.
“The archive wasn't intended for the public,” he said.
“Nor was Taren intended to lead,” Nemi replied.
“Destinations are models, not commands.”
“The Chamber shaped his life before he could consent.”
“Parents shape. Schools shape. Hunger shapes. Institutions can at least publish their criteria.”
“Then publish them.”
Seln did not answer. Behind him, the saryx pulled at its chain. The agents raised their weapons.
“Don't kill it,” Nemi said.
“It's dangerous.”
“It's a prisoner.”
“Those aren't mutually exclusive categories.”
Yet Seln ordered the weapons lowered. To Nemi, that was the most disturbing thing: the Custodian could be persuaded in details and remain unyielding in structure.
Nemi sent a single message through the Veyr key to Vaor:
THE LIGHT IS RETURNING. THE GARDENS ARE OPEN. THE ARCHIVE PROVES BOTH ORDERS SELECTED YOU.
Seln let it go.
“Why?” Sava asked.
“Because Taren will come now,” he said. “Not for you. Not for me. For control of the sky.”
“And is that what you want?”
Seln looked towards the caged saryx. The creature opened its vertical mouth without a sound.
“I want the world to see him choose what he is.”
THE SKY WITHOUT A CEILING
The message went to Vaor through Daran's key. Nemi formulated it three times before sending. She did not write that they had won. She did not write that Seln had yielded. She wrote only the facts: the light was gradually returning; the gates were open; the archive proved both regimes had cultivated their instruments.
At the end, she added a personal sentence for Taren:
DON'T COME TO SAVE US. COME ONLY IF YOU CAN OPEN WITHOUT TAKING.
The reply did not come immediately.
In the western tunnels, the Confederation's army had already set off for Ivarra.
CHAPTER XX
THE SIEGE OF THE ROOTS
TAREN · IVARRA'S OUTER TUNNELS
The siege of Ivarra began as a promise of liberation and became, before the first fight, a problem of water, air, food and authority. The city had no walls; it had systems neither attackers nor defenders could live without.
Taren did not call the Houses to war. He called them to recover their families.
The difference lasted three days.
On the fourth, seven Face Corps set off for Ivarra, each under its own sign, its own commander and its own interpretation of the Code. Communities without a House sent food trains and young people who had no relatives in the Gardens but wanted to witness the fall of the city that had not recognised their birth. Vaor sent eight thousand fighters. Behind them came the Hands, doctors, repairers and traders who knew any siege creates a market before it creates a victory.
Sava saw the column on Ivarra's screens.
“He'll destroy everything he claims to save,” she said.
Seln stood in the Chamber of Measure, surrounded by the shifting Belt map. The light was gradually returning to the west under Nemi's programme. Central crops lost a few percentage points, but the collapse predicted by official warnings did not occur.
“Perhaps,” he replied. “But I didn't force him to come.”
“You closed the Gardens after the gates had been opened.”
“I prevented an uncontrolled evacuation into a city facing a siege.”
“That's your talent. Any locked door becomes a protective measure if you describe the corridor outside well enough.”
Seln folded one sail.
“And Taren's talent is making any blade necessary once he describes the danger.”
Nemi was no longer in the Gardens. She had disappeared through the maintenance network with Hale Orm and thirty members of House families. Agents searched for them, but Seln had forbidden flooding the pipes or using gas.
“You don't want to catch her,” Sava said.
“I want to see what she does when no one can tell her she was forced.”
“You treat the city like one of your tanks.”
“All rulers experiment. The most dangerous call the result destiny.”
Outside, the army's approach could be heard long before it could be seen. The ground carried the trains' vibration, then the columns' footsteps, then the irregular beats by which each Corps announced its sign. To Ivarra's inhabitants, the messages mingled in the foundations: Tel, Orm, Vaor, the communities without a House, three new formations still without names. The Great Hearing separated them and displayed probabilities. But the bodies of those in the city received only one meaning: something numerous was coming towards them.
Seln ordered the public alarm signals not to be activated. He said panic would block the tunnels before the attack began. Instead of sirens, home walls projected calm instructions: *Keep supplies for twenty days. Do not change levels without authorisation. Children remain in educational units until further orders.* The quiet wording frightened those who remembered Vaor's mist even more.
Sava walked down from the Chamber of Measure. Lifts had been reserved for interventions, and walking through the city showed her what the map could not: queues at water distribution; blankets fixed over ventilation openings to prevent possible gas; parents keeping children home despite contrary orders. An old man was sealing his door with root paste. When he recognised her, he asked whether the army would cut off the air.
“I don't know,” Sava replied.
He kept working.
“Then why do you wear the Chamber's cloak?”
Sava found no answer that did not resemble an excuse. The cloak opened doors for her, but did not give her the certainty people saw in it.
In the Gardens, the families had split. Some packed and waited for their relatives' army. Others barricaded their homes against the same army. Hale tried to organise evacuation corridors with Nemi, but every open door produced a new dispute. A Tel woman refused to leave without the two families with whom she had shared a home for nine years. An Orm man wanted to take only his biological children and claimed those raised alongside them belonged to Ivarra. Three adolescents declared they recognised no House and demanded permission to stay.
“Freedom would be simpler if no one had anywhere to return to,” Hale said.
“Then it wouldn't be freedom any more,” Nemi replied. “It would be evacuation.”
She carried Oru's slate beneath her cloak and touched it whenever the network branched. Her father's map had not been made for war. Its markings showed pressures, root species, postponed repairs. That was precisely why it was useful: it described the city before every pipe acquired a military value.
Sava found them beside a stopped waterfall. The water had been diverted to reservoirs, and only a layer of dark algae remained in the basin.
“Taren has set off,” Nemi said.
“I know.”
“I told him not to come to save us.”
“You asked him for something no ruler knows how to prove beforehand.”
“Do you think he'll take the city?”
Sava looked at the Gardens, the comfortable homes and the gates turned opaque again.
“I think each side will try to keep what it says it's protecting. That frightens me more than their intentions.”
Nemi handed her a copy of the map. It showed five routes to the Chamber of Measure and dozens of points where one explosion could stop the air for a neighbourhood.
“Oru left redundancies,” she said. “But the administration closed half of them to reduce losses.”
Sava smiled without joy. In peacetime, efficiency had been the virtue that eliminated reserves. Now every saving became a ready-built weapon.
“Reopen what you can,” she said. “Without asking permission.”
“Is that the Chamber's order?”
“It's your mother's advice.”
Nemi looked at her for a moment, then turned her body to the work. It was one of the few times she accepted authority precisely because it had been named correctly.
•   •   •
Taren's army reached the outer tunnels. Ivarra had no walls. It had filters, transport nodes, fire doors and thousands of kilometres of infrastructure that could be closed in sections. An attacker could conquer a neighbourhood and discover its air was pumped from a chamber five levels below, under central control.
The House commanders proposed cutting off the water.
Taren refused.
They proposed stopping ventilation to the administrative levels.
He refused again.
“Seln will keep his agents among civilians,” House Tel's commander said. “If we don't touch civilians, we don't touch him.”
“Then we'll reach him slowly.”
“Winter killed more than three days without water would.”
“That's precisely why we won't become better at his method.”
Raal watched him from the far end of the table. He had aged quickly. The scar around his muzzle had deepened, and only one of his chisel teeth remained intact.
“You'll lose the Houses,” he said after the meeting.
“I never had them.”
“They came for families, spoils and a sky they could control. If you give them only the families, they'll take the rest without you.”
“Then we'll stop them.”
“With whom?”
Taren did not answer.
That night, the Orm Corps attacked the water node alone. Central agents deployed immobilising foam and caught two hundred fighters. The rest retreated, leaving them behind. Taren sent the First Face to free them and arrested the Orm commander for violating the plan.
Half the Orm Corps left the siege.
The next day, House Tel tried to enter through the freight trains. Ivarra let the first carriage pass, then closed the gates between the second and third. The fighters inside were captured without a fight. Those outside demanded Taren stop the station's air to force the agents to release them.
He refused.
By the third day, his alliance was beginning to resemble a crowd that had discovered too many exits.
The attackers' camp had grown along the outer tunnels like a badly planned city. Every House had erected its own sleeping membranes, kitchens and guard posts. Temporary pipes crossed transport routes. Waste collected in pits too close to reservoirs. Traders sold food, medicine, blades and memorial slates not yet inscribed. Taren spent almost as much time stopping quarrels over space as planning the siege.
Vaor worked differently. Fighters slept beside Hands and repairers, grouped by neighbourhood rather than rank. Raal had insisted on shared kitchens. He said an army that eats in different orders quickly learns to die in different orders.
On the second evening, a drainage pipe burst beneath the weight of a Tel depot. Wastewater flooded two Orm tents, and the dispute brought three hundred fighters into the corridor. Taren arrived before the heavy weapons were activated. He made no speech. He put them to moving the depot, cleaning the water and digging a settling chamber together. The commanders protested that he was humiliating his Faces with Hands' work.
“Then learn what happens when Faces pitch their tent over a pipe,” he said.
Sen worked among them with an oversized shovel. At first, the fighters let him imitate effort. Raal took his tool and gave him a smaller one.
“Heirs usually get unsuitable objects,” he said. “So they look bigger beside them.”
“I'm not an heir.”
“Then dig.”
Taren saw them in passing and did not intervene. He had promised himself not to give the boy rank, a guard or a distinctive sign. But he could not prevent the gazes. When Sen lifted a stone, those around him saw the First Face's son. When he made a mistake, it became a test of blood. When he was silent, silence was interpreted as discipline. The child had no place where his gestures could be small.
At the night council, the seven Corps again demanded a plan to divide Ivarra. Instead, Taren projected a map of functions: water, air, hospitals, depots, transport, mirrors. Each Corps would protect a system after entering, not occupy territory.
“Whoever controls the system controls the territory,” the Tel commander said.
“Temporarily.”
“Everything lasting begins as temporary.”
“That's precisely why the keys won't stay with you.”
A murmur passed around the table. The Houses had come under a shared story, not a shared command. Taren had offered their captive relatives as the reason, Seln as the opponent and the Code as the limit. He had not offered the reward each had added for itself.
After the commanders left, Raal stayed and ate from a cold bowl. He had removed a prosthetic chisel tooth; without it, his speech was slightly unclear.
“Tel will try again,” he said.
“I know.”
“Orm will return if it sees an open gate. The other Houses will wait until they can say you broke the agreement first.”
“And Vaor?”
“Vaor follows you.”
Taren looked up.
“You didn't say that's the good part.”
“It isn't.”
Raal pushed the bowl towards him. Taren had not eaten since the start of the day.
“When I was in the Night,” Raal continued, “no one followed us for long. There, if the leader took the wrong road, the cold contradicted him before people did. Here, you can be wrong for years, and every victory becomes proof the road was right.”
“Do you want me to leave?”
“I want you to know what you carry behind you. It isn't only people. It's how they explain their own losses.”
Taren ate. The paste tasted of metal and roots stored too long. In the next tunnel, someone sang about Deaf Lake. The song made him taller, lonelier and more certain than he had been. Oru did not appear. Sava appeared only as the sister who counted the dead. Vessa did not exist at all.
“After Ivarra,” Taren said, “we abolish the House Corps.”
Raal laughed briefly.
“With what?”
“A shared structure.”
“Meaning your army.”
Taren did not answer. Outside the tent, the beats of House signs continued through the ground. Each promised belonging. No new institution could compete with a rhythm learned in childhood unless it offered a stronger rhythm still.
Nemi's message arrived before dawn. Taren listened three times. At the fourth, he stopped after the last sentence: *Come only if you can open without taking.*
Vessa stood in the doorway. She had arrived with the medical train and still wore the journey's dust on her cloak.
“Can you?” she asked.
“I don't know.”
“Then the right answer isn't attack.”
“If I stay, Seln closes the Gardens and moves the light again. The Houses attack alone. Ivarra will have the same war without the only command trying to limit it.”
“That's every indispensable man's argument.”
“It's also true sometimes.”
Vessa approached the map. Oru's route pulsed beneath the city.
“Take what you need to open it,” she said. “Then get someone to take the keys from you.”
“Who?”
“I don't know. But not finding the person doesn't make keeping them a solution.”
Taren touched the Gardens marker.
“Sen stays with you.”
“Sen isn't a medical object.”
“He's eight.”
“And already knows you hide the important things from him. He'll find a way.”
“Then tie him up.”
“I hear your imaginary father speaking.”
Taren turned his body face-on.
“I didn't have one.”
“Exactly.”
Vessa left before he could answer. That morning, Taren chose a thousand First Face members, mostly from Vaor, some from the Houses, some of Raal's former outcasts. He explained the route, the order of objectives and the rule he repeated until people began saying it with him: no vital function would be stopped to hasten victory.
He did not tell them what to do if that very rule made them lose.
•   •   •
Then Nemi sent Oru's map.
Years earlier, the engineer had mapped the root layer beneath Ivarra. The city had grown over old pipes abandoned after the Cleansing. Some connected to the genetic museum, others to nutrient tanks. Nemi had found a route to the Low Square.
Taren chose a thousand First Face members and entered a pipe where they could walk only bent over. Sen was among them.
He discovered him after two hours, hidden beneath a medical carrier's cloak.
“You're going back,” Taren said.
“The tunnel behind us closed.”
Raal checked the map.
“He's telling the truth.”
“Then stay with the doctors.”
“I can fight.”
“That's precisely why you're staying with the doctors.”
Sen turned his body face-on.
“At my age, you were treated as a defect. I'm treated as an inheritance. Neither is a choice.”
Taren caught him by the armour.
“My choice is for you not to die here.”
“That's what the Chamber said about you when it postponed your children.”
Taren released him. The words belonged to Sava, Vessa, the whole world that had learned to use his own contradictions.
“Beside Raal,” he said. “You don't leave him.”
“Is that an order?”
“Yes.”
“Good.”
For Sen, obedience was a relief when it came from the right person. Taren saw the danger and had no time to correct it.
The pipe was narrower than the map showed. Roots had grown through the joints, and sediment raised the floor. The fighters advanced bent over, one by one, weapons fixed to their backs. They could see no farther than the body ahead. For a species accustomed to keeping a wide field, the narrowing gaze produced an almost physical unease.
Raal walked beside Sen after the boy was discovered. He did not scold him. He showed him where to put his feet and how to breathe when the ceiling lowered.
“Father wanted to send me back,” Sen said.
“And you wanted to stay. Two desires don't yet make a good decision.”
“Do you think I shouldn't be here?”
“I think you're already here.”
“That's not an answer.”
“In war, you'll get many sentences that describe the situation and pretend to be answers. Learn to recognise them.”
Sen moved his sails irritably. Behind him, a doctor nudged him to continue.
During a halt, Raal showed him the trace of an old repair. The paste still bore Oru's imprint, a three-arc pattern used to mark unfinished interventions.
“Did you know him?” Sen asked.
“A little.”
“Everyone says he was good.”
“What does good mean?”
“He didn't kill.”
“Pipes don't usually kill either. Oru did more than avoid.”
“What?”
Raal touched the repair.
“He left things behind that others could continue.”
Sen looked at Raal's armour, worn blades and scar.
“What do you leave?”
“I'm trying to find out before the tunnel closes.”
Farther ahead, Taren heard the exchange and continued without turning. He was grateful to Raal and hated him a little for the ease with which he spoke to the boy. He himself turned every conversation with Sen into a lesson, warning or order. He did not know how to be present without preparing something.
In Ivarra, Nemi waited for the signal in the Lower Archive. Hale held his injured arm against his chest. The wound came from an agent he had struck with a wrench after the man tried to close a gate. Until then, Hale had described himself as a technician. Now the slate recorded an assault against him, and one day the Memory Corps would decide what name it received.
“Do you think they'll call us liberators?” he asked.
“Who?”
“Those who write after us.”
“I'm one of them.”
“Then what name do you give us?”
Nemi looked at the armed families, the pipes, the saryx's empty cage.
“People who opened a door and don't know what comes through it.”
Hale smiled faintly.
“That won't fit on a plaque.”
“That's why plaques lie well.”
The signal came through water: three beats, a pause, two. Taren had reached the museum.
They emerged into the museum reservoirs. The saryx connected to wires had disappeared. The cage was open, and black traces marked the floor.
“Did Seln release it?” Raal asked.
“He wouldn't risk the city,” Taren said.
“That's exactly what he says about you.”
Nemi waited for them in the Lower Archive. She had cut her black cloak to crawl through pipes. Hale was wounded in the arm. Behind them, the House families held weapons taken from agents.
“The mirror station is under control,” Nemi said. “But Seln moved the Great Hearing's command to the Chamber of Measure. If we disconnect him abruptly, the city's systems go into safe mode.”
“How long without air?”
“The old levels, twenty minutes. The Gardens, perhaps ten.”
“Then we don't disconnect him.”
“We have to reach the Chamber and transfer the keys with the Custodian active.”
“He'll block access.”
“Not completely. The system protects him, but it also protects the population. Oru left a route through the water nodes.”
Taren touched the slate's edge. Oru's handwriting appeared between layers of data: never close both valves simultaneously. A technical observation that had become, after his death, a form of help.
They divided the group. Nemi and Hale would follow the network towards the Chamber. Raal would take Sen and free the Gardens. Taren would lead the main force towards the Low Square, where he could open the gates for the rest of the army.
At the first junction, Ivarra attacked through sound.
The emergency speakers emitted the low frequency of an adult saryx. It was more than noise. The Great Hearing had calibrated it for generations to trigger the maximum panic response. The corridor itself seemed to fill with wings.
The fighters stopped. Some flattened themselves against the floor. Others turned and fled over those behind them. Armour snagged, blades discharged accidentally. Within seconds, the formation became the same crowd that had killed in the Low Square.
Taren felt the sound in his marrow too. He was not fearless. He never had been. His difference was only a moment, and the system had built a frequency long enough to fill it.
He drove his blade into the floor and struck the handle three times.
The metal sent the vibration through the roots.
Stop. Face. Attack.
Raal answered from another level. Then Nemi. Then hundreds of members, some fallen, some still fleeing. The new signal overlapped the old. It did not cancel it. It offered the body a second story.
The First Face turned.
Central agents waited around corners with foam and white projectiles. The fight spread through the pipes. There was no front line, only rooms changing masters and doors closing over the wounded. Taren saw his people obey the Code in one tunnel and break it in the next. He stopped two executions. He did not see the third.
After the panic frequency, the tunnel smelled of alarm secretions, burnt foam and blood. Some fighters still trembled although the sound had stopped. A Tel member had broken both chisel teeth trying to bite through his helmet's edge. A Vaor woman pressed against the wall and repeated that she could hear her young, although she had never had children.
Taren halted the formation. Every minute let the agents regroup, but the people could no longer be treated as a continuous line. Doctors injected inhibitors only into those who consented. The others received water, contact and small tasks: counting the wounded, checking weapons, beating the new rhythm into the pipes.
“We can't stop after every sound,” an Orm officer said.
“We just stopped without choosing,” Taren replied. “Now we choose how long it lasts.”
He knelt beside the woman who heard her children.
“What's your name?”
“Lira Sen.”
“There's no one in the tunnel by that name,” the doctor said.
“I didn't ask you,” Taren said. “Lira, what's in your hand?”
She looked at the blade as though seeing it for the first time.
“A foam knife.”
“Who do you give it to?”
“The doctor.”
She held it out. Her breathing slowed. Taren asked her to stay with the wounded. He did not call her weak or promise the fear had passed. When he stood, Sen watched him from beside Raal.
“She ran,” the boy said.
“Yes.”
“And she stays in the First Face?”
“If she wants.”
“Then what does the name mean?”
Taren wiped the blood from his palm.
“It means we come back. Not that we never leave.”
It was a more modest definition than the songs'. Sen seemed disappointed.
The formation moved again. In the next chambers, fighting became close and without an image. Central agents wore no personal signs. They withdrew in order, closed doors, used foam and projectiles that severed tendons without piercing organs. That restraint itself made some attackers angrier. It was easier to hate an enemy who denied himself visible cruelty after his system had killed from afar.
Taren saw a Vaor fighter striking a fallen agent. He stopped him at the second blow.
“My brother died in the mist!” the fighter shouted.
“This one didn't give the order.”
“He carried it out.”
“And now you're carrying out what you think I want.”
The man lowered his weapon. Not because he was convinced, but because Taren stood before him. The difference troubled Taren more than the blow.
In another corridor, a Tel team killed three agents after surrender. No one in command learned until the bodies were found. The Code worked only where someone was willing to enforce it against their own people.
Nemi and Hale advanced towards the Chamber of Measure through the water nodes. Above them, the city vibrated in contradictory rhythms: alarm, retreat, attack, lost children, open gates, closed gates. The Great Hearing tried to classify them. Hale's portable screen displayed thousands of automatic decisions every second. Ventilation moved air from a damaged neighbourhood. Reservoirs isolated contamination. Doors opened evacuation routes and closed them when density became dangerous.
“If we stop the system, we kill the city,” Hale said.
“If we leave it only to Seln, the city remains his weapon.”
“Then there's no good button.”
“No. There's only who can press it and who can stop the hand.”
They reached a gallery where water rose to their chests. Hale, weakened by his wound, slipped. Nemi caught his belt, but the current pulled them both towards a filter screen. For a few moments, archives, keys and war were reduced to breathing and a metal edge. Hale struck the pipe with his foot. The vibration travelled.
From downstream came the answer of a Hands team reopening the secondary valve on Sava's orders. The water level fell. Nemi never saw those who had saved them. In the official recording, the act would appear as “flow restoration”.
•   •   •
In the Gardens of Kinship, Raal opened the gates. The families came out in disorder. Sen was guiding them towards the evacuation pipes when the saryx descended from the ceiling.
The creature had grown up in captivity and did not know how to hunt well. It was hungry, blinded by light and full of broken electrodes. It attacked the first large movement: a group of running children.
Sen stopped.
He raised his blade, exactly as he had seen in the stories.
Raal struck him from the side and threw him under a table. The saryx caught Raal instead. Its claws pierced his armour, and its vertical mouth closed over his shoulder.
Raal did not try to run. He drove his only intact chisel tooth into the mouth's soft tissue and held the creature close, giving the others time to pass.
Sen came out from under the table and raised a thermal projectile.
“No!” Raal shouted. “The children!”
A discharge would have set the whole garden alight. Sen lowered the weapon and pulled the children towards the tunnel. When the last passed through, the saryx had lifted Raal off the floor.
Raal looked at him with his one visible eye.
“Now,” he said.
Sen fired.
Fire engulfed them both.
The explosion opened a crack in the Gardens' wall, directly onto the Low Square. Smoke emerged where, nine years earlier, Taren had killed the first creature.
The outer gates opened.
The Houses' armies entered Ivarra.
Taren reached the square before them and placed his First Face between civilians and allies. When Orm fighters tried to enter shops, he disarmed them. When Tel demanded the right to spoils for the years of winter, he replied that the first person taking food without a record would be tried under the Law of the Face.
“Did you conquer the capital to guard its depots?” a commander shouted.
“I came to open the gates,” Taren replied. “Not change who closes them.”
Not everyone listened. Looting broke out in the southern neighbourhoods. Central agents and Corps killed one another in tunnels without witnesses. By mid-cycle, three thousand people had died, mostly civilians caught between groups claiming to protect them.
After Raal's death, Sen made no sound. He held his weapon in both hands, and the creature's blood ran down his forearms. The children Raal had pushed towards the exit cried and crowded around the tables. Some looked at Sen as their saviour. None had seen who threw himself in first.
The doctor approached Raal's body. His armour was open from shoulder to abdomen. Raal still breathed, infrequently, through a punctured lung.
“Get them out,” he told Sen.
The boy knelt.
“I'll take you to the doctors.”
“These are doctors.”
“I'll take you to Vessa.”
Raal tried to laugh and coughed blood.
“Don't turn every dead person into an impossible task. Get the children out.”
“Why did you do that?”
“Because you were in the way.”
Sen shook his head angrily.
“Tell me the truth.”
“That is it. I pushed you. Now push them.”
Raal moved his remaining intact sail towards the children once. Then his body relaxed. The doctor checked his pupils and closed his mouth.
Sen stayed beside him until one of the children tugged at his cloak.
“Is another one coming?”
There was no other saryx. But shots came from the corridors, and smoke descended through the ceiling. Sen stood. He paired the children, counted them twice and led them towards evacuation. He spoke in Taren's voice, briefly and without promises. He did exactly what Raal had asked. Later, he would remember only that he had killed the creature.
In the Low Square, the gates opened under the explosion's pressure. Air from the outer tunnels entered like a dirty wind, carrying the camp's smell and the Houses' shouts. The first units crossed without waiting for an order. Taren tried to form civilian corridors, but the square filled with signs, weapons and people searching for relatives.
A woman from the Gardens recognised her brother in the Tel Corps. She ran towards him, and a central agent mistook her for an attacker. The projectile broke her knee. Her brother killed the agent before he could explain. In another corner, two Orm families refused to leave with the unit that had come to free them. The commander ordered them taken “for safety”.
Taren understood that opening was not an event. It was the beginning of a struggle over every freed person.
“Vaor units, protect exits and hospitals!” he beat through the ground. “No requisitions! No families moved by force!”
The signal repeated, but the other Corps transmitted their own orders. The square had become a language with too many speakers.
Near the old Retreat monument, they found Raal's body being carried on a stretcher and Sen walking beside it. Taren saw the blood, the weapon and the burn on the boy's arm. For a moment, he did not understand which body was his son's. Then Sen looked up.
“I got the children out,” he said.
Taren touched his head, a gesture too small for what he felt and too public for what the boy needed.
“Raal?”
Sen looked at the stretcher.
“The saryx was Seln's.”
Taren recognised the beginning of a story that could justify anything that followed. He caught him by the shoulders.
“Raal chose to push you away and asked you to get the children out. We'll learn the rest.”
“I killed him.”
“You killed the animal.”
“It's the same thing.”
“No.”
Sen pulled his shoulders free.
“For you, everything becomes different when you have to choose who bears the blame.”
He went towards the doctors. Taren stayed beside Raal while the army passed through the gates and civilians ran in both directions.
He had no time to mourn him. The first looting report came from the south. House Tel had occupied a food depot and closed the doors with people inside. Orm claimed the transport station. A Corps without a House had raised its own flag over a school and declared the neighbourhood free.
Taren opened the general channel.
“The objective is the Chamber of Measure. All units remain in their assigned sectors.”
The Tel commander answered after a pause.
“Your objective. Ours began after the gates opened.”
In the city centre, Nemi had reached the Chamber entrance. Sava and Seln waited for her. Hale leaned against the wall, almost unconscious. Ventilation fluctuated. On the map, each colour of the alliance spread through Ivarra like a separate infection.
Taren set off towards them with Sen and the rest of the First Face. Behind them, three thousand dead, wounded and missing people were already beginning to receive reasons.
CHAPTER XXI
