The instructor resumed the exercise and, this time, made them flee according to the old protocol. The children collided, laughed and asked to repeat the frontal fight. Nemi recorded the attempt. She knew that, a few years later, the clip might be used as evidence of flight’s uselessness, not of a lack of training.
After the lesson, she crossed the Low Square. Ivarra was no longer the city in which she had grown up. The underground levels still existed, but towers rose through the protective membranes towards the sky. Magnetic fields reduced the radiation. Transparent lifts carried residents to the surface for work, ceremonies and walks. Open space, once a punishment, had become a sign of rank.
In the old markets, traders sold eye corrections, shoulder prostheses and decorative sail trimming. Clinics promised “command vision”. Parents brought healthy children to have their peripheral vision reduced before the academy.
Vessa had fought the operation until her final years. She had published studies of accidents caused by the loss of lateral vision, sleep disorders and depression among those modified too early. The Custodianship had introduced consent at adulthood. Families had responded with “gradual correction” programmes beginning in childhood, small enough for each intervention to appear reversible.
When Vessa died twenty years earlier, the Faces had given her a military funeral for her contribution to field medicine. Her genetic criticism had been mentioned in an appendix. Nemi kept her last message in the archive: *I did not lose because the data was weak. I lost because every effect appeared after the reward that produced it.*
The Vertical Custodianship had brought peace to the Belt again. The Houses no longer fought open wars; they sent their young on the Margin Campaigns, ritualised conflicts for control of new tunnels, reactors and colonisation rights. The Code limited weapons, participant numbers and duration. The dead were counted as the price of social mobility.
The Faces led the army, police and risk administrations. The Hands produced and were entitled to protection. Memory kept the records, but access depended on the Custodianship’s authorisation. Those once called the Protected had become the Interior People: children, the sick, the elderly and all adults who refused the risk examination. They had guaranteed food, safe housing and almost no influence over frontier decisions.
The law said anyone could move from one function to another.
The data showed that ninety-one per cent of Faces had at least one Face parent.
Nemi kept this figure in the public layer of the archive. Every year, the Custodianship accompanied it with an explanation about family tradition and early preparation.
Sen had died at forty-one, after a campaign at the edge of Day. Not in battle, but from a marrow cancer associated with the genetic combinations favoured in Face lines. He had had nine acknowledged children and probably others. His eldest daughter, Arven, had become Custodian after an examination with seven candidates, five of them close relatives.
Arven was competent, disciplined and sincerely convinced that heredity had played no part.
The order of functions had become visible in the architecture. The Faces lived on the high levels, close to command centres and the surface. The Hands occupied the productive layers, among farms, reactors and transport. Memory lived around the archives, in cooled, windowless rooms. The Interior People received safe housing in the deep cores. No one called the distribution segregation. Each level was presented as optimal for its function.
Sava had died eight years after Taren. She had spent her final years leading a commission on mobility between functions. Its final report showed that examinations were formally open, but nutrition, education, family networks and bodily modifications determined the result before the test. The Council had adopted seven minor recommendations and postponed funding for the rest.
At her funeral, Nemi had read a single sentence from the report: *A door is not open if only some grow up with bodies shaped to fit it.* The crowd had applauded. The following year, the Face academies had used the quotation in a meritocratic scholarship campaign.
Sen had led the Belt for twenty-four years. At first, he had limited emergency mandates and opened the military archives. They had stopped three wars between Houses and transformed their Corps into shared units. Then the frontiers acquired other names: Margin Campaigns, stabilisation missions, contests for tunnel rights. Violence had not disappeared. It had been measured, scheduled and distributed by the Custodianship.
In the fifth year, a campaign lasted no more than thirty days and used non-penetrating weapons. In the tenth, exceptions allowed lethal projectiles for reactor defence. In the fifteenth, Houses could win colonisation rights in proportion to “risk assumed”. Young Faces joined voluntarily. Their families received prestige and reproductive quotas. Peace now had a controlled place in which to produce warriors.
Sen upheld the Code with a severity Taren had never shown. He dismissed commanders for looting, executed two for killing prisoners and required every unit to carry Hands medics. The Interior People considered him a protector. The Faces considered him proof that their power was moral. The fact that the rules were applied correctly made the institution harder to challenge.
Nemi met him twice a year. They were not close. They were bound by a shared childhood, the family’s dead and the fact that each had become custodian of a rival version of the past.
At their last meeting, Sen was forty. Marrow cancer had thinned his body, but he wore ceremonial armour. The doctors had offered him a transplant from an unrelated line. His immune system, shaped by the same interventions that had increased his resilience, had rejected every sample.
“Did Vessa predict this?” he asked.
“She predicted a risk,” Nemi replied. “Not your destiny.”
“Publish all the data.”
“The Custodianship classified it as genetic security information.”
“I am the Custodianship.”
“Which is precisely why the order must be signed before you die.”
Sen smiled. In illness, he looked more like the boy who had held Raal’s necklace than like Taren.
“You still talk like your mother.”
“Someone has to.”
He signed the publication order. The data showed an increased incidence of cancers, infertility and cardiac disorders in the selected Face lines. The Council recommended genetic diversification. Families accepted in theory and continued choosing the traits the examinations rewarded.
“I made the world safer,” Sen said.
It was not a question.
“For many, yes.”
“You always have a sentence afterwards.”
“Yes.”
“Say it.”
Nemi looked at the armour, the medical tubes and the vertical city beyond the window.
“You made danger a path to status. Now you need margins so the Faces know who they are.”
“The margins exist.”
“You also seek them out.”
Sen was silent.
“Father would have gone towards Leth if he had known it existed,” he said at last.
At that time, nothing had been detected in Leth. Nemi recorded the wording and labelled it a personal hypothesis. After his death, the songs had turned it into his final vision: the stars as an inevitable frontier.
Arven, his eldest daughter, won the Custodianship examination a year after the funeral. She had been a commander, had studied orbital ecology and had voted against her father in a margin campaign. She was better prepared than the other candidates. The fact that five out of seven were her relatives did not appear in the competence criteria.
The civilian node challenged the process. Memory published the genealogy. Arven requested a repeat of one test and won again. To her supporters, this demonstrated merit. To her opponents, it demonstrated that the family had possessed enough power to turn the challenge into another victory.
•   •   •
She summoned Nemi to the Sky Hall, built at the top of Ivarra’s tallest tower. The room had transparent walls and no shelter. For the old generations, it was a place of torture. For the new ones, a privilege.
An image of a probe floated above the table.
“It entered the system nine days ago,” Arven said.
The object was black, as long as a train and covered in layers of interstellar ice. It did not respond to signals. Its trajectory was not natural. It had passed Vei, adjusted its speed and was heading towards the mirror swarm.
“Origin?” Nemi asked.
“The Leth system, twelve light-years away. The spectra indicate a twilight planet and industrial activity.”
“Intelligent life.”
“Probably.”
“A weapon?”
Arven moved a sail.
“Unknown.”
Around the table stood the representatives of the Five Nodes. The Custodianship proposed capturing the probe, expanding the orbital fleet and building a propulsion gate towards Leth. The Hands had calculated the cost: twelve years of industrial surplus. The Chamber of Measure warned that the population would have to be reduced to free resources. Memory demanded that the object be decoded first. The Interior People’s node had voted against any expedition.
“We do not know whether they are coming towards us,” Nemi said.
The Fleet commander turned his convergent gaze towards her. The operation had brought his eyes disturbingly close to facing forward.
“The probe changed its trajectory after detecting the mirrors.”
“Perhaps it is seeking contact.”
“Contact is an approach whose risk is decided by the slowest participant. We will not be that participant.”
“There is no indication of hostility.”
“The saryx in the Low Square did not send a declaration either.”
Nemi looked through the tower wall at Taren’s statue, small in the square below.
“The comparison is false.”
“All comparisons are false until they survive.”
Seln would have liked the phrasing.
The Sky Hall still made Nemi nauseous. Its transparent walls revealed the curve of the Belt, the dark fields and Vei’s fixed brilliance. Her body demanded a ceiling and edges. Implants could reduce the response, but she had refused. She said Memory also needed the things the age considered symptoms.
Beside the council table stood Sio Eri, leader of the team that had analysed the probe. He had been born without a number during the solar storm in Vaor and had slept his first hours in a filter box. Now he was a Hands specialist in orbital organic systems. He wore long sails and had not had his eyes corrected. In the tower, his profile looked like a political statement, although he maintained that he feared the operation.
Nemi had met him a few times. Sio kept an old recording of Taren warming his box beside the pipe. He did not show it in public.
“The probe is not only mechanical,” he told the council. “The hull contains tissues capable of repairing themselves. Some died in transit. Others are inactive, not dead.”
“A crew?” Arven asked.
“We do not know. Perhaps a navigation system. Perhaps organisms being transported. The empty chambers have volumes compatible with bodies, but that compatibility begins with the bodies we imagine.”
The Fleet commander opened the trajectory model.
“It adjusted its course towards the mirrors.”
“Yes,” Sio said. “After we sent a test sequence.”
“It responded.”
“Or avoided. The adjustment reduced the risk of collision with the swarm.”
“It still entered capture range.”
“Because we expanded it.”
Arven looked at him face-on.
“Your conclusion?”
“We have no basis for hostility or for innocence. We have a constructed object, from an industrial world, that reacted to our infrastructure.”
“The exact definition of strategic contact,” the commander said.
“It is the definition of the facts. The word *strategic* is what you add.”
Nemi recognised something of Oru in Sio: the refusal to let function swallow the object. It seemed unfair that the association came to her only because she knew who had warmed his box at birth.
The Interior People’s representative, a woman named Fara, asked for the probe to be placed in an isolated orbit and studied before any project towards Leth.
“It may carry biological agents,” she said.
“It is already isolated,” the Fleet replied.
“Then why the urgency of the gate?”
The commander indicated the distance of twelve light-years.
“Because any reply sent now arrives after twenty-four years. Any construction begun after we understand them may come too late.”
“Too late for what?”
“To avoid being the unprepared side.”
Fara turned her body sideways, a gesture the Faces often interpreted as weakness. Nemi saw it as an attempt to keep the entire table in her field of vision.
“You are always the unprepared side before you define what you are preparing for,” Fara said.
Arven did not raise her voice. Her competence lay in her ability to turn conflict into a sequence of options.
“We will not launch without a vote. But we have to build the capacity to decide. A gate is not an invasion.”
“Nor was a Face House a dynasty,” Nemi said. “At first.”
Arven gave her a tired smile.
“That is why you are here.”
“So you can listen to me?”
“So we cannot say we were not warned.”
This was Memory’s mature form in the new order: it did not stop the decision, but it took away its innocence.
Arven opened a second model. The propulsion gate used the mirrors as an acceleration network. The technology had been possible for decades, but no project had justified the cost. Now, the mere existence of someone else turned the impossible into an emergency.
“It is not only defence,” the Custodian said. “The Belt is full. The Margin Campaigns redistribute tunnels; they do not create a world. Leth may have other resources, other ecosystems, other space.”
“And inhabitants.”
“Then we will negotiate.”
“With a fleet built before we hear their voice.”
Arven approached the window.
“Taren found a society in which every role was complete and every future allocated. He opened it. We have reached the edge again.”
“Taren did not only open things. He closed them too.”
“That is why we keep you alive, Nemi. To say the sentence after the song.”
“Do you listen to it?”
“Sometimes.”
The Council voted. The Custodianship, the Hands and the Chamber of Measure approved the gate. Memory abstained. The Interior People voted against it.
Three out of five.
The law worked exactly as it had been written.
In the following months, Ivarra filled with recruitment drives. Children drew the probe on walls and added wings, fangs or armour, although no one knew what it contained. The Faces named the project the Horizon. Birth rights for fleet families were doubled as compensation for risk. Clinics reported a new demand: tolerance of open spaces, reduced sleep, total ocular convergence.
After the vote, Fara requested publication of the cost by function. The Hands had calculated twelve years of surplus, but the figure concealed its distribution. The new reactors would be built on their levels. Population reduction fell mainly on the Interior People, whose birth rights received no risk compensation. The fleet’s Faces received double quotas. Memory would lose personnel to the decoding project.
The public report provoked protests. In the deep tunnels, the Interior People beat the old retreat signal: not from fear, but in refusal of departure for Leth. The Faces answered with the frontal rhythm. For the first time in decades, two stories of the body confronted one another through Ivarra’s foundations.
Arven did not suppress the protests. She allowed them six cycles, then declared that blocking orbital transport threatened infrastructure and sent units without lethal weapons. Two hundred people were injured. No death appeared in the Custodianship’s report. For an order that had learned to measure peace by the absence of deaths, the operation was a success.
Nemi visited one of the clinics treating the protesters. A man with a broken shoulder asked her why Memory had abstained instead of voting against it.
“Because we did not have enough data about the probe,” she replied.
“You had enough about the cost.”
“Yes.”
“Then abstaining was a vote for those who already had three.”
Nemi did not contradict him. Procedural neutrality had once again proved to be a decision with a beneficiary.
Sio continued analysing the object. The organic structures contained repeated sequences that might be language or repair instructions. One chamber retained traces of volatile compounds and a folded membrane. The team reconstructed a possible form: a small organism with radial symmetry, adapted to lower gravity. The model was so uncertain that Nemi refused to publish the image as a representation. The Fleet nevertheless used it in recruitment materials, with armour added.
Children drew “Lethians” with fangs. Traders sold toys. Preachers in some communities said the beings had come for light; others said they did not exist and the probe had been sent by the Great Hearing to create a new front. Uncertainty produced more stories than information.
Sio showed Nemi a sequence the algorithms had ignored. It repeated only after avoidance manoeuvres.
“It may be a distress signal,” he said.
“It may be a greeting.”
“It may be the tissue checking a pump.”
“What do you think?”
Sio looked at the object floating in the isolation field.
“I think we want to find a mind too quickly, because a mind can be an enemy, a partner or a judge. A pump gives us no meaning.”
Nemi thought of the first shadow in the square. A hungry animal had become the beginning of an order. Perhaps history did not need intelligent adversaries. It needed only something onto which a society could project its need.
Before the gate’s activation, Arven came to the archive alone. She wore no armour. She asked Nemi for all the unpublished recordings about Taren.
“Why?”
“I want to know what he would have done.”
“The dead man you are looking for was constructed by the living according to their needs.”
“You knew him.”
“Yes. And precisely because of that, I can tell you he would have wanted contradictory things. He would have prepared the defence. He would have hated the use of fear. He would have been drawn to the edge. He would have asked who controlled the gate. He would probably have taken the key if someone else seemed more dangerous.”
“That does not help me.”
“It helps you avoid using his name as a vote.”
Arven sat among the shelves. For the first time, she looked not like a Custodian, but like a woman who had been given a full world and a new distance.
“The Belt is full,” she said. “Young Faces fight over tunnels that no longer exist. The Interior People ask for larger families. The Hands say the reactors have reached their limit. If we do not open, the pressure stays here.”
“Then the gate is also a social pressure valve.”
“Yes.”
“Say that publicly.”
“They will think we are shipping away the inconvenient.”
“Are you?”
Arven did not answer at once.
“The volunteers will mostly be Faces without rank and Hands without access to the upper levels.”
“Then yes.”
“Not by order.”
“A structure can move people without commanding them. It shows them where a future exists.”
Arven raised her eyes towards the archives.
“Do you think we should cancel?”
Nemi thought of the children who no longer knew how to flee, of Sio born without a number, of the finite fields and the probe that had crossed twelve light-years without anyone knowing why.
“I think you must leave without calling the unknown property,” she said. “And send people capable of turning back if they discover that their presence does harm.”
“The Faces are trained to advance.”
“Then perhaps they are not the only ones you should send.”
Arven changed the first ship’s crew composition. She added Interior People, ecologists and negotiators. Fleet Command reduced their numbers during the logistics stage. On the final manifest, no Interior Person remained; the spaces had been reclassified for medical support. Arven signed. She said the next ship would be different.
Nemi kept both lists.
•   •   •
Nemi visited the Gardens of Kinship, by then transformed into the Museum of Peace. Seln’s room was preserved with its bed, table and his commentaries projected on the walls. He had died six years after the trial. His final note said:
AN ORDER IS NOT DEFEATED WHEN YOU CHANGE ITS RULER. IT IS DEFEATED WHEN ITS INSTRUMENTS BECOME UNUSABLE.
Ivarra’s instruments had not become unusable. They had been extended to the stars.
On the day of the gate’s activation, the entire population came to the surface. The towers protected the Belt with magnetic fields, and the red sky was no longer considered a place of death. Children sat in the front row. Some had modified eyes, others trimmed sails; all wore the frontal sign on their chests.
The probe from Leth had been captured. Inside, they had found organic structures, an undeciphered message and empty chambers the size of bodies. No one knew whether the passengers had died, departed or resembled nothing that could be recognised as a passenger.
That uncertainty was enough.
In the Museum of Peace, Seln’s room had been restored too beautifully. The bed bore no impression of his body, the table had no scratches, and the opaque wall opened for visitors. Children passed through the room and listened to his commentaries selected by curators. Most often quoted were the warnings about Taren’s power. Less often came the justifications for the mist and the Gardens.
Nemi requested access to the complete archive. Seln’s final note remained there, followed by an unfinished fragment: *Instruments become unusable only when society accepts the cost of living without their services. Almost no victory accepts that cost.*
She did not know whether the sentence excused him or extended his accusation.
That same day, she visited Oru’s root. The water station had been incorporated into a memorial park. Children ran among the pipes and touched the bitter fruit, then spat it out. A plaque called him an “engineer of resilience who unwittingly prepared the path to victory”. Nemi had requested that *unwittingly* be removed and replaced with a description of his work. The committee had said the public needed the connection to great history.
Sio was waiting for her there. He wore the Hands launch suit. He had been added to the crew as a specialist in the probe’s biology.
“You are leaving,” Nemi said.
“Yes.”
“You voted against the gate.”
“I voted against construction before contact. The gate exists now. I would rather someone were there who does not see every tissue as a weapon.”
“That is the same sentence by which good people enter bad institutions.”
“I know.”
“And?”
Sio touched the warm pipe. In his personal archive, that was where life began.
“Sometimes the institution leaves anyway.”
Nemi gave him a copy of Oru’s map, adapted to the orbital ship. Not because the technology was useful, but because his markings showed a way of thinking: never both valves closed; every system must leave a path for whoever comes afterwards.
“Do not turn it into a relic,” she said.
“I will use it.”
“Better.”
The mirrors aligned one by one. Their light formed a column above the Belt, so powerful that shadows disappeared. The first ship rose from the orbital tower, carrying three thousand Faces, one thousand Hands, two hundred members of Memory and no Interior People.
Nemi stood beside Taren’s statue. In her hand was the slate on which she had recorded Lur, Daran’s death, the siege and the first transfer of the sky. She began one last note.
The first ship bore the name *Raal*. The committee had chosen the name for courage and sacrifice. No plaque mentioned that Raal had been an outlaw, a thief, an opponent of ranks and the first to tell Taren that his army could become a caste. Sen had kept his necklace all his life; Arven had now mounted it in the command room.
Among the three thousand Faces were Sen’s grandchildren, children of the Houses and young people from public academies. The thousand Hands had been selected for rare skills. The two hundred members of Memory carried copies of the archives. The Interior People had been absent from the manifest, but eighty-seven had boarded as medical staff, carers and technicians without official classification. Institutions knew how to preserve the shape of a rule by changing people’s names.
Nemi began recording. Around her, the crowd beat the rhythm of the gates. She tried the sentence about selection, food and songs.
“They did not become what they were meant to be,” she said. “No species has such a destination. They became what received more births, more food, more songs and more reasons to believe itself necessary. The rest was called weakness, then protected, then excluded.”
She stopped. The sentence sounded too much like a conclusion, and life had never respected conclusions.
She deleted the recording.
Deleting it did not eliminate the idea. Nemi recorded again, this time without a conclusion.
“I have seen a world in which flight was a virtue and one in which it became a shame,” she said. “Both called their selection natural. I have seen peace using fear and war offering meaning. I have seen people saved by institutions that measured them and hurt by leaders who loved them. What leaves today is not the end of that contradiction. It is our way of carrying it with us.”
She stopped. Behind the camera, a child asked why she spoke so sadly on a day of opening.
Nemi turned. The child had eyes corrected to face forward and uncut sails, a rare combination. He wore the sign of a Hands school.
“I am not sad,” she said. “I am trying to leave room for what we do not know.”
“Arven says Leth is a new world.”
“It is an old world for those who live there.”
The child thought.
“Then we are the new ones.”
“Yes.”
That was the best sentence of the day. Nemi kept it with his name.
Above them, the gate opened like a second horizon.
The children raised their heads. None sought shelter. None wondered whether the luminous shape might be a predator. In their eyes there was only distance and the promise that somewhere beyond it lay something that did not yet know their names.
That night, no one beat the ground to sound the alarm.
They all beat it together, to ask for the gates to open.
THE END
